(nominal) per capita">94th</a>)</td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label"><a href="/wiki/Gini_coefficient" title="Gini coefficient">Gini</a>&#160;<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CIADemo-4">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup></span></div></td></tr></tbody></table>
<p>The <b>Dominican Republic</b> (<span class="rt-commentedText nowrap"><span class="IPA nopopups noexcerpt" lang="en-fonipa"><a href="/wiki/Help:IPA/English" title="Help:IPA/English">/<span style="border-bottom:1px dotted"><span title="&#39;d&#39; in &#39;dye&#39;">d</span><span title="/ə/: &#39;a&#39; in &#39;about&#39;">ə</span><span title="/ˈ/: primary stress follows">ˈ</span><span title="&#39;m&#39; in &#39;my&#39;">m</span><span title="/ɪ/: &#39;i&#39; in &#39;kit&#39;">ɪ</span><span title="&#39;n&#39; in &#39;nigh&#39;">n</span><span title="/ɪ/: &#39;i&#39; in &#39;kit&#39;">ɪ</span><span title="&#39;k&#39; in &#39;kind&#39;">k</span><span title="/ən/: &#39;on&#39; in &#39;button&#39;">ən</span></span>/</a></span></span> <a href="/wiki/Help:Pronunciation_respelling_key" title="Help:Pronunciation respelling key"><i title="English pronunciation respelling">də-<span style="font-size:90%">MIN</span>-ik-ən</i></a>; <a href="/wiki/Spanish_language" title="Spanish language">Spanish</a>: <i lang="es">República Dominicana</i>, <small>pronounced&#160;</small><span title="Representation in the International Phonetic Alphabet (IPA)" class="IPA" lang="es-Latn-fonipa"><a href="/wiki/Help:IPA/Spanish" title="Help:IPA/Spanish">[reˈpuβlika ðominiˈkana]</a></span>&#32;<span class="nowrap" style="font-size:85%">(<span class="unicode haudio"><span class="fn"><span style="white-space:nowrap;margin-right:.25em;"><a href="/wiki/File:ES-pe_-_Rep%C3%BAblica_Dominicana.ogg" title="About this sound"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8a/Loudspeaker.svg/11px-Loudspeaker.svg.png" decoding="async" width="11" height="11" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8a/Loudspeaker.svg/17px-Loudspeaker.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8a/Loudspeaker.svg/22px-Loudspeaker.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="20" data-file-height="20" /></a></span><a href="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/b/b6/ES-pe_-_Rep%C3%BAblica_Dominicana.ogg" class="internal" title="ES-pe - República Dominicana.ogg">listen</a></span></span>)</span>) is a country located on the island of <a href="/wiki/Hispaniola" title="Hispaniola">Hispaniola</a> in the <a href="/wiki/Greater_Antilles" title="Greater Antilles">Greater Antilles</a> archipelago of the <a href="/wiki/Caribbean" title="Caribbean">Caribbean</a> region. It occupies the eastern five-eighths of the island, which it shares with <a href="/wiki/Haiti" title="Haiti">Haiti</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-16" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-16">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-17" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-17">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup> making Hispaniola one of only two Caribbean islands, along with <a href="/wiki/Saint_Martin_(island)" title="Saint Martin (island)">Saint Martin</a>, that is shared by two <a href="/wiki/Sovereign_state" title="Sovereign state">sovereign states</a>. The Dominican Republic is the second-largest nation in the <a href="/wiki/Antilles" title="Antilles">Antilles</a> by area (after <a href="/wiki/Cuba" title="Cuba">Cuba</a>) at 48,671 square kilometers (18,792&#160;sq&#160;mi), and third-largest by population, with approximately 10.7 million people (2022 est.), down from 10.8 million in 2020, of whom approximately 3.3 million live in the metropolitan area of <a href="/wiki/Santo_Domingo" title="Santo Domingo">Santo Domingo</a>, the capital city.<sup id="cite_ref-CIADemo_4-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CIADemo-4">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-18" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-18">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-conapofa_19-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-conapofa-19">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup> The official language of the country is <a href="/wiki/Spanish_language" title="Spanish language">Spanish</a>.
</p><p>The native <a href="/wiki/Ta%C3%ADno" title="Taíno">Taíno</a> people had inhabited Hispaniola before the arrival of Europeans, dividing it into five chiefdoms.<sup id="cite_ref-CIADemo_4-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CIADemo-4">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> They had constructed an advanced farming and hunting society, and were in the process of becoming an organized civilization.<sup id="cite_ref-Brown_20-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Brown-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> The Taínos also inhabited Cuba, <a href="/wiki/Jamaica" title="Jamaica">Jamaica</a>, <a href="/wiki/Puerto_Rico" title="Puerto Rico">Puerto Rico</a>, and <a href="/wiki/The_Bahamas" title="The Bahamas">the Bahamas</a>. The <a href="/wiki/Republic_of_Genoa" title="Republic of Genoa">Genoese</a> mariner <a href="/wiki/Christopher_Columbus" title="Christopher Columbus">Christopher Columbus</a> explored and claimed the island for <a href="/wiki/Crown_of_Castile" title="Crown of Castile">Castile</a>, landing there on his first voyage in 1492.<sup id="cite_ref-CIADemo_4-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CIADemo-4">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> The <a href="/wiki/Captaincy_General_of_Santo_Domingo" title="Captaincy General of Santo Domingo">colony of Santo Domingo</a> became the site of the first permanent European settlement in the Americas and the first seat of Spanish colonial rule in the <a href="/wiki/New_World" title="New World">New World</a>. It would also become the site to introduce importations of <a href="/wiki/Atlantic_slave_trade" title="Atlantic slave trade">enslaved Africans</a> to the Americas. In 1697, Spain recognized French dominion over the western third of the island, which became the independent state of <a href="/wiki/First_Empire_of_Haiti" title="First Empire of Haiti">Haiti</a> in 1804.<sup id="cite_ref-CIADemo_4-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CIADemo-4">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>After more than three hundred years of Spanish rule, the Dominican people declared independence in November 1821.<sup id="cite_ref-CIADemo_4-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CIADemo-4">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> The leader of the independence movement, <a href="/wiki/Jos%C3%A9_N%C3%BA%C3%B1ez_de_C%C3%A1ceres" title="José Núñez de Cáceres">José Núñez de Cáceres</a>, intended the Dominican nation to unite with the country of <a href="/wiki/Gran_Colombia" title="Gran Colombia">Gran Colombia</a>, but the newly independent Dominicans were forcefully annexed by Haiti in February 1822. Independence came 22 years later in 1844,<sup id="cite_ref-CIADemo_4-10" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CIADemo-4">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> after victory in the <a href="/wiki/Dominican_War_of_Independence" title="Dominican War of Independence">Dominican War of Independence</a>. Over the next 72 years, the Dominican Republic experienced mostly civil wars (financed with loans from European merchants), several failed invasions by its neighbour, Haiti, and brief return to Spanish colonial status, before permanently ousting the Spanish during the <a href="/wiki/Dominican_Restoration_War" title="Dominican Restoration War">Dominican War of Restoration</a> of 1863–1865.<sup id="cite_ref-21" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-21">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-22" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-22">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-23" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-23">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup> During this period, three presidents were assassinated (<a href="/wiki/Jos%C3%A9_Antonio_Salcedo" title="José Antonio Salcedo">José Antonio Salcedo</a> in 1864, <a href="/wiki/Ulises_Heureaux" title="Ulises Heureaux">Ulises Heureaux</a> in 1899, and <a href="/wiki/Ram%C3%B3n_C%C3%A1ceres" title="Ramón Cáceres">Ramón Cáceres</a> in 1911).
</p><p>The <a href="/wiki/United_States_occupation_of_the_Dominican_Republic_(1916%E2%80%931924)" title="United States occupation of the Dominican Republic (1916–1924)">U.S. occupied</a> the Dominican Republic (1916–1924) due to threats of defaulting on foreign debts; a subsequent calm and prosperous six-year period under <a href="/wiki/Horacio_V%C3%A1squez" title="Horacio Vásquez">Horacio Vásquez</a> followed. From 1930 the dictatorship of <a href="/wiki/Rafael_Trujillo" title="Rafael Trujillo">Rafael Leónidas Trujillo</a> ruled until his assassination in 1961.<sup id="cite_ref-CIADemo_4-11" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CIADemo-4">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Juan_Bosch_(politician)" title="Juan Bosch (politician)">Juan Bosch</a> was elected president in 1962 but was deposed in a military coup in 1963. A <a href="/wiki/Dominican_Civil_War" title="Dominican Civil War">civil war in 1965</a>, the country's last, was ended by U.S. military intervention and was followed by the authoritarian rule of <a href="/wiki/Joaqu%C3%ADn_Balaguer" title="Joaquín Balaguer">Joaquín Balaguer</a> (1966–1978 and 1986–1996). Since 1978, the Dominican Republic has moved toward <a href="/wiki/Representative_democracy" title="Representative democracy">representative democracy</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-24" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-24">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup> and has been led by <a href="/wiki/Leonel_Fern%C3%A1ndez" title="Leonel Fernández">Leonel Fernández</a> for most of the time after 1996. <a href="/wiki/Danilo_Medina" title="Danilo Medina">Danilo Medina</a> succeeded Fernández in 2012, winning 51% of the electoral vote over his opponent ex-president <a href="/wiki/Hip%C3%B3lito_Mej%C3%ADa" title="Hipólito Mejía">Hipólito Mejía</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-25" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-25">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup> He was later succeeded by <a href="/wiki/Luis_Abinader" title="Luis Abinader">Luis Abinader</a> in the 2020 presidential election after <a href="/wiki/2020_Dominican_Republic_protests" title="2020 Dominican Republic protests">anti-government protests erupted</a> that year.<sup id="cite_ref-26" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-26">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The Dominican Republic has the <a href="/wiki/Economy_of_the_Dominican_Republic" title="Economy of the Dominican Republic">largest economy</a> (according to the <a href="/wiki/United_States_Department_of_State" title="United States Department of State">U.S. State Department</a> and the <a href="/wiki/World_Bank" title="World Bank">World Bank</a>) in the Caribbean and Central American region and is the <a href="/wiki/List_of_Latin_American_and_Caribbean_countries_by_GDP_(PPP)" title="List of Latin American and Caribbean countries by GDP (PPP)">seventh-largest economy</a> in <a href="/wiki/Latin_America" title="Latin America">Latin America</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-gdp_rank_27-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-gdp_rank-27">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-worldbank.org_28-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-worldbank.org-28">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup> Over the last 25 years, the Dominican Republic has had the fastest-growing economy in the <a href="/wiki/Western_Hemisphere" title="Western Hemisphere">Western Hemisphere</a> – with an average <a href="/wiki/List_of_countries_by_real_GDP_growth_rate" title="List of countries by real GDP growth rate">real GDP growth rate</a> of 5.3% between 1992 and 2018.<sup id="cite_ref-:0_29-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:0-29">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup> GDP growth in 2014 and 2015 reached 7.3 and 7.0%, respectively, the highest in the Western Hemisphere.<sup id="cite_ref-:0_29-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:0-29">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup> In the first half of 2016, the Dominican economy grew 7.4% continuing its trend of rapid <a href="/wiki/Economic_growth" title="Economic growth">economic growth</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-:1_30-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:1-30">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup> Recent growth has been driven by construction, manufacturing, tourism, and mining. The country is the site of the third largest <a href="/wiki/Gold_mine" class="mw-redirect" title="Gold mine">gold mine</a> in the world, the <a href="/wiki/Pueblo_Viejo_mine" title="Pueblo Viejo mine">Pueblo Viejo mine</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-investingnews.com_31-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-investingnews.com-31">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-lawrieongold.com_32-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-lawrieongold.com-32">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup> Private consumption has been strong, as a result of low inflation (under 1% on average in 2015), job creation, and a high level of <a href="/wiki/Remittance" title="Remittance">remittances</a>. <a href="/wiki/Income_inequality" class="mw-redirect" title="Income inequality">Income inequality</a>, for generations an unsolved issue, has faded thanks to its rapid economic growth and now the Dominican Republic exhibits a <a href="/wiki/Gini_coefficient" title="Gini coefficient">Gini coefficient</a> of 39, similar to that of <a href="/wiki/Israel" title="Israel">Israel</a> and <a href="/wiki/Uruguay" title="Uruguay">Uruguay</a>, and better than countries like the <a href="/wiki/United_States" title="United States">United States</a>, <a href="/wiki/Costa_Rica" title="Costa Rica">Costa Rica</a> or <a href="/wiki/Chile" title="Chile">Chile</a>. <a href="/wiki/Illegal_immigration" title="Illegal immigration">Illegal</a> Immigration from Haiti has resulted in government action. Immigration from Haiti has increased tensions between Dominicans and Haitians.<sup id="cite_ref-33" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-33">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-34" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-34">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-35" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-35">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-36" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-36">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-37" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-37">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup> The Dominican Republic is also home to 114,050 illegal immigrants from <a href="/wiki/Venezuela" title="Venezuela">Venezuela</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-CIADemo_4-12" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CIADemo-4">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> According to the UN, the country struggles with systemic racism and discrimination based on race, mostly targeted towards people of Haitian origin. <sup id="cite_ref-38" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-38">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-39" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-39">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The Dominican Republic is the <a href="/wiki/World_Tourism_rankings" title="World Tourism rankings">most visited destination</a> in the Caribbean.<sup id="cite_ref-WTO_Tourism_Highlights_2018_Edition_40-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-WTO_Tourism_Highlights_2018_Edition-40">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup> The year-round golf courses and resorts are major attractions.<sup id="cite_ref-consulate_41-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-consulate-41">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup> A geographically diverse nation, the Dominican Republic is home to both the Caribbean's tallest mountain peak, <a href="/wiki/Pico_Duarte" title="Pico Duarte">Pico Duarte</a>, and the Caribbean's largest lake and lowest point, <a href="/wiki/Lake_Enriquillo" title="Lake Enriquillo">Lake Enriquillo</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Baker_2008_190_42-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Baker_2008_190-42">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup> The island has an average temperature of 26&#160;°C (78.8&#160;°F) and great climatic and biological diversity.<sup id="cite_ref-consulate_41-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-consulate-41">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup> The country is also the site of the first cathedral, castle, monastery, and fortress built in the Americas, located in Santo Domingo's <a href="/wiki/Ciudad_Colonial_(Santo_Domingo)" title="Ciudad Colonial (Santo Domingo)">Colonial Zone</a>, a <a href="/wiki/World_Heritage_Site" title="World Heritage Site">World Heritage Site</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Colonial_City_of_Santo_Domingo_43-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Colonial_City_of_Santo_Domingo-43">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-unesco.org_44-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-unesco.org-44">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup> The Dominican Republic is highly vulnerable to <a href="/wiki/Natural_disaster" title="Natural disaster">natural disasters</a>.
</p>
<meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" />
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Etymology">Etymology</span></h2>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:127px;"><a href="/wiki/File:St_Dominic_by_Pierre_Le_Gros.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/41/St_Dominic_by_Pierre_Le_Gros.jpg/125px-St_Dominic_by_Pierre_Le_Gros.jpg" decoding="async" width="125" height="217" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/41/St_Dominic_by_Pierre_Le_Gros.jpg/188px-St_Dominic_by_Pierre_Le_Gros.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/41/St_Dominic_by_Pierre_Le_Gros.jpg/250px-St_Dominic_by_Pierre_Le_Gros.jpg 2x" data-file-width="700" data-file-height="1216" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:St_Dominic_by_Pierre_Le_Gros.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>St Dominic, the patron saint of astronomers</div></div></div>
<p>The name Dominican originates from <a href="/wiki/Saint_Dominic" title="Saint Dominic">Santo Domingo de Guzmán</a> (Saint Dominic), the patron saint of <a href="/wiki/Astronomer" title="Astronomer">astronomers</a>, and founder of the <a href="/wiki/Dominican_Order" title="Dominican Order">Dominican Order</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-CIADemo_4-13" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CIADemo-4">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The Dominican Order established a house of high studies on the colony of <a href="/wiki/Captaincy_General_of_Santo_Domingo" title="Captaincy General of Santo Domingo">Santo Domingo</a> that is now known as the <a href="/wiki/Universidad_Santo_Tom%C3%A1s_de_Aquino" title="Universidad Santo Tomás de Aquino">Universidad Autónoma de Santo Domingo</a>, the first University in the New World. They dedicated themselves to the education of the inhabitants of the island, and to the protection of the native <a href="/wiki/Ta%C3%ADno_people" class="mw-redirect" title="Taíno people">Taíno people</a> who were subjected to slavery.<sup id="cite_ref-45" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-45">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>For most of its history, up until independence, the colony was known simply as <i><span title="Spanish-language text"><i lang="es">Santo Domingo</i></span></i><sup id="cite_ref-firstcolony_46-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-firstcolony-46">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup> – the name of its present capital and patron saint, Saint Dominic – and continued to be commonly known as such in English until the early 20th century.<sup id="cite_ref-47" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-47">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup>  The residents were called "Dominicans" (<span title="Spanish-language text"><i lang="es">Dominicanos</i></span>), the adjectival form of "Domingo", and as such, the revolutionaries named their newly independent country the "Dominican Republic" (<span title="Spanish-language text"><i lang="es">la República Dominicana</i></span>).
</p><p>In the <a href="/wiki/National_Anthem_of_the_Dominican_Republic" title="National Anthem of the Dominican Republic">national anthem of the Dominican Republic</a> (<span title="Spanish-language text"><i lang="es">himno nacional de la República Dominicana</i></span>), the term "Dominicans" does not appear. The author of its lyrics, <a href="/wiki/Emilio_Prud%27Homme" title="Emilio Prud&#39;Homme">Emilio Prud'Homme</a>, consistently uses the poetic term "Quisqueyans" (<span title="Spanish-language text"><i lang="es">Quisqueyanos</i></span>). The word "Quisqueya" derives from the <a href="/wiki/Ta%C3%ADno_language" title="Taíno language">Taíno language</a>, and means "mother of the lands" (<span title="Spanish-language text"><i lang="es">madre de las tierras</i></span>). It is often used in songs as another name for the country. The name of the country in English is often shortened to "the D.R." (<span title="Spanish-language text"><i lang="es">la R.D.</i></span>), but this is rare in Spanish.<sup id="cite_ref-Kraft_48-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Kraft-48">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="History">History</span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/History_of_the_Dominican_Republic" title="History of the Dominican Republic">History of the Dominican Republic</a></div>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Pre-European_history">Pre-European history</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Chiefdoms_of_Hispaniola" title="Chiefdoms of Hispaniola">Chiefdoms of Hispaniola</a></div>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Copia_de_Cacicazgos_de_la_Hispaniola.png" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8d/Copia_de_Cacicazgos_de_la_Hispaniola.png/220px-Copia_de_Cacicazgos_de_la_Hispaniola.png" decoding="async" width="220" height="110" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8d/Copia_de_Cacicazgos_de_la_Hispaniola.png/330px-Copia_de_Cacicazgos_de_la_Hispaniola.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8d/Copia_de_Cacicazgos_de_la_Hispaniola.png/440px-Copia_de_Cacicazgos_de_la_Hispaniola.png 2x" data-file-width="2341" data-file-height="1171" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Copia_de_Cacicazgos_de_la_Hispaniola.png" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>The five caciquedoms of Hispaniola</div></div></div>

style="width:161px;max-width:161px"><div class="thumbimage" style="height:119px;overflow:hidden"><a href="/wiki/File:Ta%C3%ADno_pictographs_Cuevas_de_las_Maravillas.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/83/Ta%C3%ADno_pictographs_Cuevas_de_las_Maravillas.jpg/159px-Ta%C3%ADno_pictographs_Cuevas_de_las_Maravillas.jpg" decoding="async" width="159" height="119" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/83/Ta%C3%ADno_pictographs_Cuevas_de_las_Maravillas.jpg/239px-Ta%C3%ADno_pictographs_Cuevas_de_las_Maravillas.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/83/Ta%C3%ADno_pictographs_Cuevas_de_las_Maravillas.jpg/318px-Ta%C3%ADno_pictographs_Cuevas_de_las_Maravillas.jpg 2x" data-file-width="4032" data-file-height="3024" /></a></div></div></div><div class="trow"><div class="tsingle" style="width:255px;max-width:255px"><div class="thumbimage" style="height:168px;overflow:hidden"><a href="/wiki/File:LosTresOjos.JPG" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a7/LosTresOjos.JPG/253px-LosTresOjos.JPG" decoding="async" width="253" height="168" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a7/LosTresOjos.JPG/380px-LosTresOjos.JPG 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a7/LosTresOjos.JPG/506px-LosTresOjos.JPG 2x" data-file-width="3008" data-file-height="2000" /></a></div></div></div><div class="trow" style="display:flex"><div class="thumbcaption">The <a href="/wiki/Pomier_Caves" title="Pomier Caves">Pomier Caves</a> are a series of 55 caves located north of San Cristóbal. They contain the largest collection of 2,000-year-old rock art in the Caribbean.</div></div></div></div>
<p>The <a href="/wiki/Arawakan_languages" title="Arawakan languages">Arawakan-speaking</a> <a href="/wiki/Ta%C3%ADno" title="Taíno">Taíno</a> moved into Hispaniola from the north east region of what is now known as South America, displacing earlier inhabitants,<sup id="cite_ref-LunaC_49-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-LunaC-49">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup> c. 650 C.E. They engaged in farming, fishing,<sup id="cite_ref-Encarta_50-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Encarta-50">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup> hunting and gathering.<sup id="cite_ref-LunaC_49-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-LunaC-49">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup> The fierce <a href="/wiki/Island_Caribs" class="mw-redirect" title="Island Caribs">Caribs</a> drove the Taíno to the northeastern Caribbean, during much of the 15th century.<sup id="cite_ref-51" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-51">&#91;51&#93;</a></sup> The estimates of Hispaniola's population in 1492 vary widely, including tens of thousands,<sup id="cite_ref-52" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-52">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup> one hundred thousand,<sup id="cite_ref-transat_53-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-transat-53">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup> three hundred thousand,<sup id="cite_ref-LunaC_49-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-LunaC-49">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup> and four hundred thousand to two million.<sup id="cite_ref-54" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-54">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup> Determining precisely how many people lived on the island in <a href="/wiki/Pre-Columbian_era" title="Pre-Columbian era">pre-Columbian times</a> is next to impossible, as no accurate records exist.<sup id="cite_ref-55" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-55">&#91;55&#93;</a></sup> By 1492, the island was divided into five Taíno chiefdoms.<sup id="cite_ref-56" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-56">&#91;56&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-57" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-57">&#91;57&#93;</a></sup> The Taíno name for the entire island was either <i>Ayiti</i> or <i>Quisqueya</i>.<sup id="cite_ref-anghiera_58-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-anghiera-58">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup><sup class="noprint Inline-Template noprint noexcerpt Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:NOTRS" class="mw-redirect" title="Wikipedia:NOTRS"><span title="This claim needs references to better sources. (November 2021)">better&#160;source&#160;needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>The Spaniards arrived in 1492. Initially, after friendly relationships, the Taínos resisted the conquest, led by the female Chief <a href="/wiki/Anacaona" title="Anacaona">Anacaona</a> of Xaragua and her ex-husband Chief <a href="/wiki/Caonabo" title="Caonabo">Caonabo</a> of Maguana, as well as Chiefs <a href="/wiki/Guacanagar%C3%ADx" title="Guacanagaríx">Guacanagaríx</a>, <a href="/wiki/Guam%C3%A1" title="Guamá">Guamá</a>, <a href="/wiki/Hatuey" title="Hatuey">Hatuey</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Enriquillo" title="Enriquillo">Enriquillo</a>. The latter's successes gained his people an autonomous enclave for a time on the island. Within a few years after 1492, the population of Taínos had declined drastically, due to <a href="/wiki/Smallpox" title="Smallpox">smallpox</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-59" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-59">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup> measles, and other diseases that arrived with the Europeans.<sup id="cite_ref-history_60-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-history-60">&#91;60&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The first recorded smallpox outbreak, in the Americas, occurred on Hispaniola in 1507.<sup id="cite_ref-history_60-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-history-60">&#91;60&#93;</a></sup> The last record of pure Taínos in the country was from 1864. Still, Taíno biological heritage survived to an important extent, due to intermixing. Census records from 1514 reveal that 40% of Spanish men in Santo Domingo were married to Taíno women,<sup id="cite_ref-61" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-61">&#91;61&#93;</a></sup> and some present-day Dominicans have Taíno ancestry.<sup id="cite_ref-Guitar_62-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Guitar-62">&#91;62&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Martinez_63-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Martinez-63">&#91;63&#93;</a></sup> Remnants of the Taíno culture include their cave paintings,<sup id="cite_ref-64" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-64">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup> such as the <a href="/wiki/Pomier_Caves" title="Pomier Caves">Pomier Caves</a>, as well as pottery designs, which are still used in the small artisan village of Higüerito, <a href="/wiki/Moca,_Dominican_Republic" title="Moca, Dominican Republic">Moca</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-65" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-65">&#91;65&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="European_colonization">European colonization</span></h3>
<p><a href="/wiki/Christopher_Columbus" title="Christopher Columbus">Christopher Columbus</a> arrived on the island on December 5, 1492, during the first of his <a href="/wiki/Voyages_of_Christopher_Columbus" title="Voyages of Christopher Columbus">four voyages</a> to the Americas. He claimed the land for Spain and named it <i>La Española</i>, due to its diverse climate and terrain, which reminded him of the <a href="/wiki/Spain" title="Spain">Spanish landscape</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-66" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-66">&#91;66&#93;</a></sup> In 1496, <a href="/wiki/Bartholomew_Columbus" title="Bartholomew Columbus">Bartholomew Columbus</a>, Christopher's brother, built the city of <a href="/wiki/Ciudad_Colonial_(Santo_Domingo)" title="Ciudad Colonial (Santo Domingo)">Santo Domingo</a>, Western Europe's first permanent settlement in the "<a href="/wiki/New_World" title="New World">New World</a>". The Spaniards created a <a href="/wiki/Plantation_economy" title="Plantation economy">plantation economy</a> on the island.<sup id="cite_ref-transat_53-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-transat-53">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup> The colony was the springboard for the further Spanish conquest of America and for decades the headquarters of Spanish power in the hemisphere.
</p><p>The Taínos nearly disappeared, above all, due to European <a href="/wiki/Infectious_disease" class="mw-redirect" title="Infectious disease">infectious diseases</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-67" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-67">&#91;67&#93;</a></sup> Other causes were abuse, suicide, the breakup of family, starvation,<sup id="cite_ref-LunaC_49-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-LunaC-49">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup> the <a href="/wiki/Encomienda" title="Encomienda">encomienda</a> system,<sup id="cite_ref-68" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-68">&#91;68&#93;</a></sup> which resembled a <a href="/wiki/Feudalism" title="Feudalism">feudal system</a> in Medieval Europe,<sup id="cite_ref-69" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-69">&#91;69&#93;</a></sup> war with the Spaniards, changes in lifestyle, and mixing with other peoples. Laws passed for the native peoples' protection (beginning with the <a href="/wiki/Laws_of_Burgos" title="Laws of Burgos">Laws of Burgos, 1512–1513</a>)<sup id="cite_ref-70" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-70">&#91;70&#93;</a></sup> were never truly enforced. African slaves were imported to replace the dwindling Taínos.
</p>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Maroons_preparing_to_ambush_a_convoy.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/74/Maroons_preparing_to_ambush_a_convoy.jpg/220px-Maroons_preparing_to_ambush_a_convoy.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="159" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/74/Maroons_preparing_to_ambush_a_convoy.jpg/330px-Maroons_preparing_to_ambush_a_convoy.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/74/Maroons_preparing_to_ambush_a_convoy.jpg/440px-Maroons_preparing_to_ambush_a_convoy.jpg 2x" data-file-width="897" data-file-height="648" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Maroons_preparing_to_ambush_a_convoy.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Maroons preparing for an ambush</div></div></div>
<p>On December 25, 1521, enslaved Africans of <a href="/wiki/Senegalese" class="mw-redirect" title="Senegalese">Senegalese</a> <a href="/wiki/Wolof_people" title="Wolof people">Wolof</a> origin led the first major <a href="/wiki/Slave_rebellion" title="Slave rebellion">slave revolt</a> of the Americas on the plantation of <a href="/wiki/Diego_Columbus" title="Diego Columbus">Diego Colón</a>, son of <a href="/wiki/Christopher_Columbus" title="Christopher Columbus">Christopher Columbus</a>. They fought the Spanish colonists for a year, until the rebellion was brutally crushed in December 1522. After this, laws were passed in order to enforce harsh punishments on those who planned to stage another uprising. But despite this, slave revolts continued to transpire as many of the slaves successfully escaped. This also resulted in the establishment of the first <a href="/wiki/Maroons" title="Maroons">Maroon</a> communities of the Americas, and many Maroon leaders emerged from these revolts. Leaders such as <a href="/wiki/Sebasti%C3%A1n_Lemba" title="Sebastián Lemba">Sebastian Lemba</a>, a Maroon born in Africa who successfully rebelled in 1532, became the most prolific leader of this era. His actions would inspire other leaders such as Juan Vaquero, Diego del Guzmán, Fernando Montoro, Juan Criollo and Diego del Campo, to lead successful revolts of their own. Maroons would continue to place Spanish control in jeopardy, as many parts of the island fell under Maroon control. Although many of the leaders would eventually be captured and executed by the Admiral, Maroon activities would continue to be present in the island well into the 17th century.<sup id="cite_ref-71" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-71">&#91;71&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>After its conquest of the <a href="/wiki/Aztec_Empire" title="Aztec Empire">Aztecs</a> and <a href="/wiki/Inca_Empire" title="Inca Empire">Incas</a>, Spain neglected its Caribbean holdings. Hispaniola's sugar plantation economy quickly declined. Most Spanish colonists left for the silver-mines of <a href="/wiki/New_Spain" title="New Spain">Mexico</a> and <a href="/wiki/Viceroyalty_of_Peru" title="Viceroyalty of Peru">Peru</a>, while new immigrants from Spain bypassed the island. Agriculture dwindled, new imports of slaves ceased, and white colonists, free blacks, and slaves alike lived in poverty, weakening the racial hierarchy and aiding <i>intermixing</i>, resulting in a population of predominantly mixed Spaniard, Taíno, and African descent. Except for the city of Santo Domingo, which managed to maintain some legal exports, Dominican ports were forced to rely on contraband trade, which, along with livestock, became one of the main sources of livelihood for the island's inhabitants.
</p><p>In the mid-17th century, France sent colonists to settle the island of <a href="/wiki/Tortuga_(Haiti)" title="Tortuga (Haiti)">Tortuga</a> and the northwestern coast of Hispaniola (which the Spaniards had <a href="/wiki/Devastations_of_Osorio" title="Devastations of Osorio">abandoned by 1606</a>) due to its strategic position in the region. In order to entice the <a href="/wiki/Pirate" class="mw-redirect" title="Pirate">pirates</a>, France supplied them with women who had been taken from prisons, accused of prostitution and thieving. After decades of armed struggles with the French settlers, Spain ceded the western coast of the island to France with the 1697 <a href="/wiki/Treaty_of_Ryswick" class="mw-redirect" title="Treaty of Ryswick">Treaty of Ryswick</a>, whilst the Central Plateau remained under Spanish domain. France created a wealthy colony on the island, while the Spanish colony continued to suffer economic decline.<sup id="cite_ref-google48_72-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-google48-72">&#91;72&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On April 17, 1655, <a href="/wiki/Commonwealth_of_England" title="Commonwealth of England">English</a> forces landed on Hispaniola, and marched 30 miles overland to <a href="/wiki/Santo_Domingo" title="Santo Domingo">Santo Domingo</a>, the main Spanish stronghold on the island, where they <a href="/wiki/Siege_of_Santo_Domingo_(1655)" title="Siege of Santo Domingo (1655)">laid siege</a> to it. Spanish <a href="/wiki/Lancer" title="Lancer">lancers</a> attacked the English forces, sending them careening back toward the beach in confusion. The English commander hid behind a tree where, in the words of one of his soldiers, he was "so much possessed with terror that he could hardly speak".<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (March 2021)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> The Spanish defenders who had secured victory were rewarded with titles from the <a href="/wiki/Spanish_Empire" title="Spanish Empire">Spanish Crown</a>.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="18th_century">18th century</span></h3>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:172px;"><a href="/wiki/File:DOMREP-s-dom-panteon-innen.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/02/DOMREP-s-dom-panteon-innen.jpg/170px-DOMREP-s-dom-panteon-innen.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="256" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/02/DOMREP-s-dom-panteon-innen.jpg/255px-DOMREP-s-dom-panteon-innen.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/02/DOMREP-s-dom-panteon-innen.jpg/340px-DOMREP-s-dom-panteon-innen.jpg 2x" data-file-width="807" data-file-height="1215" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:DOMREP-s-dom-panteon-innen.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>National pantheon in Santo Domingo built from 1714 to 1746</div></div></div>
<p>The <a href="/wiki/House_of_Bourbon" title="House of Bourbon">House of Bourbon</a> replaced the <a href="/wiki/House_of_Habsburg" title="House of Habsburg">House of Habsburg</a> in Spain in 1700, and introduced economic reforms that gradually began to revive trade in Santo Domingo. The crown progressively relaxed the rigid controls and restrictions on commerce between Spain and the colonies and among the colonies. The last <i>flotas</i> sailed in 1737; the monopoly port system was abolished shortly thereafter. By the middle of the century, the population was bolstered by emigration from the <a href="/wiki/Canary_Islands" title="Canary Islands">Canary Islands</a>, resettling the northern part of the colony and planting tobacco in the <a href="/wiki/Cibao_Valley" class="mw-redirect" title="Cibao Valley">Cibao Valley</a>, and importation of slaves was renewed.
</p><p>Santo Domingo's exports soared and the island's agricultural productivity rose, which was assisted by the involvement of <a href="/wiki/History_of_Spain_(1700%E2%80%931810)" class="mw-redirect" title="History of Spain (1700–1810)">Spain</a> in the <a href="/wiki/Seven_Years%27_War" title="Seven Years&#39; War">Seven Years' War</a>, allowing <a href="/wiki/Privateer" title="Privateer">privateers</a> operating out of Santo Domingo to once again patrol surrounding waters for enemy <a href="/wiki/Merchant_ship" title="Merchant ship">merchantmen</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Hazard_73-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Hazard-73">&#91;73&#93;</a></sup> Dominican privateers in the service of the Spanish Crown had already been active in the <a href="/wiki/War_of_Jenkins%27_Ear" title="War of Jenkins&#39; Ear">War of Jenkins' Ear</a> just two decades prior, and they sharply reduced the amount of enemy trade operating in <a href="/wiki/West_Indies" title="West Indies">West Indian</a> waters.<sup id="cite_ref-Hazard_73-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Hazard-73">&#91;73&#93;</a></sup> The <a href="/wiki/Prize_(law)" title="Prize (law)">prizes</a> they took were carried back to Santo Domingo, where their cargoes were sold to the colony's inhabitants or to foreign merchants doing business there. The enslaved population of the colony also rose dramatically, as numerous captive Africans were taken from enemy <a href="/wiki/Slave_ship" title="Slave ship">slave ships</a> in West Indian waters.<sup id="cite_ref-Hazard_73-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Hazard-73">&#91;73&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-74" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-74">&#91;74&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Between 1720 and 1774, Dominican privateers cruised the waters from Santo Domingo to the coast of <a href="/wiki/Southern_Caribbean" title="Southern Caribbean">Tierra Firme</a>, taking British, French, and Dutch ships with cargoes of African slaves and other commodities.<sup id="cite_ref-75" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-75">&#91;75&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:172px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Hispaniola_Treaty_of_Aranjuez_1777.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/05/Hispaniola_Treaty_of_Aranjuez_1777.jpg/170px-Hispaniola_Treaty_of_Aranjuez_1777.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="226" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/05/Hispaniola_Treaty_of_Aranjuez_1777.jpg/255px-Hispaniola_Treaty_of_Aranjuez_1777.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/05/Hispaniola_Treaty_of_Aranjuez_1777.jpg/340px-Hispaniola_Treaty_of_Aranjuez_1777.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1038" data-file-height="1378" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Hispaniola_Treaty_of_Aranjuez_1777.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Contemporary map showing the border situation on Hispaniola following the <a href="/wiki/Treaty_of_Aranjuez_(1777)" title="Treaty of Aranjuez (1777)">Treaty of Aranjuez (1777)</a></div></div></div>
<p>The colony of Santo Domingo saw a population increase during the 18th century, as it rose to about 91,272 in 1750. Of this number, approximately 38,272 were white landowners, 38,000 were free mixed people of color, and some 15,000 were slaves.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (October 2020)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> This contrasted sharply with the population of the French colony of Saint-Domingue (present-day Haiti)&#160;– the wealthiest colony in the Caribbean and whose population of one-half a million was 90% enslaved and overall, seven times as numerous as the Spanish colony of Santo Domingo.<sup id="cite_ref-google48_72-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-google48-72">&#91;72&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-76" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-76">&#91;76&#93;</a></sup> The 'Spanish' settlers, whose blood by now was mixed with that of Taínos, Africans, and Canary <a href="/wiki/Guanches" title="Guanches">Guanches</a>, proclaimed: 'It does not matter if the French are richer than us, we are still the true inheritors of this island. In our veins runs the blood of the heroic <i>conquistadores</i> who won this island of ours with sword and blood.' As restrictions on colonial trade were relaxed, the colonial elites of Saint-Domingue offered the principal market for Santo Domingo's exports of beef, hides, mahogany, and tobacco. With the outbreak of the <a href="/wiki/Haitian_Revolution" title="Haitian Revolution">Haitian Revolution</a> in 1791, the rich urban families linked to the colonial bureaucracy fled the island, while most of the rural <i>hateros</i> (cattle ranchers) remained, even though they lost their principal market.
</p><p>Inspired by disputes between whites and mulattoes in Saint-Domingue, a slave revolt broke out in the French colony. Although the population of Santo Domingo was perhaps one-fourth that of Saint-Domingue, this did not prevent the King of Spain from launching an invasion of the French side of the island in 1793, attempting to seize all, or part, of the western third of the island  in an alliance of convenience with the rebellious slaves.<sup id="cite_ref-Scheina_77-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Scheina-77">&#91;77&#93;</a></sup> In August 1793, a <a href="/wiki/Column_(formation)" title="Column (formation)">column</a> of Dominican troops advanced into Saint-Domingue and were joined by Haitian rebels.<sup id="cite_ref-78" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-78">&#91;78&#93;</a></sup> However, these rebels soon turned against Spain and instead joined France. The Dominicans were not defeated militarily, but their advance was restrained, and when in 1795 Spain ceded Santo Domingo to France by the <a href="/wiki/Peace_of_Basel" title="Peace of Basel">Treaty of Basel</a>, Dominican attacks on Saint-Domingue ceased.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="French_occupation">French occupation</span></h3>
<p>Between 1795-1802, the French colonists would endure and suppress several slave revolts in Santo Domingo such as the back to back revolts of Hincha and Samaná in the spring of 1795, the large-scale Nigua rebellion in 1796, and the Gambia revolt of 1802.<sup id="cite_ref-79" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-79">&#91;79&#93;</a></sup> After declaring independence in 1804, <a href="/wiki/Jean-Jacques_Dessalines" title="Jean-Jacques Dessalines">Jean-Jacques Dessalines</a>, attempted to take control of the eastern side of the island in 1805, laying <a href="/wiki/Siege_of_Santo_Domingo_(1805)" title="Siege of Santo Domingo (1805)">siege to the city</a> until being forced to retreat in light of news of a possible invasion by a French naval squadron believed to be heading towards Haiti, and during his retreat, subjecting the Dominicans to a <a href="/wiki/Massacre_of_Moca,_Santo_Domingo" class="mw-redirect" title="Massacre of Moca, Santo Domingo">massacre</a>. The French retained Santo Domingo until 1809, when combined Spanish and Dominican forces, aided by the British, defeated the French, leading to a <a href="/wiki/Espa%C3%B1a_Boba" title="España Boba">recolonization by Spain</a>.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Ephemeral_independence">Ephemeral independence</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Republic_of_Spanish_Haiti" title="Republic of Spanish Haiti">Republic of Spanish Haiti</a></div>
<p>After a dozen years of discontent and failed independence plots by various opposing groups, including a failed 1812 revolt led by Dominican conspirators José Leocadio, Pedro de Seda, and Pedro Henríquez, Santo Domingo's former Lieutenant-Governor (top administrator), <a href="/wiki/Jos%C3%A9_N%C3%BA%C3%B1ez_de_C%C3%A1ceres" title="José Núñez de Cáceres">José Núñez de Cáceres</a>, declared the colony's independence from the <a href="/wiki/Spanish_Empire" title="Spanish Empire">Spanish crown</a> as <a href="/wiki/Republic_of_Spanish_Haiti" title="Republic of Spanish Haiti">Spanish Haiti</a>, on November 30, 1821. This period is also known as the Ephemeral independence.<sup id="cite_ref-Bethell1986_80-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Bethell1986-80">&#91;80&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span id="Haitian_occupation_of_Santo_Domingo_.281822.E2.80.9344.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Haitian_occupation_of_Santo_Domingo_(1822–44)">Haitian occupation of Santo Domingo (1822–44)</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Haitian_occupation_of_Santo_Domingo" title="Haitian occupation of Santo Domingo">Haitian occupation of Santo Domingo</a></div>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Hispaniola_1820-1822.png" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5e/Hispaniola_1820-1822.png/220px-Hispaniola_1820-1822.png" decoding="async" width="220" height="99" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5e/Hispaniola_1820-1822.png/330px-Hispaniola_1820-1822.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5e/Hispaniola_1820-1822.png/440px-Hispaniola_1820-1822.png 2x" data-file-width="1352" data-file-height="611" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Hispaniola_1820-1822.png" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Hispaniola</div></div></div>
<p>The newly independent republic ended two months later under the Haitian government led by <a href="/wiki/Jean-Pierre_Boyer" title="Jean-Pierre Boyer">Jean-Pierre Boyer</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-guitar_81-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-guitar-81">&#91;81&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>As Toussaint Louverture had done two decades earlier, the Haitians abolished slavery. In order to raise funds for the huge indemnity of 150 million francs that Haiti agreed to pay the former French colonists, and which was subsequently lowered to 60 million francs, the Haitian government imposed heavy taxes on the Dominicans. Since Haiti was unable to adequately provision its army, the occupying forces largely survived by commandeering or confiscating food and supplies at gunpoint. Attempts to <a href="/wiki/Land_reform" title="Land reform">redistribute</a> land conflicted with the system of communal land tenure (<i>terrenos comuneros</i>), which had arisen with the ranching economy, and some people resented being forced to grow cash crops under Boyer and <a href="/wiki/Joseph_Balthazar_Inginac" title="Joseph Balthazar Inginac">Joseph Balthazar Inginac</a>'s <i>Code Rural</i>.<sup id="cite_ref-82" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-82">&#91;a&#93;</a></sup> In the rural and rugged mountainous areas, the Haitian administration was usually too inefficient to enforce its own laws. It was in the city of Santo Domingo that the effects of the occupation were most acutely felt, and it was there that the movement for independence originated.
</p><p>The Haitians associated the <a href="/wiki/Roman_Catholic_Church" class="mw-redirect" title="Roman Catholic Church">Roman Catholic Church</a> with the French slave-masters who had exploited them before independence and confiscated all church property, deported all foreign clergy, and severed the ties of the remaining clergy to the <a href="/wiki/Holy_See" title="Holy See">Vatican</a>. All levels of education collapsed; the university was shut down, as it was starved both of resources and students, with young Dominican men from 16 to 25 years old being drafted into the Haitian army.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (August 2021)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> Boyer's occupation troops, who were largely Dominicans,<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (July 2021)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> were unpaid and had to "forage and sack" from Dominican civilians. Haiti imposed a "heavy tribute" on the Dominican people.<sup id="cite_ref-Matibag_83-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Matibag-83">&#91;82&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: page number needed">&#58;&#8202;page number needed&#8202;</span></sup>
</p><p>Haiti's constitution forbade white elites from owning land, and Dominican major landowning families were forcibly deprived of their properties. During this time, many white elites in Santo Domingo did not consider owning slaves due to the economic crisis that Santo Domingo faced during the <a href="/wiki/Espa%C3%B1a_Boba" title="España Boba">España Boba</a> period. The few landowners that wanted slavery established in Santo Domingo had to emigrate to <a href="/wiki/Captaincy_General_of_Cuba" title="Captaincy General of Cuba">Cuba</a>, <a href="/wiki/Captaincy_General_of_Puerto_Rico" title="Captaincy General of Puerto Rico">Puerto Rico</a>, or <a href="/wiki/Gran_Colombia" title="Gran Colombia">Gran Colombia</a>. Many landowning families stayed on the island, with a heavy concentration of landowners settling in the Cibao region. After independence, and eventually being under <a href="/wiki/Spanish_occupation_of_the_Dominican_Republic" title="Spanish occupation of the Dominican Republic">Spanish rule</a> once again in 1861, many families returned to Santo Domingo including new waves of immigration from <a href="/wiki/Spain" title="Spain">Spain</a>.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (July 2020)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p>
<h3><span id="Dominican_War_of_Independence_.281844.E2.80.9356.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Dominican_War_of_Independence_(1844–56)">Dominican War of Independence (1844–56)</span></h3>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:152px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Juan_pablo_duarte_diez.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a7/Juan_pablo_duarte_diez.jpg/150px-Juan_pablo_duarte_diez.jpg" decoding="async" width="150" height="202" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a7/Juan_pablo_duarte_diez.jpg/225px-Juan_pablo_duarte_diez.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a7/Juan_pablo_duarte_diez.jpg/300px-Juan_pablo_duarte_diez.jpg 2x" data-file-width="600" data-file-height="806" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Juan_pablo_duarte_diez.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div><a href="/wiki/Juan_Pablo_Duarte" title="Juan Pablo Duarte">Juan Pablo Duarte</a>, founding father of the Dominican Republic.</div></div></div>
<p>In 1838, <a href="/wiki/Juan_Pablo_Duarte" title="Juan Pablo Duarte">Juan Pablo Duarte</a> founded a secret society called <a href="/wiki/La_Trinitaria_(Dominican_Republic)" title="La Trinitaria (Dominican Republic)">La Trinitaria</a>, which sought the complete independence of Santo Domingo without any foreign intervention.<sup id="cite_ref-pons_84-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-pons-84">&#91;83&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: p147–149">&#58;&#8202;p147–149&#8202;</span></sup> Also <a href="/wiki/Francisco_del_Rosario_S%C3%A1nchez" title="Francisco del Rosario Sánchez">Francisco del Rosario Sánchez</a> and <a href="/wiki/Ramon_Matias_Mella" class="mw-redirect" title="Ramon Matias Mella">Ramon Matias Mella</a>, despite not being among the founding members of La Trinitaria, were decisive in the fight for independence. Duarte, Mella, and Sánchez are considered the three Founding Fathers of the Dominican Republic.<sup id="cite_ref-85" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-85">&#91;84&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1843, the new Haitian president, <a href="/wiki/Charles_Rivi%C3%A8re-H%C3%A9rard" title="Charles Rivière-Hérard">Charles Rivière-Hérard</a>, exiled or imprisoned the leading <i>Trinitarios</i> (Trinitarians).<sup id="cite_ref-Scheina_77-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Scheina-77">&#91;77&#93;</a></sup> After subduing the Dominicans, Rivière-Hérard, a mulatto, faced a rebellion by blacks in <a href="/wiki/Port-au-Prince" title="Port-au-Prince">Port-au-Prince</a>. Haiti had formed two regiments composed of Dominicans from the city of Santo Domingo; these were used by Rivière-Hérard to suppress the uprising.<sup id="cite_ref-Scheina_77-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Scheina-77">&#91;77&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:152px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Flag_of_the_Dominican_Republic_(up_to_1844).svg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/59/Flag_of_the_Dominican_Republic_%28up_to_1844%29.svg/150px-Flag_of_the_Dominican_Republic_%28up_to_1844%29.svg.png" decoding="async" width="150" height="94" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/59/Flag_of_the_Dominican_Republic_%28up_to_1844%29.svg/225px-Flag_of_the_Dominican_Republic_%28up_to_1844%29.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/59/Flag_of_the_Dominican_Republic_%28up_to_1844%29.svg/300px-Flag_of_the_Dominican_Republic_%28up_to_1844%29.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="800" data-file-height="500" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Flag_of_the_Dominican_Republic_(up_to_1844).svg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Original <a href="/wiki/Flag_of_the_Dominican_Republic" title="Flag of the Dominican Republic">flag of the Dominican Republic</a> (up to 1849)</div></div></div>
<p>On February 27, 1844, the surviving members of <i>La Trinitaria</i>, now led by <a href="/wiki/Tom%C3%A1s_Bobadilla" title="Tomás Bobadilla">Tomás Bobadilla</a>, declared the independence from Haiti. The <i>Trinitarios</i> were backed by <a href="/wiki/Pedro_Santana" title="Pedro Santana">Pedro Santana</a>, a wealthy cattle rancher from <a href="/wiki/El_Seibo" class="mw-redirect" title="El Seibo">El Seibo</a>, who became general of the army of the nascent republic. The Dominican Republic's first <a href="/wiki/Constitution_of_the_Dominican_Republic" title="Constitution of the Dominican Republic">Constitution</a> was adopted on November 6, 1844, and was modeled after the <a href="/wiki/United_States_Constitution" class="mw-redirect" title="United States Constitution">United States Constitution</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Encarta_50-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Encarta-50">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup> The decades that followed were filled with tyranny, factionalism, economic difficulties, rapid changes of government, and exile for political opponents. Archrivals Santana and <a href="/wiki/Buenaventura_B%C3%A1ez" title="Buenaventura Báez">Buenaventura Báez</a> held power most of the time, both ruling arbitrarily. They promoted competing plans to annex the new nation to another power: Santana favored Spain, and Báez the United States.
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1096954695/mw-parser-output/.tmulti"/>
Threatening the nation's independence were renewed Haitian invasions. In March 1844, Rivière-Hérard attempted to reimpose his authority, but the Dominicans put up stiff opposition and inflicted heavy casualties on the Haitians.<sup id="cite_ref-86" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-86">&#91;85&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-87" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-87">&#91;86&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:169px;"><a href="/wiki/File:J-M-Imbert.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/1/14/J-M-Imbert.jpg" decoding="async" width="167" height="169" class="thumbimage" data-file-width="167" data-file-height="169" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:J-M-Imbert.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>General <a href="/wiki/Jos%C3%A9_Mar%C3%ADa_Imbert" title="José María Imbert">José María Imbert</a>. He led Dominican troops in the <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Santiago_(1844)" title="Battle of Santiago (1844)">Battle of Santiago</a>.</div></div></div>
<p>The <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Azua" title="Battle of Azua">Battle of Azua</a> was the first major battle of the <a href="/wiki/Dominican_War_of_Independence" title="Dominican War of Independence">Dominican War of Independence</a> and was fought on March 19. The Dominicans opened the battle with a cannon barrage followed by rifle discharges and machete charges. When the Haitian commander, Vicent Jean Degales, was beheaded by the Dominicans, his troops retreated in disarray. The outnumbered Dominican forces suffered only five casualties in the battle while the Haitians sustained over 1,000 killed. The <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Santiago_(1844)" title="Battle of Santiago (1844)">Battle of Santiago</a> was the second major battle of the war and was fought on March 30. The Haitians charged the Dominicans under <a href="/wiki/Grapeshot" title="Grapeshot">grapeshot</a> and musketry fire and were repulsed. At sea, the Dominicans defeated the Haitians at the <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Tortuguero" title="Battle of Tortuguero">Battle of Tortuguero</a> off the coast of Azua on April 15, temporarily expelling Haitian forces.
</p><p>In early July 1844, Duarte was urged by his followers to take the title of President of the Republic. Duarte agreed, but only if free elections were arranged. However, Santana's forces took Santo Domingo on July 12, and they declared Santana ruler of the Dominican Republic. Santana then put Mella, Duarte, and Sánchez in jail. On February 27, 1845, Santana executed <a href="/wiki/Mar%C3%ADa_Trinidad_S%C3%A1nchez" title="María Trinidad Sánchez">María Trinidad Sánchez</a>, heroine of La Trinitaria, and others for conspiracy.
</p><p>On June 17, 1845, small Dominican <a href="/wiki/Detachment_(military)" title="Detachment (military)">detachments</a> invaded Haiti, capturing <a href="/wiki/Lascahobas" title="Lascahobas">Lascahobas</a> and <a href="/wiki/Hinche" title="Hinche">Hinche</a>. The Dominicans established an <a href="/wiki/Outpost_(military)" title="Outpost (military)">outpost</a> at Cachimán, but the arrival of Haitian reinforcements soon compelled them to retreat back across the frontier. Haiti launched a new invasion on August 6. A member of La Trinitaria, José María Serra, claimed that over 3,000 Haitian soldiers and less than 20 Dominican militias had been killed at this point.<sup id="cite_ref-Republicana_88-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Republicana-88">&#91;87&#93;</a></sup> On September 17, the Dominicans defeated the Haitian <a href="/wiki/Vanguard" title="Vanguard">vanguard</a> near the frontier at the <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Estrelleta" title="Battle of Estrelleta">Battle of Estrelleta</a>, where the Dominican <a href="/wiki/Infantry_square" title="Infantry square">infantry square</a> repulsed a Haitian cavalry charge with bayonets. The Dominicans suffered no deaths during the battle and only three wounded. On November 27, the Dominicans defeated the Haitian army at the <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Beler" title="Battle of Beler">Battle of Beler</a>. Haitian losses were 350 killed, while the Dominicans suffered 16 killed. Among the dead  were three Haitian generals, including the army's commander, Seraphin. The Dominicans repelled the Haitian forces, on both land and sea, by December 1845.
</p><p>The Haitians invaded again in 1849, forcing the president of the Dominican Republic, <a href="/wiki/Manuel_Jimenes" title="Manuel Jimenes">Manuel Jimenes</a>, to call upon Santana, whom he had ousted as president, to lead the Dominicans against this new invasion. Santana met the enemy at <a href="/wiki/San_Jos%C3%A9_de_Ocoa" title="San José de Ocoa">Ocoa</a>, April 21, with only 400 militiamen, and succeeded in defeating the 18,000-strong Haitian army.<sup id="cite_ref-89" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-89">&#91;88&#93;</a></sup> The battle began with heavy cannon fire by the entrenched Haitians and ended with a Dominican assault followed by <a href="/wiki/Hand-to-hand_combat" title="Hand-to-hand combat">hand-to-hand combat</a>. Three Haitian generals were killed. In November 1849, Dominican seamen raided the Haitian coasts, plundered seaside villages, as far as <a href="/wiki/Dame-Marie,_Grand%27Anse" title="Dame-Marie, Grand&#39;Anse">Dame Marie</a>, and butchered crews of captured enemy ships.<sup id="cite_ref-90" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-90">&#91;89&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-91" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-91">&#91;90&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>By 1854, both countries were at war again. In November, a Dominican squadron composed of the <a href="/wiki/Brigantine" title="Brigantine">brigantine</a> <i>27 de Febrero</i> and <a href="/wiki/Schooner" title="Schooner">schooner</a> <i>Constitución</i> captured a Haitian warship and bombarded <a href="/wiki/Anse-%C3%A0-Pitres" title="Anse-à-Pitres">Anse-à-Pitres</a> and <a href="/wiki/Belle-Anse" title="Belle-Anse">Saltrou</a>. In November 1855, Haiti invaded again. Over 1,000 Haitians (including two generals) were killed in the battles of Santomé and Cambronal in December 1855. The Haitians suffered 1,500 killed at Sabana Larga and Jácuba in January 1856, and in an engagement at <a href="/wiki/Ouanaminthe" title="Ouanaminthe">Ouanaminthe</a>, over 1,000 Haitian troops were killed and many were wounded and missing.<sup id="cite_ref-92" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-92">&#91;91&#93;</a></sup>
</p>

<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="First_Republic">First Republic</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1096954695/mw-parser-output/.tmulti"/><div class="thumb tmulti tright"><div class="thumbinner multiimageinner" style="width:292px;max-width:292px"><div class="trow"><div class="tsingle" style="width:152px;max-width:152px"><div class="thumbimage"><a href="/wiki/File:Santana.gif" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0d/Santana.gif/150px-Santana.gif" decoding="async" width="150" height="196" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0d/Santana.gif/225px-Santana.gif 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0d/Santana.gif/300px-Santana.gif 2x" data-file-width="383" data-file-height="500" /></a></div></div><div class="tsingle" style="width:136px;max-width:136px"><div class="thumbimage"><a href="/wiki/File:Buenaventura_Ba%C3%A9z.gif" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7e/Buenaventura_Ba%C3%A9z.gif/134px-Buenaventura_Ba%C3%A9z.gif" decoding="async" width="134" height="196" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7e/Buenaventura_Ba%C3%A9z.gif/201px-Buenaventura_Ba%C3%A9z.gif 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7e/Buenaventura_Ba%C3%A9z.gif/268px-Buenaventura_Ba%C3%A9z.gif 2x" data-file-width="1715" data-file-height="2505" /></a></div></div></div><div class="trow" style="display:flex"><div class="thumbcaption"><a href="/wiki/Pedro_Santana" title="Pedro Santana">Pedro Santana</a> and <a href="/wiki/Buenaventura_B%C3%A1ez" title="Buenaventura Báez">Buenaventura Báez</a>, the <i><a href="/wiki/Caudillo" title="Caudillo">caudillos</a></i> who led the Dominican Republic during its first republican period</div></div></div></div>
<p>The Dominican Republic's first <a href="/wiki/Constitution_of_the_Dominican_Republic" title="Constitution of the Dominican Republic">constitution</a> was adopted on November 6, 1844. The state was commonly known as Santo Domingo in English until the early 20th century.<sup id="cite_ref-93" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-93">&#91;92&#93;</a></sup>  It featured a presidential form of government with many liberal tendencies, but it was marred by Article 210, imposed by <a href="/wiki/Pedro_Santana" title="Pedro Santana">Pedro Santana</a> on the constitutional assembly by force, giving him the privileges of a dictatorship until the war of independence was over. These privileges not only served him to win the war but also allowed him to persecute, execute and drive into exile his political opponents, among which Duarte was the most important.
</p><p>The constant threat of renewed Haitian invasion required all men of fighting age to take up arms in defense against the Haitian military. Theoretically, fighting age was generally defined as between 15 and 18 years of age to 40 or 50 years. Despite wide, popular glorification of military service, many in the ranks of the Liberation Army were <a href="/wiki/Mutiny" title="Mutiny">mutinous</a> and <a href="/wiki/Desertion" title="Desertion">desertion</a> rates were high despite penalties as severe as death for shirking the obligation of military service.
</p><p>The population of the Dominican Republic in 1845 was approximately 230,000 people (100,000 whites; 40,000 blacks; and 90,000 mulattoes).<sup id="cite_ref-94" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-94">&#91;93&#93;</a></sup> Due to the rugged mountainous terrain of the island the regions of the Dominican Republic developed in isolation from one another. In the south, also known at the time as Ozama, the economy was dominated by cattle-ranching (particularly in the southeastern savannah) and cutting mahogany and other hardwoods for export. This region retained a semi-feudal character, with little commercial agriculture, the hacienda as the dominant social unit, and the majority of the population living at a subsistence level. In the north (better-known as Cibao), the nation's richest farmland, farmers supplemented their subsistence crops by growing tobacco for export, mainly to Germany. Tobacco required less land than cattle ranching and was mainly grown by smallholders, who relied on itinerant traders to transport their crops to Puerto Plata and Monte Cristi. Santana antagonized the Cibao farmers, enriching himself and his supporters at their expense by resorting to multiple peso printings that allowed him to buy their crops for a fraction of their value. In 1848, he was forced to resign and was succeeded by his vice-president, <a href="/wiki/Manuel_Jimenes" title="Manuel Jimenes">Manuel Jimenes</a>.
</p><p>After defeating a new Haitian invasion in 1849, Santana marched on Santo Domingo and deposed Jimenes in a <a href="/wiki/Coup_d%27%C3%A9tat" title="Coup d&#39;état">coup d'état</a>. At his behest, Congress elected <a href="/wiki/Buenaventura_B%C3%A1ez" title="Buenaventura Báez">Buenaventura Báez</a> as president, but Báez was unwilling to serve as Santana's puppet, challenging his role as the country's acknowledged military leader. In 1853, Santana was elected president for his second term, forcing Báez into exile. Three years later, he negotiated a treaty leasing a portion of <a href="/wiki/Saman%C3%A1_Peninsula" title="Samaná Peninsula">Samaná Peninsula</a> to a U.S. company; popular opposition forced him to abdicate, enabling Báez to return and seize power. With the treasury depleted, Báez printed eighteen million uninsured pesos, purchasing the 1857 tobacco crop with this currency and exporting it for hard cash at immense profit to himself and his followers. Cibao tobacco planters, who were ruined when hyperinflation ensued, revolted and formed a new government headed by <a href="/wiki/Jos%C3%A9_Desiderio_Valverde" title="José Desiderio Valverde">José Desiderio Valverde</a> and headquartered in Santiago de los Caballeros. In July 1857, General Juan Luis Franco Bidó besieged Santo Domingo. The Cibao-based government declared an amnesty to exiles and Santana returned and managed to replace Franco Bidó in September 1857. After a year of civil war, Santana captured Santo Domingo in June 1858, overthrew both Báez and Valverde and installed himself as president.<sup id="cite_ref-Cross_95-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Cross-95">&#91;94&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Restoration_republic">Restoration republic</span></h3>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Jura_del_gobernador_y_capit%C3%A1n_general_de_Santo_Domingo,_don_Pedro_Santana_(Museo_del_Prado).jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c7/Jura_del_gobernador_y_capit%C3%A1n_general_de_Santo_Domingo%2C_don_Pedro_Santana_%28Museo_del_Prado%29.jpg/220px-Jura_del_gobernador_y_capit%C3%A1n_general_de_Santo_Domingo%2C_don_Pedro_Santana_%28Museo_del_Prado%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="167" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c7/Jura_del_gobernador_y_capit%C3%A1n_general_de_Santo_Domingo%2C_don_Pedro_Santana_%28Museo_del_Prado%29.jpg/330px-Jura_del_gobernador_y_capit%C3%A1n_general_de_Santo_Domingo%2C_don_Pedro_Santana_%28Museo_del_Prado%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c7/Jura_del_gobernador_y_capit%C3%A1n_general_de_Santo_Domingo%2C_don_Pedro_Santana_%28Museo_del_Prado%29.jpg/440px-Jura_del_gobernador_y_capit%C3%A1n_general_de_Santo_Domingo%2C_don_Pedro_Santana_%28Museo_del_Prado%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2953" data-file-height="2245" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Jura_del_gobernador_y_capit%C3%A1n_general_de_Santo_Domingo,_don_Pedro_Santana_(Museo_del_Prado).jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Pedro Santana is sworn in as governor-general of the new Spanish province.</div></div></div>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Soldados_espa%C3%B1oles_desembarcan_en_Monte_Cristi.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/cf/Soldados_espa%C3%B1oles_desembarcan_en_Monte_Cristi.jpg/220px-Soldados_espa%C3%B1oles_desembarcan_en_Monte_Cristi.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="157" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/cf/Soldados_espa%C3%B1oles_desembarcan_en_Monte_Cristi.jpg/330px-Soldados_espa%C3%B1oles_desembarcan_en_Monte_Cristi.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/cf/Soldados_espa%C3%B1oles_desembarcan_en_Monte_Cristi.jpg/440px-Soldados_espa%C3%B1oles_desembarcan_en_Monte_Cristi.jpg 2x" data-file-width="984" data-file-height="700" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Soldados_espa%C3%B1oles_desembarcan_en_Monte_Cristi.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div><a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Monte_Cristi" title="Battle of Monte Cristi">Battle of Monte Cristi</a> (1864)</div></div></div>
<p>In 1861, after imprisoning, silencing, exiling, and executing many of his opponents and due to political and economic reasons, Santana asked Queen <a href="/wiki/Isabella_II_of_Spain" title="Isabella II of Spain">Isabella II of Spain</a> to retake control of the Dominican Republic, after a period of only 17 years of independence. Spain, which had not come to terms with the <a href="/wiki/Spanish_American_wars_of_independence" title="Spanish American wars of independence">loss of its American colonies</a> 40 years earlier, accepted his proposal and made the country a colony again.<sup id="cite_ref-96" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-96">&#91;95&#93;</a></sup> Haiti, fearful of the reestablishment of Spain as colonial power, gave refuge and logistics to revolutionaries seeking to reestablish the independent nation of the Dominican Republic. The ensuing civil war, known as the <i>War of Restoration</i>, claimed more than 50,000 lives.<sup id="cite_ref-97" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-97">&#91;96&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The <a href="/wiki/Dominican_Restoration_War" title="Dominican Restoration War">War of Restoration</a> began in Santiago on August 16, 1863. Spain had a difficult time fighting the Dominican <a href="/wiki/Guerrilla_warfare" title="Guerrilla warfare">guerrillas</a>. Over the course of the war, the Spanish would spend over 33 million pesos and suffer 30,000 casualties,<sup id="cite_ref-98" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-98">&#91;97&#93;</a></sup> including 10,888 killed or wounded in action. In the south, Dominican forces under <a href="/wiki/Jos%C3%A9_Mar%C3%ADa_Cabral" title="José María Cabral">José María Cabral</a> defeated the Spanish in the Battle of La Canela on December 4, 1864. The victory showed the Dominicans that they could defeat the Spaniards in <a href="/wiki/Pitched_battle" title="Pitched battle">pitched battle</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-99" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-99">&#91;98&#93;</a></sup> After two years of fighting, Spain abandoned the island in 1865.<sup id="cite_ref-100" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-100">&#91;99&#93;</a></sup> Political strife again prevailed in the following years; warlords ruled, military revolts were extremely common, and the nation amassed debt.
</p><p>After the <a href="/wiki/Ten_Years%27_War" title="Ten Years&#39; War">Ten Years' War</a> (1868–78) broke out in <a href="/wiki/Spanish_Cuba" class="mw-redirect" title="Spanish Cuba">Spanish Cuba</a>, Dominican exiles, including <a href="/wiki/M%C3%A1ximo_G%C3%B3mez" title="Máximo Gómez">Máximo Gómez</a>, <a href="/wiki/Luis_Marcano" title="Luis Marcano">Luis Marcano</a> and <a href="/wiki/Modesto_D%C3%ADaz" title="Modesto Díaz">Modesto Díaz</a>, joined the <a href="/wiki/Cuban_Revolutionary_Army" title="Cuban Revolutionary Army">Cuban Revolutionary Army</a> and provided its initial training and leadership.
</p><p>In 1869, U.S. President <a href="/wiki/Ulysses_S._Grant" title="Ulysses S. Grant">Ulysses S. Grant</a> ordered U.S. Marines to the island for the first time.<sup id="cite_ref-Greenberg_101-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Greenberg-101">&#91;100&#93;</a></sup> Pirates operating from Haiti had been raiding U.S. commercial shipping in the Caribbean, and Grant directed the Marines to stop them at their source.<sup id="cite_ref-Greenberg_101-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Greenberg-101">&#91;100&#93;</a></sup> Following the virtual takeover of the island, Báez offered to <a href="/wiki/Annexation_of_Santo_Domingo" title="Annexation of Santo Domingo">sell the country to the United States</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Greenberg_101-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Greenberg-101">&#91;100&#93;</a></sup> Grant desired a naval base at <a href="/wiki/Saman%C3%A1_Bay" title="Samaná Bay">Samaná</a> and also a place for resettling newly freed <a href="/wiki/African_Americans" title="African Americans">African Americans</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-102" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-102">&#91;101&#93;</a></sup> The treaty, which included U.S. payment of $1.5 million for Dominican debt repayment, was defeated in the <a href="/wiki/United_States_Senate" title="United States Senate">United States Senate</a> in 1870<sup id="cite_ref-guitar_81-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-guitar-81">&#91;81&#93;</a></sup> on a vote of 28–28, two-thirds being required.<sup id="cite_ref-103" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-103">&#91;102&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-104" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-104">&#91;103&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-105" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-105">&#91;104&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Báez was <a href="/wiki/Six_Years%27_War" title="Six Years&#39; War">toppled in 1874</a>, returned, and was toppled for good in 1878. A new generation was thence in charge, with the passing of Santana (he died in 1864) and Báez from the scene. Relative peace came to the country in the 1880s, which saw the coming to power of General <a href="/wiki/Ulises_Heureaux" title="Ulises Heureaux">Ulises Heureaux</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-countrystudies_106-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-countrystudies-106">&#91;105&#93;</a></sup> "Lilís", as the new president was nicknamed, enjoyed a period of popularity. He was, however, "a consummate dissembler", who put the nation deep into debt while using much of the proceeds for his personal use and to maintain his police state.
