Heureaux became rampantly despotic and unpopular.<sup id="cite_ref-countrystudies_106-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-countrystudies-106">&#91;105&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-107" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-107">&#91;106&#93;</a></sup> In 1899, he was assassinated. However, the relative calm over which he presided allowed improvement in the Dominican economy. The sugar industry was modernized,<sup id="cite_ref-Hall_108-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Hall-108">&#91;107&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: p10">&#58;&#8202;p10&#8202;</span></sup> and the country attracted foreign workers and immigrants.
</p><p>Lebanese, Syrians, Turks, and Palestinians began to arrive in the country during the latter part of the 19th century.<sup id="cite_ref-Brown_20-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Brown-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> At first, the <a href="/wiki/Arabs" title="Arabs">Arab</a> immigrants often faced discrimination in the Dominican Republic, but they were eventually assimilated into Dominican society, giving up their own culture and language.<sup id="cite_ref-Brown_20-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Brown-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> During the U.S. occupation of 1916–24, peasants from the countryside, called Gavilleros, would not only kill U.S. Marines, but would also attack and kill Arab vendors traveling through the countryside.<sup id="cite_ref-109" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-109">&#91;108&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span id="20th_century_.281900.E2.80.9330.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="20th_century_(1900–30)">20th century (1900–30)</span></h3>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Alejandro_Woss_y_Gil.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e3/Alejandro_Woss_y_Gil.jpg/220px-Alejandro_Woss_y_Gil.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="160" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e3/Alejandro_Woss_y_Gil.jpg/330px-Alejandro_Woss_y_Gil.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e3/Alejandro_Woss_y_Gil.jpg/440px-Alejandro_Woss_y_Gil.jpg 2x" data-file-width="550" data-file-height="400" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Alejandro_Woss_y_Gil.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>President <a href="/wiki/Alejandro_Woss_y_Gil" title="Alejandro Woss y Gil">Alejandro Woss y Gil</a> taking office in 1903</div></div></div>
<p>From 1902 on, short-lived governments were again the norm, with their power usurped by <a href="/wiki/Caudillo" title="Caudillo">caudillos</a> in parts of the country. Furthermore, the national government was bankrupt and, unable to pay its debts to European creditors, faced the threat of military intervention by France, <a href="/wiki/German_Empire" title="German Empire">Germany</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Kingdom_of_Italy" title="Kingdom of Italy">Italy</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-cs9_110-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-cs9-110">&#91;109&#93;</a></sup> United States President <a href="/wiki/Theodore_Roosevelt" title="Theodore Roosevelt">Theodore Roosevelt</a> sought to prevent European intervention, largely to protect the routes to the future <a href="/wiki/Panama_Canal" title="Panama Canal">Panama Canal</a>, as the canal was already under construction. He made a small military intervention to ward off European powers, to proclaim his famous <a href="/wiki/Roosevelt_Corollary" title="Roosevelt Corollary">Roosevelt Corollary</a> to the <a href="/wiki/Monroe_Doctrine" title="Monroe Doctrine">Monroe Doctrine</a>, and also to obtain his 1905 Dominican agreement for U.S. administration of Dominican customs, which was the chief source of income for the Dominican government. A 1906 agreement provided for the arrangement to last 50 years. The United States agreed to use part of the customs proceeds to reduce the immense foreign debt of the Dominican Republic and assumed responsibility for said debt.<sup id="cite_ref-Encarta_50-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Encarta-50">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-cs9_110-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-cs9-110">&#91;109&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:152px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Ramon_Caceres_1907_(cropped).jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e7/Ramon_Caceres_1907_%28cropped%29.jpg/150px-Ramon_Caceres_1907_%28cropped%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="150" height="195" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e7/Ramon_Caceres_1907_%28cropped%29.jpg/225px-Ramon_Caceres_1907_%28cropped%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e7/Ramon_Caceres_1907_%28cropped%29.jpg/300px-Ramon_Caceres_1907_%28cropped%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1865" data-file-height="2424" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Ramon_Caceres_1907_(cropped).jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div><a href="/wiki/Ram%C3%B3n_C%C3%A1ceres" title="Ramón Cáceres">Ramón Cáceres</a></div></div></div>
<p>After six years in power, President <a href="/wiki/Ram%C3%B3n_C%C3%A1ceres" title="Ramón Cáceres">Ramón Cáceres</a> (who had himself assassinated Heureaux)<sup id="cite_ref-countrystudies_106-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-countrystudies-106">&#91;105&#93;</a></sup> was assassinated in 1911. The result was several years of great political instability and <a href="/wiki/Dominican_Civil_War_(1911%E2%80%931912)" title="Dominican Civil War (1911–1912)">civil war</a>. U.S. mediation by the <a href="/wiki/William_Howard_Taft" title="William Howard Taft">William Howard Taft</a> and <a href="/wiki/Woodrow_Wilson" title="Woodrow Wilson">Woodrow Wilson</a> administrations achieved only a short respite each time. A political deadlock in 1914 was broken after an ultimatum by Wilson telling the Dominicans to choose a president or see the U.S. impose one. A provisional president was chosen, and later the same year relatively free elections put former president (1899–1902) <a href="/wiki/Juan_Isidro_Jimenes_Pereyra" class="mw-redirect" title="Juan Isidro Jimenes Pereyra">Juan Isidro Jimenes Pereyra</a> back in power. To achieve a more broadly supported government, Jimenes named opposition individuals to his cabinet. But this brought no peace and, with his former <a href="/wiki/Secretary_of_War" class="mw-redirect" title="Secretary of War">Secretary of War</a> <a href="/wiki/Desiderio_Arias" title="Desiderio Arias">Desiderio Arias</a> maneuvering to depose him and despite a U.S. offer of military aid against Arias, Jimenes resigned on May 7, 1916.<sup id="cite_ref-congress_111-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-congress-111">&#91;110&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Ocupacion-1916.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f5/Ocupacion-1916.jpg/220px-Ocupacion-1916.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="136" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f5/Ocupacion-1916.jpg/330px-Ocupacion-1916.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f5/Ocupacion-1916.jpg/440px-Ocupacion-1916.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1014" data-file-height="628" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Ocupacion-1916.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>The <a href="/wiki/United_States_Marine_Corps" title="United States Marine Corps">United States Marine Corps</a> landing on Dominican soil in 1916</div></div></div>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Marines.mil.png" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e2/Marines.mil.png/220px-Marines.mil.png" decoding="async" width="220" height="138" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e2/Marines.mil.png/330px-Marines.mil.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e2/Marines.mil.png/440px-Marines.mil.png 2x" data-file-width="572" data-file-height="360" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Marines.mil.png" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Marines of the <a href="/wiki/4th_Marine_Regiment" title="4th Marine Regiment">4th regiment</a> with a captured rebel <a href="/wiki/Mitrailleuse" title="Mitrailleuse">mitrailleuse</a> at Santiago</div></div></div>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:USMC_Fortaleza_Ozama_from_river_1922_restored.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2b/USMC_Fortaleza_Ozama_from_river_1922_restored.jpg/220px-USMC_Fortaleza_Ozama_from_river_1922_restored.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="147" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2b/USMC_Fortaleza_Ozama_from_river_1922_restored.jpg/330px-USMC_Fortaleza_Ozama_from_river_1922_restored.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2b/USMC_Fortaleza_Ozama_from_river_1922_restored.jpg/440px-USMC_Fortaleza_Ozama_from_river_1922_restored.jpg 2x" data-file-width="900" data-file-height="600" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:USMC_Fortaleza_Ozama_from_river_1922_restored.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>The flag of the <a href="/wiki/United_States" title="United States">United States</a> waving over <a href="/wiki/Fortaleza_Ozama" title="Fortaleza Ozama">Ozama Fortress</a> during the <a href="/wiki/United_States_occupation_of_the_Dominican_Republic_(1916%E2%80%9324)" class="mw-redirect" title="United States occupation of the Dominican Republic (1916–24)">U.S. occupation</a> of the Dominican Republic, c. 1922</div></div></div>
<p>Wilson thus ordered the U.S. occupation of the Dominican Republic. <a href="/wiki/United_States_Marine_Corps" title="United States Marine Corps">U.S. Marines</a> landed on May 16, 1916, and had control of the country two months later. The military government established by the U.S., led by Vice Admiral <a href="/wiki/Harry_Shepard_Knapp" title="Harry Shepard Knapp">Harry Shepard Knapp</a>, was widely repudiated by the Dominicans, with caudillos in the mountainous eastern regions leading guerrilla campaigns against U.S. forces.<sup id="cite_ref-congress_111-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-congress-111">&#91;110&#93;</a></sup> Arias's forces, who had no machine guns or modern artillery, tried to take on the U.S. Marines in conventional battles, but were defeated at the <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Las_Trencheras" title="Battle of Las Trencheras">Battle of Las Trencheras</a> (the trenches), <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Guayacanas" title="Battle of Guayacanas">Battle of Guayacanas</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_San_Francisco_de_Macoris" title="Battle of San Francisco de Macoris">Battle of San Francisco de Macoris</a>.
</p><p>The occupation regime kept most Dominican laws and institutions and largely pacified the general population. The occupying government also revived the Dominican economy, reduced the nation's debt, built a road network that at last interconnected all regions of the country, and created a professional National Guard to replace the warring partisan units.<sup id="cite_ref-congress_111-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-congress-111">&#91;110&#93;</a></sup> Opposition to the occupation continued, nevertheless, and after World War I it increased in the U.S. as well. There, President <a href="/wiki/Warren_G._Harding" title="Warren G. Harding">Warren G. Harding</a> (1921–23), Wilson's successor, worked to put an end to the occupation, as he had promised to do during his campaign. The U.S. government's rule ended in October 1922, and elections were held in March 1924.<sup id="cite_ref-congress_111-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-congress-111">&#91;110&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Pres.-elect_Horacio_Vasquez_of_Santo_Domingo,_lunches_with_Pres._Coolidge._Lft._to_rt.-_Col._C.O._Sherrill,_Military_Aid_to_the_Pres.;_Pres.-_elect_Vasquez_and_J._Butler_Wright,_Third_Ass%27t_LCCN2016893577.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/98/Pres.-elect_Horacio_Vasquez_of_Santo_Domingo%2C_lunches_with_Pres._Coolidge._Lft._to_rt.-_Col._C.O._Sherrill%2C_Military_Aid_to_the_Pres.%3B_Pres.-_elect_Vasquez_and_J._Butler_Wright%2C_Third_Ass%27t_LCCN2016893577.jpg/220px-thumbnail.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="278" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/98/Pres.-elect_Horacio_Vasquez_of_Santo_Domingo%2C_lunches_with_Pres._Coolidge._Lft._to_rt.-_Col._C.O._Sherrill%2C_Military_Aid_to_the_Pres.%3B_Pres.-_elect_Vasquez_and_J._Butler_Wright%2C_Third_Ass%27t_LCCN2016893577.jpg/330px-thumbnail.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/98/Pres.-elect_Horacio_Vasquez_of_Santo_Domingo%2C_lunches_with_Pres._Coolidge._Lft._to_rt.-_Col._C.O._Sherrill%2C_Military_Aid_to_the_Pres.%3B_Pres.-_elect_Vasquez_and_J._Butler_Wright%2C_Third_Ass%27t_LCCN2016893577.jpg/440px-thumbnail.jpg 2x" data-file-width="6853" data-file-height="8658" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Pres.-elect_Horacio_Vasquez_of_Santo_Domingo,_lunches_with_Pres._Coolidge._Lft._to_rt.-_Col._C.O._Sherrill,_Military_Aid_to_the_Pres.;_Pres.-_elect_Vasquez_and_J._Butler_Wright,_Third_Ass%27t_LCCN2016893577.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Dominican Republic president elect <a href="/wiki/Horacio_Vasquez" class="mw-redirect" title="Horacio Vasquez">Horacio Vasquez</a> meeting with United States officials</div></div></div>
<p>The victor was former president (1902–03) <a href="/wiki/Horacio_V%C3%A1squez" title="Horacio Vásquez">Horacio Vásquez</a>, who had cooperated with the U.S. He was inaugurated on July 13, 1924, and the last U.S. forces left in September. In six years, the Marines were involved in at least 370 engagements, with 950 "bandits" killed or wounded in action to the Marines' 144 killed.<sup id="cite_ref-112" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-112">&#91;111&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-113" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-113">&#91;112&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-govinfo_114-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-govinfo-114">&#91;113&#93;</a></sup> Vásquez gave the country six years of stable governance, in which political and civil rights were respected and the economy grew strongly, in a relatively peaceful atmosphere.<sup id="cite_ref-congress_111-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-congress-111">&#91;110&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-locdr11_115-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-locdr11-115">&#91;114&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>During the government of Horacio Vásquez, <a href="/wiki/Rafael_Trujillo" title="Rafael Trujillo">Rafael Trujillo</a> held the rank of lieutenant colonel and was chief of police. This position helped him launch his plans to overthrow the government of Vásquez. Trujillo had the support of Carlos Rosario Peña, who formed the Civic Movement, which had as its main objective to overthrow the government of Vásquez.
</p><p>In February 1930, when Vásquez attempted to win another term, his opponents rebelled in secret alliance with the commander of the National Army (the former National Guard), General Rafael Trujillo. Trujillo secretly cut a deal with rebel leader <a href="/wiki/Rafael_Estrella_Ure%C3%B1a" title="Rafael Estrella Ureña">Rafael Estrella Ureña</a>; in return for letting Ureña take power, Trujillo would be allowed to run for president in new elections. As the rebels marched toward Santo Domingo, Vásquez ordered Trujillo to suppress them. However, feigning "neutrality", Trujillo kept his men in barracks, allowing Ureña's rebels to take the capital virtually uncontested. On March 3, Ureña was proclaimed acting president with Trujillo confirmed as head of the police and the army. As per their agreement, Trujillo became the presidential nominee of the newly formed Patriotic Coalition of Citizens (Spanish: Coalición patriotica de los ciudadanos), with Ureña as his running mate.
</p><p>During the election campaign, Trujillo used the army to unleash his repression, forcing his opponents to withdraw from the race. Trujillo stood to elect himself, and in May he was elected president virtually unopposed after a violent campaign against his opponents, ascending to power on August 16, 1930. Desiderio Arias led a failed revolt against Trujillo and was killed near <a href="/wiki/Mao,_Dominican_Republic" title="Mao, Dominican Republic">Mao</a> on June 20, 1931.
</p>
<h3><span id="Trujillo_Era_.281930.E2.80.9361.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Trujillo_Era_(1930–61)">Trujillo Era (1930–61)</span></h3>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:172px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Rafael_L._Trujillo_en_la_silla_presidencial_(cropped).jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/94/Rafael_L._Trujillo_en_la_silla_presidencial_%28cropped%29.jpg/170px-Rafael_L._Trujillo_en_la_silla_presidencial_%28cropped%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="242" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/94/Rafael_L._Trujillo_en_la_silla_presidencial_%28cropped%29.jpg/255px-Rafael_L._Trujillo_en_la_silla_presidencial_%28cropped%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/94/Rafael_L._Trujillo_en_la_silla_presidencial_%28cropped%29.jpg/340px-Rafael_L._Trujillo_en_la_silla_presidencial_%28cropped%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1807" data-file-height="2569" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Rafael_L._Trujillo_en_la_silla_presidencial_(cropped).jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div><a href="/wiki/Rafael_Trujillo" title="Rafael Trujillo">Rafael Trujillo</a> imposed a dictatorship of 31 years in the country (1930–1961).</div></div></div>
<p>There was considerable economic growth during <a href="/wiki/Rafael_Trujillo" title="Rafael Trujillo">Rafael Trujillo</a>'s long and iron-fisted regime, although a great deal of the wealth was taken by the dictator and other regime elements. There was progress in healthcare, education, and transportation, with the building of hospitals, clinics, schools, roads, and harbors. Trujillo also carried out an important housing construction program, and instituted a pension plan. He finally negotiated an undisputed border with Haiti in 1935, and achieved the end of the 50-year customs agreement in 1941, instead of 1956. He made the country debt-free in 1947.<sup id="cite_ref-Encarta_50-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Encarta-50">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Galvan_116-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Galvan-116">&#91;115&#93;</a></sup> This was accompanied by absolute repression and the copious use of murder, torture, and terrorist methods against the opposition. It has been estimated that Trujillo's tyrannical rule was responsible for the death of more than 50,000 Dominicans.
</p><p>Trujillo's henchmen did not hesitate to use intimidation, torture, or assassination of political foes both at home and abroad.<sup id="cite_ref-Greenberg_101-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Greenberg-101">&#91;100&#93;</a></sup> Trujillo was responsible for the deaths of the Spaniards José Almoina in <a href="/wiki/Mexico_City" title="Mexico City">Mexico City</a> and <a href="/wiki/Jes%C3%BAs_Gal%C3%ADndez" title="Jesús Galíndez">Jesús Galíndez</a> in <a href="/wiki/New_York_City" title="New York City">New York City</a>.
</p>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Wea02216.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/ce/Wea02216.jpg/220px-Wea02216.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="172" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/ce/Wea02216.jpg/330px-Wea02216.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/ce/Wea02216.jpg/440px-Wea02216.jpg 2x" data-file-width="700" data-file-height="548" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Wea02216.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Destruction of Santo Domingo after the 1930 hurricane</div></div></div>
<p>In 1930, <a href="/wiki/1930_Dominican_Republic_hurricane" class="mw-redirect" title="1930 Dominican Republic hurricane">Hurricane San Zenon</a> destroyed Santo Domingo and killed 8,000 people. During the rebuilding process, Trujillo renamed Santo Domingo to "Ciudad Trujillo" (Trujillo City),<sup id="cite_ref-Encarta_50-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Encarta-50">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup> and the nation's&#160;– and the Caribbean's&#160;– highest mountain <i><a href="/wiki/Pico_Duarte" title="Pico Duarte">La Pelona Grande</a></i> (Spanish for: The Great Bald) to "Pico Trujillo" (Spanish for: Trujillo Peak). By the end of his first term in 1934 he was the country's wealthiest person,<sup id="cite_ref-pons_84-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-pons-84">&#91;83&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: p360">&#58;&#8202;p360&#8202;</span></sup> and one of the wealthiest in the world by the early 1950s;<sup id="cite_ref-117" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-117">&#91;116&#93;</a></sup> near the end of his regime his fortune was an estimated $800 million ($5.3 billion today).<sup id="cite_ref-Hall_108-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Hall-108">&#91;107&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: p111">&#58;&#8202;p111&#8202;</span></sup>
</p><p>Trujillo, who neglected the fact that his maternal great-grandmother was from Haiti's mulatto class, actively promoted propaganda against Haitian people.<sup id="cite_ref-killer_118-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-killer-118">&#91;117&#93;</a></sup> In 1937, he ordered what became known as the <a href="/wiki/Parsley_massacre" title="Parsley massacre">Parsley Massacre</a> or, in the Dominican Republic, as <i>El Corte</i> (The Cutting),<sup id="cite_ref-Wucker_119-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Wucker-119">&#91;118&#93;</a></sup> directing the army to kill Haitians living on the Dominican side of the border. The army killed an estimated 17,000 to 35,000 Haitian men, women, and children over six days, from the night of October 2, 1937, through October 8, 1937. To avoid leaving evidence of the army's involvement, the soldiers used <a href="/wiki/Edged_and_bladed_weapons" title="Edged and bladed weapons">edged weapons</a> rather than guns.<sup id="cite_ref-guitar_81-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-guitar-81">&#91;81&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-killer_118-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-killer-118">&#91;117&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-120" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-120">&#91;119&#93;</a></sup> The soldiers were said to have interrogated anyone with dark skin, using the <a href="/wiki/Shibboleth" title="Shibboleth">shibboleth</a> <i>perejil</i> (<a href="/wiki/Parsley" title="Parsley">parsley</a>) to distinguish Haitians from Afro-Dominicans when necessary; the 'r' of <i>perejil</i> was of difficult pronunciation for Haitians.<sup id="cite_ref-Wucker_119-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Wucker-119">&#91;118&#93;</a></sup> As a result of the massacre, the Dominican Republic agreed to pay Haiti US$750,000, later reduced to US$525,000.<sup id="cite_ref-Sagas_121-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Sagas-121">&#91;120&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-locdr11_115-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-locdr11-115">&#91;114&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>During <a href="/wiki/World_War_II" title="World War II">World War II</a>, Trujillo symbolically sided with the <a href="/wiki/Allies_of_World_War_II" title="Allies of World War II">Allies</a> and declared war on Japan the day after the <a href="/wiki/Attack_on_Pearl_Harbor" title="Attack on Pearl Harbor">attack on Pearl Harbor</a> and on Nazi Germany and Italy four days later. Soon after, German U-boats torpedoed and sank two Dominican merchant vessels that Trujillo had named after himself—the <i>San Rafael</i> off Jamaica and the <i><a href="/wiki/SS_Presidente_Trujillo" title="SS Presidente Trujillo">Presidente Trujillo</a></i> off <a href="/wiki/Fort-de-France" title="Fort-de-France">Fort-de-France</a>, <a href="/wiki/Martinique" title="Martinique">Martinique</a>. German U-boats also sank four Dominican-manned ships in the Caribbean. The country did not make a military contribution to the war, but Dominican sugar and other agricultural products supported the Allied war effort. American <a href="/wiki/Lend-Lease" title="Lend-Lease">Lend-Lease</a> and raw material purchases proved a powerful inducement in obtaining cooperation of the various Latin American republics. Over a hundred Dominicans served in the American armed forces. Many were political <a href="/wiki/Exile" title="Exile">exiles</a> from the Trujillo regime.
</p><p>Ramfis (the dictator's son) and <a href="/wiki/Porfirio_Rubirosa" title="Porfirio Rubirosa">Porfirio Rubirosa</a> became a major part of the Rafael Trujillo regime's image in the foreign press, as a result of their <a href="/wiki/Jet_setting" class="mw-redirect" title="Jet setting">jet setting</a> lifestyle and relationships with Hollywood actresses.
</p><p>Trujillo's dictatorship was marred by botched invasions, international scandals and assassination attempts. 1947 brought the failure of a planned invasion by leftist Dominican exiles from the Cuban island of Cayo Confites. July 1949 was the year of a failed invasion by Dominican rebels from <a href="/wiki/Guatemala" title="Guatemala">Guatemala</a>, and on June 14, 1959, there was a <a href="/wiki/Foreign_interventions_by_Cuba#1959_Dominican_Republic_invasion_attempt" title="Foreign interventions by Cuba">failed Cuban invasion</a> at <a href="/wiki/Constanza,_Dominican_Republic" title="Constanza, Dominican Republic">Constanza</a>, Maimón and Estero Hondo.<sup id="cite_ref-122" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-122">&#91;121&#93;</a></sup> On June 26, 1959, Cuba broke diplomatic relations with the Dominican Republic due to widespread Dominican <a href="/wiki/Human_rights" title="Human rights">human rights</a> abuses and hostility toward the <a href="/wiki/Cuban_people" class="mw-redirect" title="Cuban people">Cuban people</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-123" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-123">&#91;122&#93;</a></sup> Cuban leader <a href="/wiki/Fidel_Castro" title="Fidel Castro">Fidel Castro</a> feared a possible attack from the Dominican Republic and was determined to acquire jet aircraft as a preventive measure. Cuba's ability to repel an air attack was very precarious, since the Dominicans possessed 40 jet aircraft whereas Cuba had only one.<sup id="cite_ref-124" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-124">&#91;123&#93;</a></sup> The Dominican Air Force had the theoretical ability to reach and bomb <a href="/wiki/Havana" title="Havana">Havana</a> within 3 hours.
</p><p>On November 25, 1960, Trujillo's henchmen killed three of the four <a href="/wiki/Mirabal_sisters" title="Mirabal sisters">Mirabal sisters</a>, nicknamed <i>Las Mariposas</i> (The Butterflies). The victims were Patria Mercedes Mirabal (born on February 27, 1924), Argentina Minerva Mirabal (born on March 12, 1926), and Antonia María Teresa Mirabal (born on October 15, 1935). Along with their husbands, the sisters were conspiring to overthrow Trujillo in a violent revolt. The Mirabals had communist ideological leanings, as did their husbands. The sisters have received many honors posthumously and have many memorials in various cities in the Dominican Republic. Salcedo, their home province, changed its name to <a href="/wiki/Hermanas_Mirabal_Province" title="Hermanas Mirabal Province">Provincia Hermanas Mirabal</a> (Mirabal Sisters Province). The <a href="/wiki/International_Day_for_the_Elimination_of_Violence_against_Women" title="International Day for the Elimination of Violence against Women">International Day for the Elimination of Violence against Women</a> is observed on the anniversary of their deaths.
</p>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:1960._Junio,_24._Atentado_a_R%C3%B3mulo_Betancourt_en_Los_Pr%C3%B3ceres.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d9/1960._Junio%2C_24._Atentado_a_R%C3%B3mulo_Betancourt_en_Los_Pr%C3%B3ceres.jpg/220px-1960._Junio%2C_24._Atentado_a_R%C3%B3mulo_Betancourt_en_Los_Pr%C3%B3ceres.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="175" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d9/1960._Junio%2C_24._Atentado_a_R%C3%B3mulo_Betancourt_en_Los_Pr%C3%B3ceres.jpg/330px-1960._Junio%2C_24._Atentado_a_R%C3%B3mulo_Betancourt_en_Los_Pr%C3%B3ceres.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d9/1960._Junio%2C_24._Atentado_a_R%C3%B3mulo_Betancourt_en_Los_Pr%C3%B3ceres.jpg/440px-1960._Junio%2C_24._Atentado_a_R%C3%B3mulo_Betancourt_en_Los_Pr%C3%B3ceres.jpg 2x" data-file-width="988" data-file-height="788" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:1960._Junio,_24._Atentado_a_R%C3%B3mulo_Betancourt_en_Los_Pr%C3%B3ceres.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Explosion in Paseo Los Próceres during the Betancourt assassination attempt, June 24, 1960</div></div></div>
<p>For a long time, the U.S. and the Dominican elite supported the Trujillo government. This support persisted despite the assassinations of political opposition, the massacre of Haitians, and Trujillo's plots against other countries. The U.S. believed Trujillo was the lesser of two or more evils.<sup id="cite_ref-Wucker_119-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Wucker-119">&#91;118&#93;</a></sup> The U.S. finally broke with Trujillo in 1960, after Trujillo's agents attempted to assassinate the Venezuelan president, <a href="/wiki/R%C3%B3mulo_Betancourt" title="Rómulo Betancourt">Rómulo Betancourt</a>, a fierce critic of Trujillo.<sup id="cite_ref-locdr11_115-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-locdr11-115">&#91;114&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-125" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-125">&#91;124&#93;</a></sup> Dominican agents placed a bomb in the Venezuelan president's car in <a href="/wiki/Caracas" title="Caracas">Caracas</a>, which exploded, injuring Betancourt and killing a number of his advisers.
</p><p>In June 1960, Trujillo legalized the Communist Party and attempted to establish close political relations with the Soviet Bloc. Both the assassination attempt and the maneuver toward the Soviet Bloc provoked immediate condemnation throughout Latin America. Once its representatives confirmed Trujillo's complicity in the assassination attempt, the <a href="/wiki/Organization_of_American_States" title="Organization of American States">Organization of American States</a>, for the first time in its history, decreed sanctions against a member state. The United States severed diplomatic relations with the Dominican Republic on August 26, 1960, and in January 1961 suspended the export of trucks, parts, crude oil, gasoline and other petroleum products. U.S. President <a href="/wiki/Dwight_D._Eisenhower" title="Dwight D. Eisenhower">Dwight D. Eisenhower</a> also took advantage of OAS sanctions to cut drastically purchases of Dominican sugar, the country's major export. This action ultimately cost the Dominican Republic almost $22,000,000 in lost revenues at a time when its economy was in a rapid decline. Trujillo had become expendable.<sup id="cite_ref-Cord_Meyer_126-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Cord_Meyer-126">&#91;125&#93;</a></sup> Dissidents inside the Dominican Republic argued that assassination was the only certain way to remove Trujillo.<sup id="cite_ref-Cord_Meyer_126-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Cord_Meyer-126">&#91;125&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-CIA_127-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CIA-127">&#91;126&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>According to <a href="/wiki/Chester_Bowles" class="mw-redirect" title="Chester Bowles">Chester Bowles</a>, the U.S. Undersecretary of State, internal <a href="/wiki/Department_of_State" class="mw-redirect" title="Department of State">Department of State</a> discussions in 1961 on the topic were vigorous.<sup id="cite_ref-StateDR_128-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-StateDR-128">&#91;127&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Richard_N._Goodwin" title="Richard N. Goodwin">Richard N. Goodwin</a>, Assistant Special Counsel to the President, who had direct contacts with the rebel alliance, argued for intervention against Trujillo.<sup id="cite_ref-StateDR_128-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-StateDR-128">&#91;127&#93;</a></sup> Quoting Bowles directly: "The next morning I learned that in spite of the clear decision against having the dissident group request our assistance Dick Goodwin following the meeting sent a cable to CIA people in the Dominican Republic without checking with State or CIA; indeed, with the protest of the Department of State. The cable directed the CIA people in the Dominican Republic to get this request at any cost. When <a href="/wiki/Allen_Dulles" title="Allen Dulles">Allen Dulles</a> found this out the next morning, he withdrew the order. We later discovered it had already been carried out."<sup id="cite_ref-StateDR_128-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-StateDR-128">&#91;127&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span id="Post-Trujillo_.281961.E2.80.931996.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Post-Trujillo_(1961–1996)">Post-Trujillo (1961–1996)</span></h3>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:152px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Juan_Bosch_(1963).jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/cf/Juan_Bosch_%281963%29.jpg/150px-Juan_Bosch_%281963%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="150" height="200" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/cf/Juan_Bosch_%281963%29.jpg/225px-Juan_Bosch_%281963%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/cf/Juan_Bosch_%281963%29.jpg/300px-Juan_Bosch_%281963%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="791" data-file-height="1055" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Juan_Bosch_(1963).jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div><a href="/wiki/Juan_Bosch_(politician)" title="Juan Bosch (politician)">Juan Bosch</a>, the first democratically elected president after the regime of Rafael Trujillo</div></div></div>
<p>Trujillo was assassinated by Dominican dissidents on May 30, 1961.<sup id="cite_ref-locdr11_115-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-locdr11-115">&#91;114&#93;</a></sup> Although the dissidents possessed Dominican-made <a href="/wiki/Crist%C3%B3bal_Carbine" title="Cristóbal Carbine">San Cristóbal</a> submachine guns, they symbolically used U.S.-made <a href="/wiki/M1_Garand" title="M1 Garand">M-1</a> carbines supplied by the United States <a href="/wiki/Central_Intelligence_Agency" title="Central Intelligence Agency">Central Intelligence Agency</a> (CIA).<sup id="cite_ref-WHN_129-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-WHN-129">&#91;128&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Ramfis_Trujillo" title="Ramfis Trujillo">Ramfis Trujillo</a>, the dictator's son, remained in de facto control of the government for the next six months through his position as commander of the armed forces. Trujillo's brothers, Hector Bienvenido and Jose Arismendi Trujillo, returned to the country and began immediately to plot against President Balaguer. On November 18, 1961, as a planned coup became more evident, U.S. Secretary of State <a href="/wiki/Dean_Rusk" title="Dean Rusk">Dean Rusk</a> issued a warning that the United States would not "remain idle" if the Trujillos attempted to "reassert dictatorial domination" over the Dominican Republic. Following this warning, and the arrival of a fourteen-vessel U.S. naval task force within sight of Santo Domingo, Ramfis and his uncles fled the country on November 19 with $200 million from the Dominican treasury. The OAS lifted its sanctions on January 4, 1962, since the Dominican Republic no longer posed a threat to regional security.
</p><p>On December 28, 1962, the Dominican military suppressed a rebellion in Palma Sola, burning six hundred people to death by a <a href="/wiki/Napalm" title="Napalm">napalm</a> airstrike.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (July 2021)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:152px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Francisco_Caama%C3%B1o.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/01/Francisco_Caama%C3%B1o.jpg/150px-Francisco_Caama%C3%B1o.jpg" decoding="async" width="150" height="174" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/01/Francisco_Caama%C3%B1o.jpg/225px-Francisco_Caama%C3%B1o.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/01/Francisco_Caama%C3%B1o.jpg/300px-Francisco_Caama%C3%B1o.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2174" data-file-height="2525" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Francisco_Caama%C3%B1o.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div><a href="/wiki/Francisco_Caamano" class="mw-redirect" title="Francisco Caamano">Francisco Caamano</a>, rebel leader in the Dominican civil war.</div></div></div>
<p>In February 1963, a democratically elected government under leftist <a href="/wiki/Juan_Bosch_(politician)" title="Juan Bosch (politician)">Juan Bosch</a> took office but it was overthrown in September. On April 24, 1965, after 19 months of military rule, a pro-Bosch revolt broke out in Santo Domingo.<sup id="cite_ref-130" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-130">&#91;129&#93;</a></sup> The pro-Bosch forces called themselves Constitutionalists. The revolution took on the dimensions of a civil war when conservative military forces struck back against the Constitutionalists on April 25. These conservative forces called themselves Loyalists. Despite tank assaults and bombing runs by Loyalist forces, the Constitutionalists held their positions in the capital. By April 26, armed civilians outnumbered the original rebel military regulars. Radio Santo Domingo, now fully under rebel control, began to call for more violent actions and for killing of all the policemen.<sup id="cite_ref-Greenberg_101-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Greenberg-101">&#91;100&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:DR1965-2_(8161964547).jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7b/DR1965-2_%288161964547%29.jpg/220px-DR1965-2_%288161964547%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="181" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7b/DR1965-2_%288161964547%29.jpg/330px-DR1965-2_%288161964547%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7b/DR1965-2_%288161964547%29.jpg/440px-DR1965-2_%288161964547%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2800" data-file-height="2304" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:DR1965-2_(8161964547).jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>A Marine heavy machine gunner monitors activity from a street <a href="/wiki/Barricade" title="Barricade">barricade</a> in Santo Domingo.</div></div></div>
<p>On April 28, U.S. President <a href="/wiki/Lyndon_Johnson" class="mw-redirect" title="Lyndon Johnson">Lyndon Johnson</a>, concerned that communists might take over the revolt and create a "<a href="/wiki/Cuban_Revolution" title="Cuban Revolution">second Cuba</a>", sent 24,000 troops into Santo Domingo in <a href="/wiki/Operation_Powerpack" class="mw-redirect" title="Operation Powerpack">Operation Powerpack</a>. "We don't propose to sit here in a rocking chair with our hands folded and let the Communist set up any government in the Western Hemisphere," Johnson said.<sup id="cite_ref-131" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-131">&#91;130&#93;</a></sup> The forces were soon joined by comparatively small contingents from the <a href="/wiki/Organization_of_American_States" title="Organization of American States">Organization of American States</a> (OAS).<sup id="cite_ref-civil_war_132-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-civil_war-132">&#91;131&#93;</a></sup> The <a href="/wiki/4th_Marine_Expeditionary_Brigade" title="4th Marine Expeditionary Brigade">4th Marine Expeditionary Brigade</a> and the army's <a href="/wiki/82nd_Airborne_Division" title="82nd Airborne Division">82nd Airborne Division</a> spearheaded the occupation. <a href="/wiki/Psychological_Warfare" class="mw-redirect" title="Psychological Warfare">Psychological Warfare</a> and <a href="/wiki/United_States_Army_Special_Forces" title="United States Army Special Forces">Green Beret</a> units also took part in the action. The Loyalists used the U.S. presence to deploy its forces and attack Constitutionalists. As a result, Loyalist forces destroyed most Constitutionalist bases and captured the rebel radio station, effectively ending the war. A cease-fire was declared on May 21.<sup id="cite_ref-Pedraja_133-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Pedraja-133">&#91;132&#93;</a></sup> The U.S. began withdrawing some of its troops by May 26. However, Col. <a href="/wiki/Francisco_Caama%C3%B1o" title="Francisco Caamaño">Francisco Caamaño</a>'s untrained civilians attacked American positions on June 15. Despite the coordinated attack involving mortars, rocket launchers, and several light tanks, the rebels lost a 56-square-block area to 82nd Airborne Division units which had received OAS permission to advance.<sup id="cite_ref-Greenberg_101-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Greenberg-101">&#91;100&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:152px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Toma_de_posesi%C3%B3n_de_D._Joaqu%C3%ADn_Balaguer_en_1966_(1)_(cropped).jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a9/Toma_de_posesi%C3%B3n_de_D._Joaqu%C3%ADn_Balaguer_en_1966_%281%29_%28cropped%29.jpg/150px-Toma_de_posesi%C3%B3n_de_D._Joaqu%C3%ADn_Balaguer_en_1966_%281%29_%28cropped%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="150" height="201" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a9/Toma_de_posesi%C3%B3n_de_D._Joaqu%C3%ADn_Balaguer_en_1966_%281%29_%28cropped%29.jpg/225px-Toma_de_posesi%C3%B3n_de_D._Joaqu%C3%ADn_Balaguer_en_1966_%281%29_%28cropped%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a9/Toma_de_posesi%C3%B3n_de_D._Joaqu%C3%ADn_Balaguer_en_1966_%281%29_%28cropped%29.jpg/300px-Toma_de_posesi%C3%B3n_de_D._Joaqu%C3%ADn_Balaguer_en_1966_%281%29_%28cropped%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="629" data-file-height="842" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Toma_de_posesi%C3%B3n_de_D._Joaqu%C3%ADn_Balaguer_en_1966_(1)_(cropped).jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div><a href="/wiki/Joaqu%C3%ADn_Balaguer" title="Joaquín Balaguer">Joaquín Balaguer</a>, was puppet president during the Trujillo dictatorship (1960-1962), and constitutional president of the country for 22 years (1966-1978 and 1986-1996)</div></div></div>
<p>Over 4,000 Dominicans were killed in action in the civil war, most of them prior to the U.S. intervention.<sup id="cite_ref-govinfo_114-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-govinfo-114">&#91;113&#93;</a></sup> A total of 44 American <a href="/wiki/Peacekeeping" title="Peacekeeping">peacekeepers</a> died and 283 were wounded. U.S. and OAS troops remained in the country for over a year and left after supervising elections in 1966 won by <a href="/wiki/Joaqu%C3%ADn_Balaguer" title="Joaquín Balaguer">Joaquín Balaguer</a>. He had been Trujillo's last puppet-president.<sup id="cite_ref-Encarta_50-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Encarta-50">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-civil_war_132-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-civil_war-132">&#91;131&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Balaguer remained in power as president for 12 years. His tenure was a period of repression of human rights and civil liberties, ostensibly to keep pro-Castro or pro-communist parties out of power; 11,000 persons were killed, tortured or <a href="/wiki/Forced_disappearance" class="mw-redirect" title="Forced disappearance">forcibly disappeared</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-134" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-134">&#91;133&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-135" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-135">&#91;134&#93;</a></sup> His rule was criticized for a growing disparity between rich and poor. It was, however, praised for an ambitious infrastructure program, which included the construction of large housing projects, sports complexes, theaters, museums, aqueducts, roads, highways, and the massive <a href="/wiki/Columbus_Lighthouse" title="Columbus Lighthouse">Columbus Lighthouse</a>, completed in 1992 during a later tenure. During Balaguer's administration, the Dominican military forced Haitians to cut sugarcane on <a href="/wiki/Batey_(sugar_workers%27_town)" title="Batey (sugar workers&#39; town)">Dominican sugar plantations</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-136" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-136">&#91;135&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In September 1977, twelve Cuban-manned <a href="/wiki/Mikoyan-Gurevich_MiG-21" title="Mikoyan-Gurevich MiG-21">MiG-21s</a> conducted strafing flights over Puerto Plata to warn Balaguer against intercepting Cuban merchant ships <a href="/wiki/Cuban_military_internationalism" title="Cuban military internationalism">headed to or returning from Africa</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-137" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-137">&#91;136&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-138" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-138">&#91;137&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Hurricane_David" title="Hurricane David">Hurricane David</a> hit the Dominican Republic in August 1979, which left upwards of 2,000 people dead and 200,000 homeless.<sup id="cite_ref-139" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-139">&#91;138&#93;</a></sup> The hurricane caused over $1 billion in damage.
</p><p>In 1978, Balaguer was succeeded in the presidency by opposition candidate <a href="/wiki/Antonio_Guzm%C3%A1n_Fern%C3%A1ndez" title="Antonio Guzmán Fernández">Antonio Guzmán Fernández</a>, of the <a href="/wiki/Dominican_Revolutionary_Party" title="Dominican Revolutionary Party">Dominican Revolutionary Party</a> (PRD). Another PRD win in 1982 followed, under <a href="/wiki/Salvador_Jorge_Blanco" title="Salvador Jorge Blanco">Salvador Jorge Blanco</a>. Balaguer regained the presidency in 1986 and was re-elected in 1990 and 1994, this last time just defeating PRD candidate <a href="/wiki/Jos%C3%A9_Francisco_Pe%C3%B1a_G%C3%B3mez" title="José Francisco Peña Gómez">José Francisco Peña Gómez</a>, a former mayor of Santo Domingo.
</p><p>During this period, the international community condemned the Dominican government for their continued exploitation of Haitian sugar cane workers; it had been alleged that thousands of these workers had essentially been put into slavery, forced to do backbreaking work under the supervision of armed guards.
</p><p>The 1994 elections were flawed, bringing on international pressure, to which Balaguer responded by scheduling another presidential contest in 1996. Balaguer was not a candidate. The PSRC candidate was his Vice President <a href="/wiki/Jacinto_Peynado_Garrigosa" title="Jacinto Peynado Garrigosa">Jacinto Peynado Garrigosa</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-140" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-140">&#91;139&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span id="1996.E2.80.93present"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="1996–present">1996–present</span></h3>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:152px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Leonel_Fernandez_Reyna.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/64/Leonel_Fernandez_Reyna.jpg/150px-Leonel_Fernandez_Reyna.jpg" decoding="async" width="150" height="197" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/64/Leonel_Fernandez_Reyna.jpg/225px-Leonel_Fernandez_Reyna.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/64/Leonel_Fernandez_Reyna.jpg/300px-Leonel_Fernandez_Reyna.jpg 2x" data-file-width="307" data-file-height="403" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Leonel_Fernandez_Reyna.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div><a href="/wiki/Leonel_Fern%C3%A1ndez" title="Leonel Fernández">Leonel Fernández</a> was president from 1996–2000 &amp; 2004–2012.</div></div></div>
<p>In 1996, with the support of Joaquín Balaguer and the Social Christian Reform Party in a coalition called the Patriotic Front, <a href="/wiki/Leonel_Fern%C3%A1ndez" title="Leonel Fernández">Leonel Fernández</a> achieved the first-ever win for the <a href="/wiki/Dominican_Liberation_Party" title="Dominican Liberation Party">Dominican Liberation Party</a> (PLD),<sup id="cite_ref-141" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-141">&#91;140&#93;</a></sup> which Bosch had founded in 1973 after leaving the PRD (which he also had founded). Fernández oversaw a fast-growing economy: growth averaged 7.7% per year, unemployment fell, and there were stable exchange and inflation rates.<sup id="cite_ref-coha_142-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-coha-142">&#91;141&#93;</a></sup>
His administration supported the process of modernizing the judicial system, making transparent the creation of an independent Supreme Court of Justice. Efforts were also made to reform and modernize the other state bodies. In addition, relations with Cuba were reestablished and the Free Trade Agreement with Central America was signed, which was the genesis for the signing of DR-CAFTA.
</p>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:152px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Danilo_Medina2.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e6/Danilo_Medina2.jpg/150px-Danilo_Medina2.jpg" decoding="async" width="150" height="216" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/e/e6/Danilo_Medina2.jpg 1.5x" data-file-width="222" data-file-height="319" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Danilo_Medina2.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div><a href="/wiki/Danilo_Medina" title="Danilo Medina">Danilo Medina</a> was president from 2012–2020.</div></div></div>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:152px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Protestas_dominicanas_en_Santo_Domingo_2020.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/47/Protestas_dominicanas_en_Santo_Domingo_2020.jpg/150px-Protestas_dominicanas_en_Santo_Domingo_2020.jpg" decoding="async" width="150" height="84" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/47/Protestas_dominicanas_en_Santo_Domingo_2020.jpg/225px-Protestas_dominicanas_en_Santo_Domingo_2020.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/47/Protestas_dominicanas_en_Santo_Domingo_2020.jpg/300px-Protestas_dominicanas_en_Santo_Domingo_2020.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2048" data-file-height="1152" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Protestas_dominicanas_en_Santo_Domingo_2020.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div><a href="/wiki/Timeline_of_2020_Dominican_Republic_municipal_elections_protests" class="mw-redirect" title="Timeline of 2020 Dominican Republic municipal elections protests">2020 Dominican Republic municipal elections protests</a> in Plaza de La Bandera, Santo Domingo.</div></div></div>
<p>In 2000, the PRD's <a href="/wiki/Hip%C3%B3lito_Mej%C3%ADa" title="Hipólito Mejía">Hipólito Mejía</a> won the election. This was a time of economic troubles.<sup id="cite_ref-coha_142-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-coha-142">&#91;141&#93;</a></sup> Nevertheless, his government was marked by major economic and social reforms, apart from a decentralization of the national budget. Among the laws created in this period are the Social Security, the Monetary and Financial Code, the Stock Market, Electricity, Electronic Commerce, the Police Law, the Environment, Public Health, the Chamber of Accounts, the Insurance Law, Administrative Independence and Budgetary of the Legislative Power and Judicial Power; in addition, creation of the Santo Domingo Province and its municipalities, a larger budget for municipalities, as well as other laws. This meant in the 2002 elections, obtaining a congressional and municipal majority. During this period, great sports structures were built for the 2003 Pan American Games. Under Mejía, the Dominican Republic participated in the US-led coalition, as part of the Multinational <a href="/wiki/Plus_Ultra_Brigade" title="Plus Ultra Brigade">Plus Ultra Brigade</a>, during the 2003 <a href="/wiki/Iraq_War" title="Iraq War">invasion of Iraq</a>, suffering no casualties. In 2004, the country withdrew its approximately 300 soldiers from <a href="/wiki/Iraq" title="Iraq">Iraq</a>. The government of President Mejía had to negotiate the Free Trade Agreement with the United States, the main trading partner. He also promoted various commercial measures, popularly called "Economic Package". This "package" was accompanied by a series of social measures, such as aid to agricultural producers, subsidies to electricity rates, construction of streets, sidewalks, local roads, etc., as well as subsidies to poor families whose children attended schools, as well as the creation of new taxes and increases in existing ones.
</p><p>In 2003, the bankruptcy of three banking entities whose savers were protected by the government led to inflation. This caused a severe economic crisis accompanied by the devaluation of the currency and capital outflows, instability that led to the bankruptcy of many companies. With the congressional majority obtained in 2002, President Mejía promoted a constitutional reform that restored the possibility of presidential reelection, which had been abolished in 1994 at the request of his own party. This reform caused problems within his party causing a division within its main leaders.
Mejía was defeated in his re-election effort in 2004 by Leonel Fernández of the PLD who won with 57.11% of the votes the presidential elections. At the beginning of his second presidential term, he made an effort to combat the economic crisis, reestablishing macroeconomic stability, through the reduction of the dollar exchange rate and the return of confidence in the economy among other measures. However, his administrations was accused of corruption. President Fernández's management consisted of improving Santo Domingo's collective transport system, the first Metro line was built; the completion of the main communication routes to the country's tourist poles; the construction of new schools or the construction of more classrooms, as well as the provision of computer centers with modern computers and Internet to the communities in coordination with schools, churches or clubs. It continued its program of modernization of the state, strengthening the formulation and execution of the budget and promoting laws to make the public acquisition of goods and services transparent.
</p><p>In 2008, Fernández was elected for a third term.<sup id="cite_ref-ussdnote_143-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ussdnote-143">&#91;142&#93;</a></sup> Fernández and the PLD are credited with initiatives that have moved the country forward technologically, on the other hand, his administrations have been accused of corruption.<sup id="cite_ref-coha_142-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-coha-142">&#91;141&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:152px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Luis_Abinader45.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/39/Luis_Abinader45.jpg/150px-Luis_Abinader45.jpg" decoding="async" width="150" height="175" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/39/Luis_Abinader45.jpg/225px-Luis_Abinader45.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/39/Luis_Abinader45.jpg/300px-Luis_Abinader45.jpg 2x" data-file-width="471" data-file-height="549" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Luis_Abinader45.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div><a href="/wiki/Luis_Abinader" title="Luis Abinader">Luis Abinader</a>, the current president of the Dominican Republic since 2020.</div></div></div>
<p><a href="/wiki/Danilo_Medina" title="Danilo Medina">Danilo Medina</a> of the PLD was elected president in 2012 and re-elected in 2016. On the other hand, a significant increase in crime, government corruption and a weak justice system threaten to overshadow their administrative period.<sup id="cite_ref-144" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-144">&#91;143&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-145" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-145">&#91;144&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>He was succeeded by the opposition candidate <a href="/wiki/Luis_Abinader" title="Luis Abinader">Luis Abinader</a> in the <a href="/wiki/2020_Dominican_Republic_general_election" title="2020 Dominican Republic general election">2020 election</a> (weeks after <a href="/wiki/2020_Dominican_Republic_protests" title="2020 Dominican Republic protests">protests erupted in the country</a> against Medina's government), marking the end to 16 years in power of the centre-left Dominican Liberation Party (PLD).<sup id="cite_ref-146" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-146">&#91;145&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-147" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-147">&#91;146&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<div style="clear:both;"></div>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Geography">Geography</span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Geography_of_the_Dominican_Republic" title="Geography of the Dominican Republic">Geography of the Dominican Republic</a></div>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:302px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Dominican_Republic_relief_location_map.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/10/Dominican_Republic_relief_location_map.jpg/300px-Dominican_Republic_relief_location_map.jpg" decoding="async" width="300" height="244" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/10/Dominican_Republic_relief_location_map.jpg/450px-Dominican_Republic_relief_location_map.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/10/Dominican_Republic_relief_location_map.jpg/600px-Dominican_Republic_relief_location_map.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1338" data-file-height="1088" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Dominican_Republic_relief_location_map.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Topographical map of Dominican Republic</div></div></div>
<p>The Dominican Republic comprises the eastern five-eighths of <a href="/wiki/Hispaniola" title="Hispaniola">Hispaniola</a>, the second-largest island in the <a href="/wiki/Greater_Antilles" title="Greater Antilles">Greater Antilles</a>, with the <a href="/wiki/Atlantic_Ocean" title="Atlantic Ocean">Atlantic Ocean</a> to the north and the <a href="/wiki/Caribbean_Sea" title="Caribbean Sea">Caribbean Sea</a> to the south. It shares the island roughly at a 2:1 ratio with <a href="/wiki/Haiti" title="Haiti">Haiti</a>, the north-to-south (though somewhat irregular) border between the two countries being 376&#160;km (234&#160;mi).<sup id="cite_ref-CIADemo_4-14" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CIADemo-4">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> To the north and north-west lie <a href="/wiki/The_Bahamas" title="The Bahamas">The Bahamas</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Turks_and_Caicos_Islands" title="Turks and Caicos Islands">Turks and Caicos Islands</a>, and to the east, across the <a href="/wiki/Mona_Passage" title="Mona Passage">Mona Passage</a>, the US Commonwealth of <a href="/wiki/Puerto_Rico" title="Puerto Rico">Puerto Rico</a>. The country's area is reported variously as 48,442&#160;km<sup>2</sup> (18,704&#160;sq&#160;mi) (by the embassy in the United States)<sup id="cite_ref-embassy_5-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-embassy-5">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup> and 48,670&#160;km<sup>2</sup> (18,792&#160;sq&#160;mi),<sup id="cite_ref-CIADemo_4-15" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CIADemo-4">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> making it the second largest country in the <a href="/wiki/Antilles" title="Antilles">Antilles</a>, after <a href="/wiki/Cuba" title="Cuba">Cuba</a>. The Dominican Republic's capital and largest city <a href="/wiki/Santo_Domingo" title="Santo Domingo">Santo Domingo</a> is on the southern coast.<sup id="cite_ref-CIADemo_4-16" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CIADemo-4">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Monte_Cristi,_Coastline.JPG" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/37/Monte_Cristi%2C_Coastline.JPG/220px-Monte_Cristi%2C_Coastline.JPG" decoding="async" width="220" height="165" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/37/Monte_Cristi%2C_Coastline.JPG/330px-Monte_Cristi%2C_Coastline.JPG 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/37/Monte_Cristi%2C_Coastline.JPG/440px-Monte_Cristi%2C_Coastline.JPG 2x" data-file-width="1024" data-file-height="768" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Monte_Cristi,_Coastline.JPG" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div><a href="/wiki/Monte_Cristi,_Dominican_Republic" title="Monte Cristi, Dominican Republic">Monte Cristi</a> coastline.</div></div></div>
<p>The Dominican Republic has four important mountain ranges. The most northerly is the <i><a href="/wiki/Cordillera_Septentrional" title="Cordillera Septentrional">Cordillera Septentrional</a></i> ("Northern Mountain Range"), which extends from the northwestern coastal town of <a href="/wiki/San_Fernando_de_Monte_Cristi" class="mw-redirect" title="San Fernando de Monte Cristi">Monte Cristi</a>, near the Haitian border, to the <a href="/wiki/Saman%C3%A1_Peninsula" title="Samaná Peninsula">Samaná Peninsula</a> in the east, running parallel to the Atlantic coast. The highest range in the Dominican Republic&#160;– indeed, in the whole of the West Indies&#160;– is the <i><a href="/wiki/Cordillera_Central,_Dominican_Republic" title="Cordillera Central, Dominican Republic">Cordillera Central</a></i> ("Central Mountain Range"). It gradually bends southwards and finishes near the town of <a href="/wiki/Azua_de_Compostela" class="mw-redirect" title="Azua de Compostela">Azua</a>, on the Caribbean coast. In the Cordillera Central are the four highest peaks in the Caribbean: <a href="/wiki/Pico_Duarte" title="Pico Duarte">Pico Duarte</a> (3,098 metres or 10,164 feet above sea level),<sup id="cite_ref-CIADemo_4-17" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CIADemo-4">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> La Pelona (3,094 metres or 10,151 feet), La Rucilla (3,049 metres or 10,003 feet), and Pico Yaque (2,760 metres or 9,055 feet). In the southwest corner of the country, south of the Cordillera Central, there are two other ranges: the more northerly of the two is the <i><a href="/w/index.php?title=Sierra_de_Neiba&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Sierra de Neiba (page does not exist)">Sierra de Neiba</a></i>, while in the south the <i><a href="/wiki/Sierra_de_Bahoruco" class="mw-redirect" title="Sierra de Bahoruco">Sierra de Bahoruco</a></i> is a continuation of the <a href="/wiki/Massif_de_la_Selle" class="mw-redirect" title="Massif de la Selle">Massif de la Selle</a> in Haiti. There are other, minor mountain ranges, such as the <i>Cordillera Oriental</i> ("Eastern Mountain Range"), <i>Sierra Martín García</i>, <i>Sierra de Yamasá</i>, and <i>Sierra de Samaná</i>.
</p><p>Between the Central and Northern mountain ranges lies the rich and fertile <a href="/wiki/Cibao" title="Cibao">Cibao</a> valley. This major valley is home to the cities of <a href="/wiki/Santiago_de_los_Caballeros" title="Santiago de los Caballeros">Santiago</a> and <a href="/wiki/Concepci%C3%B3n_de_La_Vega" class="mw-redirect" title="Concepción de La Vega">La Vega</a> and most of the farming areas of the nation. Rather less productive are the semi-arid San Juan Valley, south of the Central Cordillera, and the Neiba Valley, tucked between the Sierra de Neiba and the Sierra de Bahoruco. Much of the land around the <a href="/wiki/Lake_Enriquillo" title="Lake Enriquillo">Enriquillo</a> Basin is below sea level, with a hot, arid, desert-like environment. There are other smaller valleys in the mountains, such as the <a href="/wiki/Constanza,_Dominican_Republic" title="Constanza, Dominican Republic">Constanza</a>, <a href="/wiki/Jarabacoa" title="Jarabacoa">Jarabacoa</a>, <a href="/wiki/Villa_Altagracia" title="Villa Altagracia">Villa Altagracia</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Bonao" title="Bonao">Bonao</a> valleys.
</p>
<div class="thumb tleft"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Los_Haitises_Park.jpeg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0f/Los_Haitises_Park.jpeg/220px-Los_Haitises_Park.jpeg" decoding="async" width="220" height="147" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0f/Los_Haitises_Park.jpeg/330px-Los_Haitises_Park.jpeg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0f/Los_Haitises_Park.jpeg/440px-Los_Haitises_Park.jpeg 2x" data-file-width="1920" data-file-height="1280" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Los_Haitises_Park.jpeg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Mangroves in <a href="/wiki/Los_Haitises_National_Park" title="Los Haitises National Park">Los Haitises National Park</a></div></div></div>
<p>The <i>Llano Costero del Caribe</i> ("Caribbean Coastal Plain") is the largest of the plains in the Dominican Republic. Stretching north and east of Santo Domingo, it contains many sugar plantations in the <a href="/wiki/Savannah" class="mw-redirect" title="Savannah">savannahs</a> that are common there. West of Santo Domingo its width is reduced to 10 kilometres (6.2&#160;mi) as it hugs the coast, finishing at the mouth of the Ocoa River. Another large plain is the <i>Plena de Azua</i> ("Azua Plain"), a very arid region in <a href="/wiki/Azua_Province" title="Azua Province">Azua Province</a>. A few other small coastal plains are on the northern coast and in the <a href="/wiki/Pedernales_Province" title="Pedernales Province">Pedernales</a> Peninsula.
</p><p>Four major rivers drain the numerous mountains of the Dominican Republic. The <a href="/wiki/Yaque_del_Norte_River" title="Yaque del Norte River">Yaque del Norte</a> is the longest and most important Dominican river. It carries excess water down from the Cibao Valley and empties into Monte Cristi Bay, in the northwest. Likewise, the <a href="/wiki/Yuna_River" title="Yuna River">Yuna River</a> serves the Vega Real and empties into Samaná Bay, in the northeast. Drainage of the San Juan Valley is provided by the San Juan River, <a href="/wiki/Tributary" title="Tributary">tributary</a> of the <a href="/wiki/Yaque_del_Sur" class="mw-redirect" title="Yaque del Sur">Yaque del Sur</a>, which empties into the Caribbean, in the south. The <a href="/wiki/Artibonite_River" title="Artibonite River">Artibonito</a> is the longest river of Hispaniola and flows westward into Haiti.
</p>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:172px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Salto_el_Lim%C3%B3n_(14688207924).jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8b/Salto_el_Lim%C3%B3n_%2814688207924%29.jpg/170px-Salto_el_Lim%C3%B3n_%2814688207924%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="227" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8b/Salto_el_Lim%C3%B3n_%2814688207924%29.jpg/255px-Salto_el_Lim%C3%B3n_%2814688207924%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8b/Salto_el_Lim%C3%B3n_%2814688207924%29.jpg/340px-Salto_el_Lim%C3%B3n_%2814688207924%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="3000" data-file-height="4000" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Salto_el_Lim%C3%B3n_(14688207924).jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>There are numerous waterfalls across Dominican Republic. In the image the Salto del Limón</div></div></div>
<p>There are many lakes and coastal lagoons. The largest lake is <a href="/wiki/Lake_Enriquillo" title="Lake Enriquillo">Enriquillo</a>, a <a href="/wiki/Salt_lake" title="Salt lake">salt lake</a> at 45 metres (148&#160;ft) below sea level, the lowest elevation in the Caribbean.<sup id="cite_ref-CIADemo_4-18" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CIADemo-4">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> Other important lakes are <a href="/w/index.php?title=Laguna_de_Rinc%C3%B3n&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Laguna de Rincón (page does not exist)">Laguna de Rincón</a> or Cabral, with <a href="/wiki/Freshwater" class="mw-redirect" title="Freshwater">fresh water</a>, and <a href="/w/index.php?title=Laguna_de_Oviedo&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Laguna de Oviedo (page does not exist)">Laguna de Oviedo</a>, a lagoon with <a href="/wiki/Brackish_water" title="Brackish water">brackish water</a>.
</p><p>There are many small offshore islands and <a href="/wiki/Cay" title="Cay">cays</a> that form part of the Dominican territory. The two largest islands near shore are <a href="/wiki/Saona_Island" title="Saona Island">Saona</a>, in the southeast, and <a href="/wiki/Beata" title="Beata">Beata</a>, in the southwest. Smaller islands include the <a href="/w/index.php?title=Cayos_Siete_Hermanos&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Cayos Siete Hermanos (page does not exist)">Cayos Siete Hermanos</a>, <a href="/w/index.php?title=Isla_Cabra&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Isla Cabra (page does not exist)">Isla Cabra</a>, <a href="/w/index.php?title=Cayo_Jackson&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Cayo Jackson (page does not exist)">Cayo Jackson</a>, <a href="/w/index.php?title=Cayo_Lim%C3%B3n&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Cayo Limón (page does not exist)">Cayo Limón</a>, <a href="/wiki/Cayo_Levantado" title="Cayo Levantado">Cayo Levantado</a>, <a href="/w/index.php?title=Cayo_la_Bocaina&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Cayo la Bocaina (page does not exist)">Cayo la Bocaina</a>, <a href="/wiki/Catalina_Island_(Dominican_Republic)" title="Catalina Island (Dominican Republic)">Catalanita</a>, <a href="/w/index.php?title=Cayo_Pisaje&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Cayo Pisaje (page does not exist)">Cayo Pisaje</a> and <a href="/wiki/Isla_Alto_Velo" class="mw-redirect" title="Isla Alto Velo">Isla Alto Velo</a>. To the north, at distances of 100–200 kilometres (62–124&#160;mi), are three extensive, largely submerged <a href="/wiki/Bank_(topography)" class="mw-redirect" title="Bank (topography)">banks</a>, which geographically are a southeast continuation of <a href="/wiki/The_Bahamas" title="The Bahamas">the Bahamas</a>: <a href="/wiki/Navidad_Bank" title="Navidad Bank">Navidad Bank</a>, <a href="/wiki/Silver_Bank" title="Silver Bank">Silver Bank</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Mouchoir_Bank" title="Mouchoir Bank">Mouchoir Bank</a>. Navidad Bank and Silver Bank have been officially claimed by the Dominican Republic.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (August 2019)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> <a href="/w/index.php?title=Isla_Cabritos&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Isla Cabritos (page does not exist)">Isla Cabritos</a> lies within <a href="/wiki/Lago_Enriquillo" class="mw-redirect" title="Lago Enriquillo">Lago Enriquillo</a>.
</p><p>The Dominican Republic is located near fault action in the Caribbean. In 1946, it suffered <a href="/wiki/1946_Dominican_Republic_earthquake" title="1946 Dominican Republic earthquake">a magnitude 8.1 earthquake</a> off the northeast coast, triggering a <a href="/wiki/Tsunami" title="Tsunami">tsunami</a> that killed about 1,800, mostly in coastal communities. Caribbean countries and the United States have collaborated to create tsunami warning systems and are mapping high-risk low-lying areas.
</p><p>The country is home to five terrestrial ecoregions: <a href="/wiki/Hispaniolan_moist_forests" title="Hispaniolan moist forests">Hispaniolan moist forests</a>, <a href="/wiki/Hispaniolan_dry_forests" title="Hispaniolan dry forests">Hispaniolan dry forests</a>, <a href="/wiki/Hispaniolan_pine_forests" title="Hispaniolan pine forests">Hispaniolan pine forests</a>, <a href="/wiki/Enriquillo_wetlands" title="Enriquillo wetlands">Enriquillo wetlands</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Greater_Antilles_mangroves" title="Greater Antilles mangroves">Greater Antilles mangroves</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-DinersteinOlson2017_148-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-DinersteinOlson2017-148">&#91;147&#93;</a></sup> It had a 2018 <a href="/wiki/Forest_Landscape_Integrity_Index" title="Forest Landscape Integrity Index">Forest Landscape Integrity Index</a> mean score of 4.18/10, ranking it 134th globally out of 172 countries.<sup id="cite_ref-FLII-Supplementary_149-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FLII-Supplementary-149">&#91;148&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Climate">Climate</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Climate_of_the_Dominican_Republic" class="mw-redirect" title="Climate of the Dominican Republic">Climate of the Dominican Republic</a></div>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:302px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Dominican_Republic_K%C3%B6ppen.svg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7e/Dominican_Republic_K%C3%B6ppen.svg/300px-Dominican_Republic_K%C3%B6ppen.svg.png" decoding="async" width="300" height="307" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7e/Dominican_Republic_K%C3%B6ppen.svg/450px-Dominican_Republic_K%C3%B6ppen.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7e/Dominican_Republic_K%C3%B6ppen.svg/600px-Dominican_Republic_K%C3%B6ppen.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="765" data-file-height="783" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Dominican_Republic_K%C3%B6ppen.svg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div><a href="/wiki/K%C3%B6ppen_climate_classification_system" class="mw-redirect" title="Köppen climate classification system">Köppen climate types</a> of the Dominican Republic</div></div></div>
<p>The Dominican Republic has a <a href="/wiki/Tropical_rainforest_climate" title="Tropical rainforest climate">tropical rainforest climate</a><sup id="cite_ref-researchgate.net_150-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-researchgate.net-150">&#91;149&#93;</a></sup> in the coastal and lowland areas. Some areas, such as most of the <a href="/wiki/Cibao" title="Cibao">Cibao</a> region, have a <a href="/wiki/Tropical_savanna_climate" title="Tropical savanna climate">tropical savanna climate</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-researchgate.net_150-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-researchgate.net-150">&#91;149&#93;</a></sup> Due to its diverse topography, Dominican Republic's climate shows considerable variation over short distances and is the most varied of all the Antilles. The annual average temperature is 25&#160;°C (77&#160;°F). At higher elevations the temperature averages 18&#160;°C (64.4&#160;°F) while near sea level the average temperature is 28&#160;°C (82.4&#160;°F). Low temperatures of 0&#160;°C (32&#160;°F) are possible in the mountains while high temperatures of 40&#160;°C (104&#160;°F) are possible in protected valleys. January and February are the coolest months of the year while August is the hottest month. Snowfall can be seen on rare occasions on the summit of <a href="/wiki/Pico_Duarte" title="Pico Duarte">Pico Duarte</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-DRcli_151-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-DRcli-151">&#91;150&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The <a href="/wiki/Wet_season" title="Wet season">wet season</a> along the northern coast lasts from November through January. Elsewhere the wet season stretches from May through November, with May being the wettest month. Average annual rainfall is 1,500 millimetres (59.1&#160;in) countrywide, with individual locations in the Valle de Neiba seeing averages as low as 350 millimetres (13.8&#160;in) while the Cordillera Oriental averages 2,740 millimetres (107.9&#160;in). The driest part of the country lies in the west.<sup id="cite_ref-DRcli_151-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-DRcli-151">&#91;150&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<ul class="gallery mw-gallery-packed">
		<li class="gallerybox" style="width: 184.66666666667px"><div style="width: 184.66666666667px">
			<div class="thumb" style="width: 182.66666666667px;"><div style="margin:0px auto;"><a href="/wiki/File:Constanza,_valle_nuevo,_clima_invierno..jpg" class="image" title="Frosted alpine forest in Constanza, Dominican Republic"><img alt="Frosted alpine forest in Constanza, Dominican Republic" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9b/Constanza%2C_valle_nuevo%2C_clima_invierno..jpg/274px-Constanza%2C_valle_nuevo%2C_clima_invierno..jpg" decoding="async" width="183" height="122" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9b/Constanza%2C_valle_nuevo%2C_clima_invierno..jpg/412px-Constanza%2C_valle_nuevo%2C_clima_invierno..jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9b/Constanza%2C_valle_nuevo%2C_clima_invierno..jpg/549px-Constanza%2C_valle_nuevo%2C_clima_invierno..jpg 2x" data-file-width="550" data-file-height="367" /></a></div></div>

</p>
