			<div class="thumb" style="width: 162.66666666667px;"><div style="margin:0px auto;"><a href="/wiki/File:Jaragua_National_Park_(Road2).JPG" class="image" title="Semi-arid climate in Pedernales, Dominican Republic"><img alt="Semi-arid climate in Pedernales, Dominican Republic" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f1/Jaragua_National_Park_%28Road2%29.JPG/244px-Jaragua_National_Park_%28Road2%29.JPG" decoding="async" width="163" height="122" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f1/Jaragua_National_Park_%28Road2%29.JPG/367px-Jaragua_National_Park_%28Road2%29.JPG 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f1/Jaragua_National_Park_%28Road2%29.JPG/488px-Jaragua_National_Park_%28Road2%29.JPG 2x" data-file-width="2048" data-file-height="1536" /></a></div></div>

</p>
			</div>
		</div></li>
</ul>
<p><br />
<a href="/wiki/Tropical_cyclones" class="mw-redirect" title="Tropical cyclones">Tropical cyclones</a> strike the Dominican Republic every couple of years, with 65% of the impacts along the southern coast. Hurricanes are most likely between June and October.<sup id="cite_ref-DRcli_151-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-DRcli-151">&#91;150&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-CIADemo_4-19" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CIADemo-4">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> The last major hurricane that struck the country was <a href="/wiki/Hurricane_Georges" title="Hurricane Georges">Hurricane Georges</a> in 1998.<sup id="cite_ref-HURDAT_152-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-HURDAT-152">&#91;151&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<div style="clear:both;"></div>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Government_and_politics">Government and politics</span></h2>

<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Santo_Domingo_National_Palace.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/31/Santo_Domingo_National_Palace.jpg/220px-Santo_Domingo_National_Palace.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="141" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/31/Santo_Domingo_National_Palace.jpg/330px-Santo_Domingo_National_Palace.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/31/Santo_Domingo_National_Palace.jpg/440px-Santo_Domingo_National_Palace.jpg 2x" data-file-width="4566" data-file-height="2936" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Santo_Domingo_National_Palace.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>The <a href="/wiki/National_Palace_(Dominican_Republic)" title="National Palace (Dominican Republic)">National Palace</a> in Santo Domingo</div></div></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Politics_of_the_Dominican_Republic" title="Politics of the Dominican Republic">Politics of the Dominican Republic</a></div>
<p>The Dominican Republic is a <a href="/wiki/Representative_democracy" title="Representative democracy">representative democracy</a> or <a href="/wiki/Democracy" title="Democracy">democratic republic</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-embassy_5-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-embassy-5">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-CIADemo_4-20" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CIADemo-4">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-ussdnote_143-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ussdnote-143">&#91;142&#93;</a></sup> with three branches of power: executive, <a href="/wiki/Legislature" title="Legislature">legislative</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Judiciary" title="Judiciary">judicial</a>. The <a href="/wiki/President_of_the_Dominican_Republic" title="President of the Dominican Republic">president of the Dominican Republic</a> heads the executive branch and executes laws passed by the <a href="/wiki/Congress_of_the_Dominican_Republic" title="Congress of the Dominican Republic">congress</a>, appoints the cabinet, and is commander in chief of the <a href="/wiki/Military_of_the_Dominican_Republic" class="mw-redirect" title="Military of the Dominican Republic">armed forces</a>. The president and vice-president run for office on the same ticket and are elected by direct vote for four-year terms. The national legislature is bicameral, composed of a <a href="/wiki/Senate_of_the_Dominican_Republic" title="Senate of the Dominican Republic">senate</a>, which has 32 members, and the <a href="/wiki/Chamber_of_Deputies_of_the_Dominican_Republic" title="Chamber of Deputies of the Dominican Republic">Chamber of Deputies</a>, with 178 members.<sup id="cite_ref-ussdnote_143-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ussdnote-143">&#91;142&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Judicial authority rests with the <a href="/wiki/Government_of_the_Dominican_Republic#Judicial_branch" class="mw-redirect" title="Government of the Dominican Republic">Supreme Court of Justice</a>'s 16 members.  The court "alone hears actions against the president, designated members of his Cabinet, and members of Congress when the legislature is in session."<sup id="cite_ref-ussdnote_143-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ussdnote-143">&#91;142&#93;</a></sup> The court is appointed by a council known as the <a href="/wiki/National_Council_of_the_Magistracy_(Dominican_Republic)" title="National Council of the Magistracy (Dominican Republic)">National Council of the Magistracy</a> which is composed of the president, the leaders of both houses of Congress, the President of the Supreme Court, and an opposition or non–governing-party member.
</p><p>The Dominican Republic has a <a href="/wiki/Multi-party_system" title="Multi-party system">multi-party political system</a>. Elections are held every two years, alternating between the <a href="/wiki/Elections_in_the_Dominican_Republic" title="Elections in the Dominican Republic">presidential elections</a>, which are held in years evenly divisible by four, and the congressional and municipal elections, which are held in even-numbered years not divisible by four. "International observers have found that presidential and congressional elections since 1996 have been generally free and fair."<sup id="cite_ref-ussdnote_143-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ussdnote-143">&#91;142&#93;</a></sup> The Central Elections Board (JCE) of nine members supervises elections, and its decisions are unappealable.<sup id="cite_ref-ussdnote_143-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ussdnote-143">&#91;142&#93;</a></sup> Starting from 2016, elections will be held jointly, after a constitutional reform.<sup id="cite_ref-153" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-153">&#91;152&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Political_culture">Political culture</span></h3>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:152px;"><a href="/wiki/File:President_Abinader_2020.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9d/President_Abinader_2020.jpg/150px-President_Abinader_2020.jpg" decoding="async" width="150" height="213" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9d/President_Abinader_2020.jpg/225px-President_Abinader_2020.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9d/President_Abinader_2020.jpg/300px-President_Abinader_2020.jpg 2x" data-file-width="593" data-file-height="843" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:President_Abinader_2020.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Dominican President <a href="/wiki/Luis_Abinader" title="Luis Abinader">Luis Abinader</a></div></div></div>
<p>The three major parties are the conservative <a href="/wiki/Social_Christian_Reformist_Party" title="Social Christian Reformist Party">Social Christian Reformist Party</a> (<a href="/wiki/Spanish_language" title="Spanish language">Spanish</a>: <i lang="es">Partido Reformista Social Cristiano (PRSC)</i>), in power 1966–78 and 1986–96; and the <a href="/wiki/Social_democracy" title="Social democracy">social democratic</a> <a href="/wiki/Dominican_Revolutionary_Party" title="Dominican Revolutionary Party">Dominican Revolutionary Party</a> (<a href="/wiki/Spanish_language" title="Spanish language">Spanish</a>: <i lang="es">Partido Revolucionario Dominicano (PRD)</i>), in power in 1963, 1978–86, and 2000–04; and the <a href="/wiki/Dominican_Liberation_Party" title="Dominican Liberation Party">Dominican Liberation Party</a> (<a href="/wiki/Spanish_language" title="Spanish language">Spanish</a>: <i lang="es">Partido de la Liberación Dominicana (PLD)</i>), in power 1996–2000 and since 2004.
</p><p>The <a href="/wiki/Dominican_Republic_presidential_election,_2008" class="mw-redirect" title="Dominican Republic presidential election, 2008">presidential elections of 2008</a> were held on May 16, 2008, with incumbent Leonel Fernández winning 53% of the vote.<sup id="cite_ref-154" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-154">&#91;153&#93;</a></sup> He defeated <a href="/wiki/Miguel_Vargas_(politician)" title="Miguel Vargas (politician)">Miguel Vargas Maldonado</a>, of the PRD, who achieved a 40.48% share of the vote. <a href="/wiki/Amable_Aristy" title="Amable Aristy">Amable Aristy</a>, of the PRSC, achieved 4.59% of the vote. Other minority candidates, which included former Attorney General <a href="/wiki/Guillermo_Moreno_Garcia" class="mw-redirect" title="Guillermo Moreno Garcia">Guillermo Moreno</a> from the <a href="/wiki/Movement_for_Independence,_Unity_and_Change" class="mw-redirect" title="Movement for Independence, Unity and Change">Movement for Independence, Unity and Change</a> (<a href="/wiki/Spanish_language" title="Spanish language">Spanish</a>: <i lang="es">Movimiento Independencia, Unidad y Cambio (MIUCA)</i>), and PRSC former presidential candidate and defector <a href="/wiki/Eduardo_Estrella" title="Eduardo Estrella">Eduardo Estrella</a>, obtained less than 1% of the vote.
</p><p>In the <a href="/wiki/Dominican_Republic_presidential_election,_2012" class="mw-redirect" title="Dominican Republic presidential election, 2012">2012 presidential elections</a>, the incumbent president <a href="/wiki/Leonel_Fern%C3%A1ndez" title="Leonel Fernández">Leonel Fernández</a> (PLD) declined his aspirations<sup id="cite_ref-155" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-155">&#91;154&#93;</a></sup> and instead the PLD elected <a href="/wiki/Danilo_Medina" title="Danilo Medina">Danilo Medina</a> as its candidate. This time the PRD presented ex-president <a href="/wiki/Hip%C3%B3lito_Mej%C3%ADa" title="Hipólito Mejía">Hipólito Mejía</a> as its choice. The contest was won by Medina with 51.21% of the vote, against 46.95% in favor of Mejía. Candidate Guillermo Moreno obtained 1.37% of the votes.<sup id="cite_ref-156" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-156">&#91;155&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 2014, the <a href="/wiki/Modern_Revolutionary_Party" title="Modern Revolutionary Party">Modern Revolutionary Party</a> (<a href="/wiki/Spanish_language" title="Spanish language">Spanish</a>: <i lang="es">Partido Revolucionario Moderno</i>) was created<sup id="cite_ref-157" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-157">&#91;156&#93;</a></sup> by a faction of leaders from the PRD, and has since become the predominant opposition party, polling in second place for the May 2016 general elections.<sup id="cite_ref-158" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-158">&#91;157&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 2020, <a href="/wiki/2020_Dominican_Republic_protests" title="2020 Dominican Republic protests">protests erupted</a> against the PLD's rule. The presidential candidate for the opposition Modern Revolutionary Party (PRM), <a href="/wiki/Luis_Abinader" title="Luis Abinader">Luis Abinader</a>,  won the <a href="/wiki/2020_Dominican_Republic_general_election" title="2020 Dominican Republic general election">election</a>, defeating the Dominican Liberation Party (PLD), which had governed since 2004.<sup id="cite_ref-159" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-159">&#91;158&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Foreign_relations">Foreign relations</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Further information: <a href="/wiki/Foreign_relations_of_the_Dominican_Republic" title="Foreign relations of the Dominican Republic">Foreign relations of the Dominican Republic</a></div>
<p>The Dominican Republic has a close relationship with the <a href="/wiki/Dominican_Republic-United_States_relations" class="mw-redirect" title="Dominican Republic-United States relations">United States</a>, and has close cultural ties with the <a href="/wiki/Commonwealth_of_Puerto_Rico" class="mw-redirect" title="Commonwealth of Puerto Rico">Commonwealth of Puerto Rico</a>, and other states and jurisdictions of the United States.
</p><p>The Dominican Republic's <a href="/wiki/Dominican_Republic%E2%80%93Haiti_relations" title="Dominican Republic–Haiti relations">relationship with neighbouring Haiti</a> is strained over mass Haitian migration to the Dominican Republic, with citizens of the Dominican Republic blaming the Haitians for increased crime and other social problems.<sup id="cite_ref-160" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-160">&#91;159&#93;</a></sup> The Dominican Republic is a regular member of the <a href="/wiki/Organisation_Internationale_de_la_Francophonie" class="mw-redirect" title="Organisation Internationale de la Francophonie">Organisation Internationale de la Francophonie</a>.
</p><p>The Dominican Republic has a <a href="/wiki/Free-trade_area" title="Free-trade area">Free Trade Agreement</a> with the United States, Costa Rica, El Salvador, Guatemala, Honduras and Nicaragua via the <a href="/wiki/Dominican_Republic%E2%80%93Central_America_Free_Trade_Agreement" title="Dominican Republic–Central America Free Trade Agreement">Dominican Republic-Central America Free Trade Agreement</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-161" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-161">&#91;160&#93;</a></sup> And an <a href="/wiki/Economic_Partnership_Agreements" title="Economic Partnership Agreements">Economic Partnership Agreement</a> with the <a href="/wiki/European_Union" title="European Union">European Union</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Caribbean_Community" title="Caribbean Community">Caribbean Community</a> via the <a href="/wiki/CARIFORUM" title="CARIFORUM">Caribbean Forum.</a><sup id="cite_ref-162" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-162">&#91;161&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Military">Military</span></h3>
<div class="thumb tleft"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Two_Commando_soldiers_of_the_MEFA_provide_security.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c7/Two_Commando_soldiers_of_the_MEFA_provide_security.jpg/220px-Two_Commando_soldiers_of_the_MEFA_provide_security.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="147" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c7/Two_Commando_soldiers_of_the_MEFA_provide_security.jpg/330px-Two_Commando_soldiers_of_the_MEFA_provide_security.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c7/Two_Commando_soldiers_of_the_MEFA_provide_security.jpg/440px-Two_Commando_soldiers_of_the_MEFA_provide_security.jpg 2x" data-file-width="3872" data-file-height="2592" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Two_Commando_soldiers_of_the_MEFA_provide_security.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Dominican soldiers training in Santo Domingo</div></div></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Armed_Forces_of_the_Dominican_Republic" title="Armed Forces of the Dominican Republic">Armed Forces of the Dominican Republic</a></div>
<p>The Armed Forces of the Dominican Republic are the military forces of the Dominican Republic. They consists of approximately 56,000 active duty personnel.<sup id="cite_ref-IISS409_163-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-IISS409-163">&#91;162&#93;</a></sup> The President of the Dominican Republic is the <a href="/wiki/Commander_in_chief" class="mw-redirect" title="Commander in chief">commander in chief</a> of the Armed Forces of the Dominican Republic and the Ministry of Defense is the chief managing body of the armed forces.
</p><p>The <a href="/wiki/Dominican_Army" title="Dominican Army">Army</a>, with 28,750 active duty personnel,<sup id="cite_ref-IISS409_163-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-IISS409-163">&#91;162&#93;</a></sup> consists of six <a href="/wiki/Infantry" title="Infantry">infantry</a> brigades, an air cavalry squadron and a combat service support brigade. The <a href="/wiki/Dominican_Air_Force" title="Dominican Air Force">Air Force</a> operates two main bases, one in southern region near <a href="/wiki/Santo_Domingo" title="Santo Domingo">Santo Domingo</a> and one in the northern region of the country, the air force operates approximately 75 aircraft including helicopters. The <a href="/wiki/Dominican_Navy" title="Dominican Navy">Navy</a> operates two major naval bases, one in Santo Domingo and one in Las Calderas on the southwestern coast.
</p><p>The armed forces have organized a Specialized Airport Security Corps (CESA) and a Specialized Port Security Corps (CESEP) to meet international security needs in these areas. The secretary of the armed forces has also announced plans to form a specialized border corps (CESEF). The armed forces provide 75% of personnel to the National Investigations Directorate (DNI) and the Counter-Drug Directorate (DNCD).<sup id="cite_ref-ussdnote_143-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ussdnote-143">&#91;142&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 2018, Dominican Republic signed the UN <a href="/wiki/Treaty_on_the_Prohibition_of_Nuclear_Weapons" title="Treaty on the Prohibition of Nuclear Weapons">treaty on the Prohibition of Nuclear Weapons</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-164" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-164">&#91;163&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Administrative_divisions">Administrative divisions</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main articles: <a href="/wiki/Provinces_of_the_Dominican_Republic" title="Provinces of the Dominican Republic">Provinces of the Dominican Republic</a> and <a href="/wiki/Municipalities_of_the_Dominican_Republic" class="mw-redirect" title="Municipalities of the Dominican Republic">Municipalities of the Dominican Republic</a></div>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:302px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Dominican_Republic,_administrative_divisions_-_de_-_monochrome.svg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2b/Dominican_Republic%2C_administrative_divisions_-_de_-_monochrome.svg/300px-Dominican_Republic%2C_administrative_divisions_-_de_-_monochrome.svg.png" decoding="async" width="300" height="244" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2b/Dominican_Republic%2C_administrative_divisions_-_de_-_monochrome.svg/450px-Dominican_Republic%2C_administrative_divisions_-_de_-_monochrome.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2b/Dominican_Republic%2C_administrative_divisions_-_de_-_monochrome.svg/600px-Dominican_Republic%2C_administrative_divisions_-_de_-_monochrome.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="1400" data-file-height="1139" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Dominican_Republic,_administrative_divisions_-_de_-_monochrome.svg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Provinces of the Dominican Republic</div></div></div>
<p>The Dominican Republic is divided into 31 <a href="/wiki/Provinces_of_the_Dominican_Republic" title="Provinces of the Dominican Republic">provinces</a>. Santo Domingo, the capital, is designated <a href="/wiki/Distrito_Nacional" title="Distrito Nacional">Distrito Nacional</a> (National District). The provinces are divided into municipalities (<i><a href="/wiki/Municipio" title="Municipio">municipios</a></i>; singular <i>municipio</i>). They are the second-level political and <a href="/wiki/Administrative_division" title="Administrative division">administrative subdivisions</a> of the country. The president appoints the governors of the 31 provinces. Mayors and municipal councils administer the 124 municipal districts and the National District (Santo Domingo). They are elected at the same time as congressional representatives.<sup id="cite_ref-ussdnote_143-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ussdnote-143">&#91;142&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The provinces are the first–level <a href="/wiki/Administrative_division" title="Administrative division">administrative subdivisions</a> of the country. The headquarters of the central government's regional offices are normally found in the capital cities of provinces. The president appoints an administrative governor (<i>Gobernador Civil</i>) for each province but not for the Distrito Nacional (Title IX of the constitution).<sup id="cite_ref-Constitution_165-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Constitution-165">&#91;164&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:302px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Ciudad_de_Santo_Domingo_de_Guzm%C3%A1n_-_Distrito_Nacional.png" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/56/Ciudad_de_Santo_Domingo_de_Guzm%C3%A1n_-_Distrito_Nacional.png/300px-Ciudad_de_Santo_Domingo_de_Guzm%C3%A1n_-_Distrito_Nacional.png" decoding="async" width="300" height="117" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/56/Ciudad_de_Santo_Domingo_de_Guzm%C3%A1n_-_Distrito_Nacional.png/450px-Ciudad_de_Santo_Domingo_de_Guzm%C3%A1n_-_Distrito_Nacional.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/56/Ciudad_de_Santo_Domingo_de_Guzm%C3%A1n_-_Distrito_Nacional.png/600px-Ciudad_de_Santo_Domingo_de_Guzm%C3%A1n_-_Distrito_Nacional.png 2x" data-file-width="1285" data-file-height="502" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Ciudad_de_Santo_Domingo_de_Guzm%C3%A1n_-_Distrito_Nacional.png" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Santo Domingo, Distrito Nacional.</div></div></div>
<p>The Distrito Nacional was created in 1936. Prior to this, the Distrito National was the old Santo Domingo Province, in existence since the country's independence in 1844. It is not to be confused with the new Santo Domingo Province split off from it in 2001. While it is similar to a province in many ways, the Distrito Nacional differs in its lack of an administrative governor and consisting only of one municipality, <a href="/wiki/Santo_Domingo" title="Santo Domingo">Santo Domingo</a>, the city council (<i><a href="/wiki/Ayuntamiento" title="Ayuntamiento">ayuntamiento</a></i>) and mayor (<i>síndico</i>) which are in charge of its administration.<sup id="cite_ref-166" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-166">&#91;165&#93;</a></sup>
</p>

<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Economy">Economy</span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Economy_of_the_Dominican_Republic" title="Economy of the Dominican Republic">Economy of the Dominican Republic</a></div>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:292px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Dominican_Republic_Product_Exports_(2019).svg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/95/Dominican_Republic_Product_Exports_%282019%29.svg/290px-Dominican_Republic_Product_Exports_%282019%29.svg.png" decoding="async" width="290" height="189" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/95/Dominican_Republic_Product_Exports_%282019%29.svg/435px-Dominican_Republic_Product_Exports_%282019%29.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/95/Dominican_Republic_Product_Exports_%282019%29.svg/580px-Dominican_Republic_Product_Exports_%282019%29.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="1100" data-file-height="715" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Dominican_Republic_Product_Exports_(2019).svg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>A proportional representation of Dominican Republic exports, 2019</div></div></div>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:GDP_per_capita_development_in_Haiti_and_Dominican_Republic.svg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/59/GDP_per_capita_development_in_Haiti_and_Dominican_Republic.svg/220px-GDP_per_capita_development_in_Haiti_and_Dominican_Republic.svg.png" decoding="async" width="220" height="155" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/59/GDP_per_capita_development_in_Haiti_and_Dominican_Republic.svg/330px-GDP_per_capita_development_in_Haiti_and_Dominican_Republic.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/59/GDP_per_capita_development_in_Haiti_and_Dominican_Republic.svg/440px-GDP_per_capita_development_in_Haiti_and_Dominican_Republic.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="850" data-file-height="600" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:GDP_per_capita_development_in_Haiti_and_Dominican_Republic.svg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Historical GDP per capita development in the Dominican Republic and Haiti</div></div></div>
<p>During the last three decades, the Dominican economy, formerly dependent on the export of agricultural commodities (mainly sugar, cocoa and coffee), has transitioned to a diversified mix of services, manufacturing, agriculture, mining, and trade. The service sector accounts for almost 60% of GDP; manufacturing, for 22%; tourism, telecommunications and finance are the main components of the service sector; however, none of them accounts for more than 10% of the whole.<sup id="cite_ref-167" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-167">&#91;166&#93;</a></sup> The Dominican Republic has a stock market, <a href="/wiki/Bolsa_de_Valores_de_la_Rep%C3%BAblica_Dominicana" title="Bolsa de Valores de la República Dominicana">Bolsa de Valores de la República Dominicana</a> (BVRD).<sup id="cite_ref-168" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-168">&#91;167&#93;</a></sup> and advanced telecommunication system and transportation infrastructure.<sup id="cite_ref-consulate_41-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-consulate-41">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup> High unemployment and income inequality are long-term challenges.<sup id="cite_ref-CIADemo_4-21" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CIADemo-4">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> International migration affects the Dominican Republic greatly, as it receives and sends large flows of migrants. Mass illegal Haitian immigration and the integration of Dominicans of Haitian descent are major issues.<sup id="cite_ref-pinadep_169-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-pinadep-169">&#91;168&#93;</a></sup> A large Dominican diaspora exists, mostly <a href="/wiki/Dominican_Americans_(Dominican_Republic)" class="mw-redirect" title="Dominican Americans (Dominican Republic)">in the United States</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-s0201_170-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-s0201-170">&#91;169&#93;</a></sup> contributes to development, sending billions of dollars to Dominican families in remittances.<sup id="cite_ref-CIADemo_4-22" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CIADemo-4">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-ussdnote_143-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ussdnote-143">&#91;142&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Remittance" title="Remittance">Remittances</a> in Dominican Republic increased to US$4571.30 million in 2014 from US$3333 million in 2013 (according to data reported by the Inter-American Development Bank). Economic growth takes place in spite of a chronic energy shortage,<sup id="cite_ref-171" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-171">&#91;170&#93;</a></sup> which causes frequent blackouts and very high prices. Despite a widening merchandise <a href="/wiki/Balance_of_trade" title="Balance of trade">trade deficit</a>, tourism earnings and remittances have helped build <a href="/wiki/Foreign_exchange_reserves" title="Foreign exchange reserves">foreign exchange reserves</a>. Following economic turmoil in the late 1980s and 1990, during which the gross domestic product (GDP) fell by up to 5% and consumer price inflation reached an unprecedented 100%, the Dominican Republic entered a period of growth and declining inflation until 2002, after which the economy entered a <a href="/wiki/Recession" title="Recession">recession</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-ussdnote_143-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ussdnote-143">&#91;142&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>This recession followed the collapse of the second-largest <a href="/wiki/Commercial_bank" title="Commercial bank">commercial bank</a> in the country, <a href="/wiki/Banco_Intercontinental" title="Banco Intercontinental">Baninter</a>, linked to a major incident of fraud valued at US$3.5 billion. The Baninter fraud had a devastating effect on the Dominican economy, with GDP dropping by 1% in 2003 as inflation ballooned by over 27%. All defendants, including the star of the trial, <a href="/wiki/Ram%C3%B3n_B%C3%A1ez_Figueroa" title="Ramón Báez Figueroa">Ramón Báez Figueroa</a> (the great-grandson of President <a href="/wiki/Buenaventura_B%C3%A1ez" title="Buenaventura Báez">Buenaventura Báez</a>),<sup id="cite_ref-NYTimes_TonySmith_172-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-NYTimes_TonySmith-172">&#91;171&#93;</a></sup> were convicted.
</p><p>According to the 2005 Annual Report of the United Nations Subcommittee on Human Development in the Dominican Republic, the country is ranked No. 71 in the world for resource availability, No. 79 for human development, and No. 14 in the world for resource mismanagement. These statistics emphasize national government corruption, foreign economic interference in the country, and the rift between the rich and poor.
</p><p>The Dominican Republic has a noted problem of <a href="/wiki/Child_labor" class="mw-redirect" title="Child labor">child labor</a> in its coffee, rice, sugarcane, and tomato industries.<sup id="cite_ref-173" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-173">&#91;172&#93;</a></sup> The labor injustices in the sugarcane industry extend to forced labor according to the <a href="/wiki/United_States_Department_of_Labor" title="United States Department of Labor">U.S. Department of Labor</a>. Three large groups own 75% of the land: the State Sugar Council (Consejo Estatal del Azúcar, CEA), <a href="/wiki/Vicini" title="Vicini">Grupo Vicini</a>, and Central Romana Corporation.<sup id="cite_ref-Country_Studies-Library_Congress_174-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Country_Studies-Library_Congress-174">&#91;173&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>According to the 2016 <a href="/wiki/Global_Slavery_Index" title="Global Slavery Index">Global Slavery Index</a>, an estimated 104,800 people are enslaved in the modern day Dominican Republic, or 1.00% of the population.<sup id="cite_ref-175" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-175">&#91;174&#93;</a></sup> Some slaves in the Dominican Republic are held on sugar plantations, guarded by men on horseback with rifles, and forced to work.<sup id="cite_ref-176" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-176">&#91;175&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-177" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-177">&#91;176&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:252px;"><a href="/wiki/File:SantoDomingoedit.JPG" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e3/SantoDomingoedit.JPG/250px-SantoDomingoedit.JPG" decoding="async" width="250" height="140" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e3/SantoDomingoedit.JPG/375px-SantoDomingoedit.JPG 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e3/SantoDomingoedit.JPG/500px-SantoDomingoedit.JPG 2x" data-file-width="2359" data-file-height="1325" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:SantoDomingoedit.JPG" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>View of <a href="/wiki/Santo_Domingo" title="Santo Domingo">Santo Domingo</a>, the Dominican Republic's capital city.</div></div></div>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Currency">Currency</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Dominican_peso" title="Dominican peso">Dominican peso</a></div>
<p>The Dominican peso (abbreviated $ or RD$; <a href="/wiki/ISO_4217" title="ISO 4217">ISO 4217</a> code is "DOP")<sup id="cite_ref-XE_178-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-XE-178">&#91;177&#93;</a></sup> is the national currency, with the <a href="/wiki/United_States_dollar" title="United States dollar">United States dollar</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Euro" title="Euro">Euro</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Canadian_dollar" title="Canadian dollar">Canadian dollar</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Swiss_franc" title="Swiss franc">Swiss franc</a> also accepted at most tourist sites. The exchange rate to the U.S. dollar, liberalized by 1985, stood at 2.70 pesos per dollar in August 1986,<sup id="cite_ref-pons_84-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-pons-84">&#91;83&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: p417, 428">&#58;&#8202;p417,&#8202;428&#8202;</span></sup> 14.00 pesos in 1993, and 16.00 pesos in 2000. As of September&#160;2018<sup class="plainlinks noexcerpt noprint asof-tag update" style="display:none;"><a class="external text" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Dominican_Republic&amp;action=edit">&#91;update&#93;</a></sup> the rate was 50.08 pesos per dollar.<sup id="cite_ref-179" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-179">&#91;178&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Tourism">Tourism</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Tourism_in_the_Dominican_Republic" title="Tourism in the Dominican Republic">Tourism in the Dominican Republic</a></div>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:252px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Punta_Cana_Beach_Cabeza_De_Toro_1.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/70/Punta_Cana_Beach_Cabeza_De_Toro_1.jpg/250px-Punta_Cana_Beach_Cabeza_De_Toro_1.jpg" decoding="async" width="250" height="167" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/70/Punta_Cana_Beach_Cabeza_De_Toro_1.jpg/375px-Punta_Cana_Beach_Cabeza_De_Toro_1.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/70/Punta_Cana_Beach_Cabeza_De_Toro_1.jpg/500px-Punta_Cana_Beach_Cabeza_De_Toro_1.jpg 2x" data-file-width="3456" data-file-height="2304" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Punta_Cana_Beach_Cabeza_De_Toro_1.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Cabeza de Toro beach, <a href="/wiki/Punta_Cana" title="Punta Cana">Punta Cana</a></div></div></div>
<p>Tourism is one of the fueling factors in the Dominican Republic's economic growth. The Dominican Republic is the most popular tourist destination in the <a href="/wiki/Caribbean" title="Caribbean">Caribbean</a>. With the construction of projects like <a href="/wiki/Cap_Cana" class="mw-redirect" title="Cap Cana">Cap Cana</a>, San Souci Port in Santo Domingo, <a href="/wiki/Casa_de_Campo,_Dominican_Republic" title="Casa de Campo, Dominican Republic">Casa De Campo</a> and the Hard Rock Hotel &amp; Casino (ancient Moon Palace Resort) in <a href="/wiki/Punta_Cana" title="Punta Cana">Punta Cana</a>, the Dominican Republic expects increased tourism activity in the upcoming years.
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Ecotourism" title="Ecotourism">Ecotourism</a> has also been a topic increasingly important in this nation, with towns like <a href="/wiki/Jarabacoa" title="Jarabacoa">Jarabacoa</a> and neighboring <a href="/wiki/Constanza,_Dominican_Republic" title="Constanza, Dominican Republic">Constanza</a>, and locations like the <a href="/wiki/Pico_Duarte" title="Pico Duarte">Pico Duarte</a>, <a href="/wiki/Bahia_de_las_Aguilas" title="Bahia de las Aguilas">Bahia de las Aguilas</a>, and others becoming more significant in efforts to increase direct benefits from tourism. Most residents from other countries <a href="/wiki/Visa_policy_of_the_Dominican_Republic" title="Visa policy of the Dominican Republic">are required to get a tourist card</a>, depending on the country they live in. In the last 10 years the Dominican Republic has become one of the world's notably progressive states in terms of recycling and waste disposal. A UN report cited there was a 221.3% efficiency increase in the previous 10 years due, in part, to the opening of the largest open air landfill site located in the north 10&#160;km from the Haitian border.
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Infrastructure">Infrastructure</span></h2>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Gualey_Santo_Domingo_Dominican_Republic.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8b/Gualey_Santo_Domingo_Dominican_Republic.jpg/220px-Gualey_Santo_Domingo_Dominican_Republic.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="176" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8b/Gualey_Santo_Domingo_Dominican_Republic.jpg/330px-Gualey_Santo_Domingo_Dominican_Republic.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8b/Gualey_Santo_Domingo_Dominican_Republic.jpg/440px-Gualey_Santo_Domingo_Dominican_Republic.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1300" data-file-height="1040" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Gualey_Santo_Domingo_Dominican_Republic.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div><a href="/wiki/Telef%C3%A9rico_de_Santo_Domingo" title="Teleférico de Santo Domingo">Teleférico de Santo Domingo</a></div></div></div>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Transportation">Transportation</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Transportation_in_the_Dominican_Republic" class="mw-redirect" title="Transportation in the Dominican Republic">Transportation in the Dominican Republic</a></div>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:282px;"><a href="/wiki/File:27_de_febrero_av._Santo_Domingo.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e8/27_de_febrero_av._Santo_Domingo.jpg/280px-27_de_febrero_av._Santo_Domingo.jpg" decoding="async" width="280" height="210" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e8/27_de_febrero_av._Santo_Domingo.jpg/420px-27_de_febrero_av._Santo_Domingo.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e8/27_de_febrero_av._Santo_Domingo.jpg/560px-27_de_febrero_av._Santo_Domingo.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1280" data-file-height="960" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:27_de_febrero_av._Santo_Domingo.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>27 de Febrero Avenue in <a href="/wiki/Santo_Domingo" title="Santo Domingo">Santo Domingo</a>.</div></div></div>
<p>The country has three national trunk highways, which connect every major town. These are <a href="/wiki/DR-1" title="DR-1">DR-1</a>, <a href="/wiki/DR-2" title="DR-2">DR-2</a>, and <a href="/wiki/DR-3" title="DR-3">DR-3</a>, which depart from Santo Domingo toward the northern (Cibao), southwestern (Sur), and eastern (El Este) parts of the country respectively. These highways have been consistently improved with the expansion and reconstruction of many sections. Two other national highways serve as spur (<a href="/wiki/DR-5" title="DR-5">DR-5</a>) or alternative routes (<a href="/wiki/DR-4" title="DR-4">DR-4</a>).
</p><p>In addition to the national highways, the government has embarked on an expansive reconstruction of spur secondary routes, which connect smaller towns to the trunk routes. In the last few years the government constructed a 106-kilometer toll road that connects Santo Domingo with the country's northeastern peninsula. Travelers may now arrive in the Samaná Peninsula in less than two hours. Other additions are the reconstruction of the DR-28 (Jarabacoa&#160;– Constanza) and <a href="/wiki/DR-12" title="DR-12">DR-12</a> (Constanza&#160;– Bonao). Despite these efforts, many secondary routes still remain either unpaved or in need of maintenance. There is currently a nationwide program to pave these and other commonly used routes. Also, the <a href="/wiki/Santiago_light_rail" class="mw-redirect" title="Santiago light rail">Santiago light rail</a> system is in planning stages but currently on hold.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Bus_services">Bus services</span></h3>
<p>There are two main bus transportation services in the Dominican Republic: one controlled by the government, through the Oficina Técnica de Transito Terrestre (OTTT) and the Oficina Metropolitana de Servicios de Autobuses (OMSA), and the other controlled by private business, among them, Federación Nacional de Transporte La Nueva Opción (FENATRANO) and the Confederacion Nacional de Transporte (CONATRA). The government transportation system covers large routes in metropolitan areas such as Santo Domingo and Santiago.
</p><p>There are many privately owned bus companies, such as Metro Servicios Turísticos and Caribe Tours, that run daily routes.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Santo_Domingo_Metro">Santo Domingo Metro</span></h3>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Stodgo_metro.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d9/Stodgo_metro.jpg/220px-Stodgo_metro.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="122" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d9/Stodgo_metro.jpg/330px-Stodgo_metro.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d9/Stodgo_metro.jpg/440px-Stodgo_metro.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1024" data-file-height="570" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Stodgo_metro.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>A pair of 9000 series are tested on the Santo Domingo Metro.</div></div></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Santo_Domingo_Metro" title="Santo Domingo Metro">Santo Domingo Metro</a></div>
<p>The Dominican Republic has a <a href="/wiki/Rapid_transit" title="Rapid transit">rapid transit</a> system in <a href="/wiki/Santo_Domingo" title="Santo Domingo">Santo Domingo</a>, the country's capital. It is the most extensive metro system in the insular <a href="/wiki/Caribbean" title="Caribbean">Caribbean</a> and Central American region by length and number of stations. The Santo Domingo Metro is part of a major "National Master Plan" to improve transportation in Santo Domingo as well as the rest of the nation. The first line was planned to relieve traffic congestion in the <a href="/wiki/DR-1" title="DR-1">Máximo Gómez</a> and <a href="/wiki/DR-13" title="DR-13">Hermanas Mirabal Avenue</a>. The second line, which opened in April 2013, is meant to relieve the congestion along the <a href="/wiki/DR-13" title="DR-13">Duarte-Kennedy-Centenario</a> Corridor in the city from west to east. The current length of the Metro, with the sections of the two lines open as of August&#160;2013<sup class="plainlinks noexcerpt noprint asof-tag update" style="display:none;"><a class="external text" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Dominican_Republic&amp;action=edit">&#91;update&#93;</a></sup>, is 27.35 kilometres (16.99&#160;mi). Before the opening of the second line, 30,856,515 passengers rode the Santo Domingo Metro in 2012.<sup id="cite_ref-SD-stats_180-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-SD-stats-180">&#91;179&#93;</a></sup> With both lines opened, ridership increased to 61,270,054 passengers in 2014.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Communications">Communications</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Telecommunications_in_the_Dominican_Republic" title="Telecommunications in the Dominican Republic">Telecommunications in the Dominican Republic</a></div>
<p>The Dominican Republic has a well developed <a href="/wiki/Telecommunications_in_the_Dominican_Republic" title="Telecommunications in the Dominican Republic">telecommunications</a> infrastructure, with extensive mobile phone and <a href="/wiki/Landline" title="Landline">landline</a> services. <a href="/wiki/Cable_Internet_access" title="Cable Internet access">Cable Internet</a> and <a href="/wiki/Digital_subscriber_line" title="Digital subscriber line">DSL</a> are available in most parts of the country, and many <a href="/wiki/Internet_service_provider" title="Internet service provider">Internet service providers</a> offer <a href="/wiki/3G" title="3G">3G</a> wireless internet service. The Dominican Republic became the second country in Latin America to have 4G LTE wireless service. The reported speeds are from 1 Mbit/s up to 100 Mbit/s for residential services.
</p><p>For commercial service there are speeds from 256 kbit/s up to 154 Mbit/s. (Each set of numbers denotes <a href="/wiki/Cable_Internet_access" title="Cable Internet access">downstream/upstream speed</a>; that is, to the user/from the user.) Projects to extend <a href="/wiki/Wi-Fi" title="Wi-Fi">Wi-Fi</a> hot spots have been made in Santo Domingo. The country's commercial radio stations and television stations are in the process of transferring to the <a href="/wiki/Data_transmission" class="mw-redirect" title="Data transmission">digital spectrum</a>, via <a href="/wiki/HD_Radio" title="HD Radio">HD Radio</a> and <a href="/wiki/High-definition_television" title="High-definition television">HDTV</a> after officially adopting <a href="/wiki/Advanced_Television_Systems_Committee_standards" class="mw-redirect" title="Advanced Television Systems Committee standards">ATSC</a> as the digital medium in the country with a switch-off of analog transmission by September 2015. The telecommunications regulator in the country is INDOTEL (<i>Instituto Dominicano de Telecomunicaciones</i>).
</p><p>The largest telecommunications company is <a href="/wiki/Claro_Rep%C3%BAblica_Dominicana" class="mw-redirect" title="Claro República Dominicana">Claro</a>&#160;– part of <a href="/wiki/Carlos_Slim" title="Carlos Slim">Carlos Slim</a>'s <a href="/wiki/Am%C3%A9rica_M%C3%B3vil" title="América Móvil">América Móvil</a>&#160;– which provides wireless, landline, broadband, and <a href="/wiki/IPTV" class="mw-redirect" title="IPTV">IPTV</a> services. In June 2009 there were more than 8 million phone line subscribers (land and cell users) in the D.R., representing 81% of the country's population and a fivefold increase since the year 2000, when there were 1.6 million. The communications sector generates about 3.0% of the GDP.<sup id="cite_ref-LD2009-06-05_181-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-LD2009-06-05-181">&#91;180&#93;</a></sup> There were 2,439,997 Internet users in March 2009.<sup id="cite_ref-182" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-182">&#91;181&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In November 2009, the Dominican Republic became the first Latin American country to pledge to include a "gender perspective" in every information and communications technology (ICT) initiative and policy developed by the government.<sup id="cite_ref-183" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-183">&#91;182&#93;</a></sup> This is part of the regional <a href="/wiki/ELAC_Action_Plans" title="ELAC Action Plans">eLAC2010</a> plan. The tool the Dominicans have chosen to design and evaluate all the public policies is the APC <a href="/wiki/Gender_Evaluation_Methodology_(GEM)" class="mw-redirect" title="Gender Evaluation Methodology (GEM)">Gender Evaluation Methodology (GEM)</a>.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Electricity">Electricity</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Electricity_sector_in_the_Dominican_Republic" title="Electricity sector in the Dominican Republic">Electricity sector in the Dominican Republic</a></div>
<p>Electric power service has been unreliable since the Trujillo era, and as much as 75% of the equipment is that old. The country's antiquated power grid causes transmission losses that account for a large share of billed electricity from generators. The privatization of the sector started under a previous administration of Leonel Fernández.<sup id="cite_ref-coha_142-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-coha-142">&#91;141&#93;</a></sup> The recent investment in a 345 kilovolt "Santo Domingo–Santiago Electrical Highway"<sup id="cite_ref-DT2009-04-29_184-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-DT2009-04-29-184">&#91;183&#93;</a></sup> with reduced <a href="/wiki/Electric_power_transmission#Losses" title="Electric power transmission">transmission losses</a>, is being heralded as a major capital improvement to the national grid since the mid-1960s.
</p><p>During the Trujillo regime electrical service was introduced to many cities. Almost 95% of usage was not billed at all. Around half of the Dominican Republic's 2.1 million houses have no meters and most do not pay or pay a fixed monthly rate for their electric service.<sup id="cite_ref-DT2006-06-01_185-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-DT2006-06-01-185">&#91;184&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Household and general electrical service is delivered at 110 <a href="/wiki/Volt" title="Volt">volts</a> alternating at 60&#160;<a href="/wiki/Hertz" title="Hertz">Hz</a>. Electrically powered items from the United States work with no modifications. The majority of the Dominican Republic has access to electricity. Tourist areas tend to have more reliable power, as do business, travel, healthcare, and vital infrastructure.<sup id="cite_ref-186" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-186">&#91;185&#93;</a></sup> Concentrated efforts were announced to increase efficiency of delivery to places where the collection rate reached 70%.<sup id="cite_ref-LD2007-04-11_187-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-LD2007-04-11-187">&#91;186&#93;</a></sup> The electricity sector is highly politicized. Some generating companies are undercapitalized and at times unable to purchase adequate fuel supplies.<sup id="cite_ref-ussdnote_143-10" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ussdnote-143">&#91;142&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Society">Society</span></h2>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Demographics">Demographics</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Demographics_of_the_Dominican_Republic" title="Demographics of the Dominican Republic">Demographics of the Dominican Republic</a></div>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:352px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Dominican_Rep_demography.png" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c7/Dominican_Rep_demography.png/350px-Dominican_Rep_demography.png" decoding="async" width="350" height="206" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c7/Dominican_Rep_demography.png/525px-Dominican_Rep_demography.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c7/Dominican_Rep_demography.png/700px-Dominican_Rep_demography.png 2x" data-file-width="725" data-file-height="426" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Dominican_Rep_demography.png" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>The Dominican Republic's population (1961–2003)</div></div></div>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:302px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Dominican_Republic_single_age_population_pyramid_2020.png" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7c/Dominican_Republic_single_age_population_pyramid_2020.png/300px-Dominican_Republic_single_age_population_pyramid_2020.png" decoding="async" width="300" height="241" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7c/Dominican_Republic_single_age_population_pyramid_2020.png/450px-Dominican_Republic_single_age_population_pyramid_2020.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7c/Dominican_Republic_single_age_population_pyramid_2020.png/600px-Dominican_Republic_single_age_population_pyramid_2020.png 2x" data-file-width="2212" data-file-height="1777" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Dominican_Republic_single_age_population_pyramid_2020.png" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Population pyramid in 2020</div></div></div>
<p>The Dominican Republic's population was 11,117,873 in 2021.<sup id="cite_ref-UN_WPP_188-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-UN_WPP-188">&#91;187&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-UN_WPP_2022_189-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-UN_WPP_2022-189">&#91;188&#93;</a></sup> In 2010, 31.2% of the population was under 15 years of age, with 6% of the population over 65 years of age.<sup id="cite_ref-WPP_2012_190-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-WPP_2012-190">&#91;189&#93;</a></sup> There were an estimated 102.3 males for every 100 females in 2020.<sup id="cite_ref-CIADemo_4-23" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CIADemo-4">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> The annual population growth rate for 2006–2007 was 1.5%, with the projected population for the year 2015 being 10,121,000.<sup id="cite_ref-esa_191-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-esa-191">&#91;190&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The population density in 2007 was 192 per km<sup>2</sup> (498 per sq mi), and 63% of the population lived in urban areas.<sup id="cite_ref-192" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-192">&#91;191&#93;</a></sup> The southern coastal plains and the Cibao Valley are the most densely populated areas of the country. The capital city Santo Domingo had a population of 2,907,100 in 2010.<sup id="cite_ref-nationsencyclopedia.com_193-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-nationsencyclopedia.com-193">&#91;192&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Other important cities are <a href="/wiki/Santiago_de_los_Caballeros" title="Santiago de los Caballeros">Santiago de los Caballeros</a> (<abbr title="population">pop.</abbr> 745,293), <a href="/wiki/La_Romana,_Dominican_Republic" title="La Romana, Dominican Republic">La Romana</a> (pop. 214,109), <a href="/wiki/San_Pedro_de_Macor%C3%ADs" title="San Pedro de Macorís">San Pedro de Macorís</a> (pop. 185,255), <a href="/wiki/Hig%C3%BCey,_Dominican_Republic" class="mw-redirect" title="Higüey, Dominican Republic">Higüey</a> (153,174), <a href="/wiki/San_Francisco_de_Macor%C3%ADs" title="San Francisco de Macorís">San Francisco de Macorís</a> (pop. 132,725), <a href="/wiki/San_Felipe_de_Puerto_Plata" class="mw-redirect" title="San Felipe de Puerto Plata">Puerto Plata</a> (pop. 118,282), and <a href="/wiki/Concepci%C3%B3n_de_la_Vega" class="mw-redirect" title="Concepción de la Vega">La Vega</a> (pop. 104,536). Per the United Nations, the urban population growth rate for 2000–2005 was 2.3%.<sup id="cite_ref-nationsencyclopedia.com_193-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-nationsencyclopedia.com-193">&#91;192&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Ethnic_groups">Ethnic groups</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/People_of_the_Dominican_Republic" title="People of the Dominican Republic">People of the Dominican Republic</a></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">See also: <a href="/wiki/Mixed_Dominicans" title="Mixed Dominicans">Mixed Dominicans</a>, <a href="/wiki/White_Dominicans" title="White Dominicans">White Dominicans</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Afro-Dominicans" title="Afro-Dominicans">Afro-Dominicans</a></div>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Dominican-people-cibao-1.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/34/Dominican-people-cibao-1.jpg/220px-Dominican-people-cibao-1.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="161" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/34/Dominican-people-cibao-1.jpg/330px-Dominican-people-cibao-1.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/34/Dominican-people-cibao-1.jpg/440px-Dominican-people-cibao-1.jpg 2x" data-file-width="681" data-file-height="498" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Dominican-people-cibao-1.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Dominican Republic people in the town of <a href="/wiki/Moca,_Espaillat" class="mw-redirect" title="Moca, Espaillat">Moca</a></div></div></div>
<p>In a 2014 population survey, 70.4% self-identified as mixed (mestizo/indio<sup id="cite_ref-194" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-194">&#91;b&#93;</a></sup> 58%, <a href="/wiki/Mulatto" title="Mulatto">mulatto</a> 12.4%), 15.8% as <a href="/wiki/Afro-Dominican_(Dominican_Republic)" class="mw-redirect" title="Afro-Dominican (Dominican Republic)">black</a>, 13.5% as <a href="/wiki/White_Dominicans" title="White Dominicans">white</a>, and 0.3% as "other".<sup id="cite_ref-CIADemo_4-24" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CIADemo-4">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Britannica_195-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Britannica-195">&#91;193&#93;</a></sup> Ethnic immigrant groups in the country include <a href="/wiki/Southwest_Asia" class="mw-redirect" title="Southwest Asia">West Asians</a>—mostly <a href="/wiki/Lebanese_people" title="Lebanese people">Lebanese</a>, <a href="/wiki/Syrian_people" class="mw-redirect" title="Syrian people">Syrians</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Palestinian_people" class="mw-redirect" title="Palestinian people">Palestinians</a>; the current president, Luis Abinader, is of Lebanese descent.<sup id="cite_ref-Irrizarri_196-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Irrizarri-196">&#91;194&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Levinson_197-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Levinson-197">&#91;195&#93;</a></sup> East Asians, <a href="/wiki/Koreans" title="Koreans">Koreans</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-migration_198-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-migration-198">&#91;196&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Ethnic_Chinese_in_the_Dominican_Republic" title="Ethnic Chinese in the Dominican Republic">ethnic Chinese</a> and <a href="/wiki/Japanese_settlement_in_the_Dominican_Republic" title="Japanese settlement in the Dominican Republic">Japanese</a>, can also be found.<sup id="cite_ref-Levinson_197-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Levinson-197">&#91;195&#93;</a></sup> Europeans are represented mostly by <a href="/wiki/Spanish_people" class="mw-redirect" title="Spanish people">Spanish</a> whites but also with smaller populations of <a href="/wiki/Germans" title="Germans">Germans</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-migration_198-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-migration-198">&#91;196&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Italian_people" class="mw-redirect" title="Italian people">Italians</a>, <a href="/wiki/French_people" title="French people">French</a>, <a href="/wiki/British_people" title="British people">British</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-199" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-199">&#91;197&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-migration_198-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-migration-198">&#91;196&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Dutch_people" title="Dutch people">Dutch</a>, <a href="/wiki/Swiss_people" title="Swiss people">Swiss</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-migration_198-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-migration-198">&#91;196&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Russians" title="Russians">Russians</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-migration_198-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-migration-198">&#91;196&#93;</a></sup> and <a href="/wiki/Hungarian_people" class="mw-redirect" title="Hungarian people">Hungarians</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Levinson_197-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Levinson-197">&#91;195&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Languages">Languages</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main articles: <a href="/wiki/Dominican_Spanish" title="Dominican Spanish">Dominican Spanish</a> and <a href="/wiki/Saman%C3%A1_English" title="Samaná English">Samaná English</a></div>
<p>The population of the Dominican Republic is mostly <a href="/wiki/Spanish_language" title="Spanish language">Spanish</a>-speaking. The local <a href="/wiki/Variation_(linguistics)" title="Variation (linguistics)">variant</a> of Spanish is called <a href="/wiki/Dominican_Spanish" title="Dominican Spanish">Dominican Spanish</a>, which closely resembles other Spanish <a href="/wiki/Vernacular" title="Vernacular">vernaculars</a> in the Caribbean and has similarities to <a href="/wiki/Canarian_Spanish" title="Canarian Spanish">Canarian Spanish</a>. In addition, it has influences from African languages and borrowed words from <a href="/wiki/Indigenous_languages_of_the_Americas#South_America_and_the_Caribbean" title="Indigenous languages of the Americas">indigenous Caribbean languages</a> particular to the island of Hispaniola.<sup id="cite_ref-200" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-200">&#91;198&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-201" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-201">&#91;199&#93;</a></sup> Schools are based on a Spanish educational model; English and French are mandatory foreign languages in both private and public schools,<sup id="cite_ref-202" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-202">&#91;200&#93;</a></sup><sup class="noprint Inline-Template" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Verifiability" title="Wikipedia:Verifiability"><span title="The material near this tag failed verification of its source citation(s). (August 2022)">failed verification</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> although the quality of foreign languages teaching is poor.<sup id="cite_ref-203" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-203">&#91;201&#93;</a></sup><sup class="noprint Inline-Template noprint noexcerpt Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:NOTRS" class="mw-redirect" title="Wikipedia:NOTRS"><span title="This claim needs references to better sources. (August 2022)">better&#160;source&#160;needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> Some private educational institutes provide teaching in other languages, notably Italian, Japanese and Mandarin.<sup id="cite_ref-204" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-204">&#91;202&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-205" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-205">&#91;203&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Haitian_Creole" title="Haitian Creole">Haitian Creole</a> is the largest minority language in the Dominican Republic and is spoken by <a href="/wiki/Haitians" title="Haitians">Haitian</a> immigrants and their descendants.<sup id="cite_ref-206" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-206">&#91;204&#93;</a></sup> There is a community of a few thousand people whose ancestors spoke <a href="/wiki/Saman%C3%A1_English" title="Samaná English">Samaná English</a> in the <a href="/wiki/Saman%C3%A1_Peninsula" title="Samaná Peninsula">Samaná Peninsula</a>. They are the descendants of formerly enslaved African Americans who arrived in the nineteenth century, but only a few elders speak the language today.<sup id="cite_ref-207" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-207">&#91;205&#93;</a></sup> Tourism, American pop culture, the influence of <a href="/wiki/Dominican_Americans" title="Dominican Americans">Dominican Americans</a>, and the country's economic ties with the United States motivate other Dominicans to learn English. The Dominican Republic is ranked 2nd in Latin America and 23rd in the World on English <a href="/wiki/Language_proficiency" title="Language proficiency">proficiency</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-208" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-208">&#91;206&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-209" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-209">&#91;207&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
