<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Religion">Religion</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Religion_in_the_Dominican_Republic" title="Religion in the Dominican Republic">Religion in the Dominican Republic</a></div>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Bas%C3%ADlica_Menor_de_Santa_Mar%C3%ADa_SD_RD_02_2017_1941.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1b/Bas%C3%ADlica_Menor_de_Santa_Mar%C3%ADa_SD_RD_02_2017_1941.jpg/220px-Bas%C3%ADlica_Menor_de_Santa_Mar%C3%ADa_SD_RD_02_2017_1941.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="145" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1b/Bas%C3%ADlica_Menor_de_Santa_Mar%C3%ADa_SD_RD_02_2017_1941.jpg/330px-Bas%C3%ADlica_Menor_de_Santa_Mar%C3%ADa_SD_RD_02_2017_1941.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1b/Bas%C3%ADlica_Menor_de_Santa_Mar%C3%ADa_SD_RD_02_2017_1941.jpg/440px-Bas%C3%ADlica_Menor_de_Santa_Mar%C3%ADa_SD_RD_02_2017_1941.jpg 2x" data-file-width="4201" data-file-height="2765" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Bas%C3%ADlica_Menor_de_Santa_Mar%C3%ADa_SD_RD_02_2017_1941.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>The Gothic <a href="/wiki/Basilica_Cathedral_of_Santa_Mar%C3%ADa_la_Menor" title="Basilica Cathedral of Santa María la Menor">Cathedral of Santa María la Menor</a>, Santo Domingo, is the oldest cathedral in the Americas, built between 1514 and 1541.</div></div></div>
<p><b>95.0% </b> Christians <br />
<b>2.6% </b> No religion <br />
<b>2.2% </b> Other religions <sup id="cite_ref-212" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-212">&#91;210&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>As of 2014<sup class="plainlinks noexcerpt noprint asof-tag update" style="display:none;"><a class="external text" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Dominican_Republic&amp;action=edit">&#91;update&#93;</a></sup>, 57% of the population (5.7 million) identified themselves as <a href="/wiki/Roman_Catholics" class="mw-redirect" title="Roman Catholics">Roman Catholics</a> and 23% (2.3 million) as <a href="/wiki/Protestants" class="mw-redirect" title="Protestants">Protestants</a> (in Latin American countries, Protestants are often called <i>Evangelicos</i> because they emphasize personal and public evangelising and many are <a href="/wiki/Evangelical_Protestant" class="mw-redirect" title="Evangelical Protestant">Evangelical Protestant</a> or of a <a href="/wiki/Pentecostal" class="mw-redirect" title="Pentecostal">Pentecostal</a> group). From 1896 to 1907 missionaries from the <a href="/wiki/Episcopal_Church_(United_States)" title="Episcopal Church (United States)">Episcopal</a>, <a href="/wiki/Free_Methodist" class="mw-redirect" title="Free Methodist">Free Methodist</a>, <a href="/wiki/Seventh-day_Adventist" class="mw-redirect" title="Seventh-day Adventist">Seventh-day Adventist</a> and <a href="/wiki/Moravians" title="Moravians">Moravians</a> churches began work in the Dominican Republic.<sup id="cite_ref-Religious_Transformations_213-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Religious_Transformations-213">&#91;211&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-214" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-214">&#91;212&#93;</a></sup> Three percent of the 10.63 million Dominican Republic population are Seventh-day Adventists.<sup id="cite_ref-215" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-215">&#91;213&#93;</a></sup> Recent immigration as well as proselytizing efforts have brought in other religious groups, with the following shares of the population: <a href="/wiki/Spiritist" class="mw-redirect" title="Spiritist">Spiritist</a>: 2.2%,<sup id="cite_ref-religiousfreedom_216-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-religiousfreedom-216">&#91;214&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/The_Church_of_Jesus_Christ_of_Latter-day_Saints" title="The Church of Jesus Christ of Latter-day Saints">The Church of Jesus Christ of Latter-day Saints</a>: 1.3%,<sup id="cite_ref-217" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-217">&#91;215&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Buddhism" title="Buddhism">Buddhist</a>: 0.1%, <a href="/wiki/Bah%C3%A1%CA%BC%C3%AD_Faith" title="Baháʼí Faith">Baháʼí</a>: 0.1%,<sup id="cite_ref-religiousfreedom_216-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-religiousfreedom-216">&#91;214&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Chinese_Folk_Religion" class="mw-redirect" title="Chinese Folk Religion">Chinese Folk Religion</a>: 0.1%,<sup id="cite_ref-religiousfreedom_216-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-religiousfreedom-216">&#91;214&#93;</a></sup> Islam: 0.02%, Judaism: 0.01%.
</p><p>The Catholic Church began to lose its strong dominance in the late 19th century. This was due to a lack of funding, priests, and support programs. During the same time, <a href="/wiki/Protestant" class="mw-redirect" title="Protestant">Protestant</a> Evangelicalism began to gain a wider support "with their emphasis on personal responsibility and family rejuvenation, economic entrepreneurship, and biblical <a href="/wiki/Fundamentalism" title="Fundamentalism">fundamentalism</a>".<sup id="cite_ref-218" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-218">&#91;216&#93;</a></sup> The Dominican Republic has two Catholic patroness saints: <i>Nuestra Señora de la Altagracia</i> (Our Lady Of High Grace) and <i>Nuestra Señora de las Mercedes</i> (Our Lady Of Mercy).
</p><p>The Dominican Republic has historically granted extensive religious freedom. According to the <a href="/wiki/United_States_Department_of_State" title="United States Department of State">United States Department of State</a>, "The constitution specifies that there is no state church and provides for freedom of religion and belief. A concordat with the Vatican designates Catholicism as the official religion and extends special privileges to the Catholic Church not granted to other religious groups. These include the legal recognition of church law, use of public funds to underwrite some church expenses, and complete exoneration from customs duties."<sup id="cite_ref-219" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-219">&#91;217&#93;</a></sup> In the 1950s restrictions were placed upon churches by the government of Trujillo. Letters of protest were sent against the mass arrests of government adversaries. Trujillo began a campaign against the Catholic Church and planned to arrest priests and bishops who preached against the government. This campaign ended before it was put into place, with his assassination.
</p><p>During World War II a group of Jews escaping <a href="/wiki/Nazi_Germany" title="Nazi Germany">Nazi Germany</a> fled to the Dominican Republic and founded the city of <a href="/wiki/Sos%C3%BAa" title="Sosúa">Sosúa</a>. It has remained the center of the Jewish population since.<sup id="cite_ref-220" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-220">&#91;218&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="20th_century_immigration">20th century immigration</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main articles: <a href="/wiki/Japanese_settlement_in_the_Dominican_Republic" title="Japanese settlement in the Dominican Republic">Japanese settlement in the Dominican Republic</a>, <a href="/wiki/Ethnic_Chinese_in_the_Dominican_Republic" title="Ethnic Chinese in the Dominican Republic">Ethnic Chinese in the Dominican Republic</a>, and <a href="/wiki/History_of_the_Jews_in_the_Dominican_Republic" title="History of the Jews in the Dominican Republic">History of the Jews in the Dominican Republic</a></div>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Colonia_Japonesa.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5a/Colonia_Japonesa.jpg/220px-Colonia_Japonesa.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="142" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5a/Colonia_Japonesa.jpg/330px-Colonia_Japonesa.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5a/Colonia_Japonesa.jpg/440px-Colonia_Japonesa.jpg 2x" data-file-width="800" data-file-height="516" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Colonia_Japonesa.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Family of <a href="/wiki/Japanese_diaspora" title="Japanese diaspora">Japanese descent</a> in <a href="/wiki/Constanza,_Dominican_Republic" title="Constanza, Dominican Republic">Constanza</a>'s neighbourhood of <a href="/wiki/Japanese_settlement_in_the_Dominican_Republic" title="Japanese settlement in the Dominican Republic">Colonia Japonesa</a></div></div></div>
<p>In the 20th century, many <a href="/wiki/Arab" class="mw-redirect" title="Arab">Arabs</a> (from <a href="/wiki/Lebanon" title="Lebanon">Lebanon</a>, <a href="/wiki/Syria" title="Syria">Syria</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Palestine_(region)" title="Palestine (region)">Palestine</a>),<sup id="cite_ref-Lebanon-migrants-register_221-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Lebanon-migrants-register-221">&#91;219&#93;</a></sup> Japanese, and, to a lesser degree, <a href="/wiki/Koreans" title="Koreans">Koreans</a> settled in the country as agricultural laborers and merchants. The Chinese companies found business in telecom, mining, and railroads. The Arab community is rising at an increasing rate and is estimated at 80,000.<sup id="cite_ref-Lebanon-migrants-register_221-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Lebanon-migrants-register-221">&#91;219&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In addition, there are descendants of immigrants who came from other Caribbean islands, including <a href="/wiki/St._Kitts_and_Nevis" class="mw-redirect" title="St. Kitts and Nevis">St. Kitts and Nevis</a>, <a href="/wiki/Antigua" title="Antigua">Antigua</a>, <a href="/wiki/Saint_Vincent_(island)" class="mw-redirect" title="Saint Vincent (island)">St. Vincent</a>, <a href="/wiki/Montserrat" title="Montserrat">Montserrat</a>, <a href="/wiki/Tortola" title="Tortola">Tortola</a>, <a href="/wiki/Saint_Croix,_U.S._Virgin_Islands" class="mw-redirect" title="Saint Croix, U.S. Virgin Islands">St. Croix</a>, <a href="/wiki/St._Thomas,_U.S._Virgin_Islands" class="mw-redirect" title="St. Thomas, U.S. Virgin Islands">St. Thomas</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Guadeloupe" title="Guadeloupe">Guadeloupe</a>.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (November 2021)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> They worked on sugarcane plantations and docks and settled mainly in the cities of <a href="/wiki/San_Pedro_de_Macor%C3%ADs" title="San Pedro de Macorís">San Pedro de Macorís</a> and <a href="/wiki/Puerto_Plata_(city)" class="mw-redirect" title="Puerto Plata (city)">Puerto Plata</a>. Puerto Rican, and to a lesser extent, Cuban immigrants fled to the Dominican Republic from the mid-1800s until about 1940 due to a poor economy and social unrest in their respective home countries.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (November 2021)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> Many Puerto Rican immigrants settled in <a href="/wiki/Hig%C3%BCey_(city)" class="mw-redirect" title="Higüey (city)">Higüey</a>, among other cities, and quickly assimilated due to similar culture. Before and during World War II, 800 Jewish refugees moved to the Dominican Republic.<sup id="cite_ref-SosuaJewishStudies_222-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-SosuaJewishStudies-222">&#91;220&#93;</a></sup><sup class="noprint Inline-Template" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Verifiability" title="Wikipedia:Verifiability"><span title="The material near this tag failed verification of its source citation(s). (July 2021)">failed verification</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>Numerous immigrants have come from other Caribbean countries, as the country has offered economic opportunities. There is an increasing number of <a href="/wiki/Puerto_Rican_people" class="mw-redirect" title="Puerto Rican people">Puerto Rican</a> immigrants, especially in and around <a href="/wiki/Santo_Domingo" title="Santo Domingo">Santo Domingo</a>; they are believed to number around 10,000.<sup id="cite_ref-223" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-223">&#91;221&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-224" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-224">&#91;222&#93;</a></sup> There are many Haitians and <a href="/wiki/Venezuelans" title="Venezuelans">Venezuelans</a> living in the Dominican Republic illegally.<sup id="cite_ref-CIADemo_4-25" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CIADemo-4">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Haitian_immigration">Haitian immigration</span></h4>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Haitians_in_the_Dominican_Republic" title="Haitians in the Dominican Republic">Haitians in the Dominican Republic</a></div>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Haiti_deforestation.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/15/Haiti_deforestation.jpg/220px-Haiti_deforestation.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="165" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/15/Haiti_deforestation.jpg/330px-Haiti_deforestation.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/15/Haiti_deforestation.jpg/440px-Haiti_deforestation.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2560" data-file-height="1920" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Haiti_deforestation.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>A satellite image of the <a href="/wiki/Dominican_Republic%E2%80%93Haiti_border" title="Dominican Republic–Haiti border">border</a> between the degraded landscape of <a href="/wiki/Haiti" title="Haiti">Haiti</a> (left) and the Dominican Republic (right), highlighting the <a href="/wiki/Deforestation_in_Haiti" title="Deforestation in Haiti">deforestation on the Haitian side</a></div></div></div>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Dominicans_and_Haitians_Braving_the_Weather.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3f/Dominicans_and_Haitians_Braving_the_Weather.jpg/220px-Dominicans_and_Haitians_Braving_the_Weather.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="147" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3f/Dominicans_and_Haitians_Braving_the_Weather.jpg/330px-Dominicans_and_Haitians_Braving_the_Weather.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3f/Dominicans_and_Haitians_Braving_the_Weather.jpg/440px-Dominicans_and_Haitians_Braving_the_Weather.jpg 2x" data-file-width="640" data-file-height="427" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Dominicans_and_Haitians_Braving_the_Weather.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Dominicans and Haitians lined up to attend medical providers from the <a href="/wiki/U.S._Army_Reserve" class="mw-redirect" title="U.S. Army Reserve">U.S. Army Reserve</a></div></div></div>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Haitian-Dominican_border_in_Grand-Bois,_Cornillon,_Haiti,_June_2017_3.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/86/Haitian-Dominican_border_in_Grand-Bois%2C_Cornillon%2C_Haiti%2C_June_2017_3.jpg/220px-Haitian-Dominican_border_in_Grand-Bois%2C_Cornillon%2C_Haiti%2C_June_2017_3.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="165" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/86/Haitian-Dominican_border_in_Grand-Bois%2C_Cornillon%2C_Haiti%2C_June_2017_3.jpg/330px-Haitian-Dominican_border_in_Grand-Bois%2C_Cornillon%2C_Haiti%2C_June_2017_3.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/86/Haitian-Dominican_border_in_Grand-Bois%2C_Cornillon%2C_Haiti%2C_June_2017_3.jpg/440px-Haitian-Dominican_border_in_Grand-Bois%2C_Cornillon%2C_Haiti%2C_June_2017_3.jpg 2x" data-file-width="3264" data-file-height="2448" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Haitian-Dominican_border_in_Grand-Bois,_Cornillon,_Haiti,_June_2017_3.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>View of border region between the Dominican Republic and Haiti. The border runs horizontally through the middle of the picture.</div></div></div>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:One_Too_Many;_50%2B_Haitian_Workers_In_Transit.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e2/One_Too_Many%3B_50%2B_Haitian_Workers_In_Transit.jpg/220px-One_Too_Many%3B_50%2B_Haitian_Workers_In_Transit.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="165" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e2/One_Too_Many%3B_50%2B_Haitian_Workers_In_Transit.jpg/330px-One_Too_Many%3B_50%2B_Haitian_Workers_In_Transit.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e2/One_Too_Many%3B_50%2B_Haitian_Workers_In_Transit.jpg/440px-One_Too_Many%3B_50%2B_Haitian_Workers_In_Transit.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1600" data-file-height="1200" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:One_Too_Many;_50%2B_Haitian_Workers_In_Transit.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Haitian workers being transported in <a href="/wiki/Punta_Cana" title="Punta Cana">Punta Cana</a>, the Dominican Republic</div></div></div>
<p><a href="/wiki/Human_Rights_Watch" title="Human Rights Watch">Human Rights Watch</a> estimated that 70,000 documented Haitian immigrants and 1,930,000 undocumented immigrants were living in Dominican Republic.
</p><p>Haiti is the neighboring nation to the Dominican Republic and is considerably poorer, less developed and is additionally the least developed country in the western hemisphere. In 2003, 80% of all Haitians were poor (54% living in abject poverty) and 47.1% were illiterate. The country of nine million people also has a fast growing population, but over two-thirds of the labor force lack formal jobs. Haiti's per capita GDP (PPP) was $1,800 in 2017, or just over one-tenth of the Dominican figure.<sup id="cite_ref-CIADemo_4-26" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CIADemo-4">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-225" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-225">&#91;223&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>As a result, hundreds of thousands of Haitians have migrated to the Dominican Republic, with some estimates of 800,000 Haitians in the country,<sup id="cite_ref-pinadep_169-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-pinadep-169">&#91;168&#93;</a></sup> while others put the Haitian-born population as high as one million.<sup id="cite_ref-226" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-226">&#91;224&#93;</a></sup> They usually work at low-paying and unskilled jobs in building construction and house cleaning and in sugar plantations.<sup id="cite_ref-ferguson_227-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ferguson-227">&#91;225&#93;</a></sup> There have been accusations that some Haitian immigrants work in slavery-like conditions and are severely exploited.<sup id="cite_ref-228" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-228">&#91;226&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Due to the lack of basic amenities and medical facilities in Haiti a large number of Haitian women, often arriving with several health problems, cross the border to Dominican soil. They deliberately come during their last weeks of pregnancy to obtain medical attention for childbirth, since Dominican public hospitals do not refuse medical services based on nationality or legal status. Statistics from a hospital in Santo Domingo report that over 22% of childbirths are by Haitian mothers.<sup id="cite_ref-LD2008-01-21_229-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-LD2008-01-21-229">&#91;227&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Haiti also <a href="/wiki/Environmental_issues_in_Haiti" title="Environmental issues in Haiti">suffers from severe environmental degradation</a>. Deforestation is rampant in Haiti; today less than 4 percent of Haiti's forests remain, and in many places the soil has eroded right down to the bedrock.<sup id="cite_ref-230" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-230">&#91;228&#93;</a></sup> Haitians burn wood charcoal for 60% of their domestic energy production. Because of Haiti running out of plant material to burn, some Haitian bootleggers have created an illegal market for charcoal on the Dominican side. Conservative estimates calculate the illegal movement of 115 tons of charcoal per week from the Dominican Republic to Haiti. Dominican officials estimate that at least 10 trucks per week are crossing the border loaded with charcoal.<sup id="cite_ref-231" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-231">&#91;229&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 2005, Dominican President Leonel Fernández criticized collective expulsions of Haitians as having taken place "in an abusive and inhuman way".<sup id="cite_ref-232" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-232">&#91;230&#93;</a></sup> After a UN delegation issued a preliminary report stating that it found a profound problem of racism and discrimination against people of Haitian origin, Dominican <a href="/wiki/Foreign_Minister" class="mw-redirect" title="Foreign Minister">Foreign Minister</a> <a href="/wiki/Carlos_Morales_Troncoso" title="Carlos Morales Troncoso">Carlos Morales Troncoso</a> issued a formal statement denouncing it, asserting that "our border with Haiti has its problems[;] this is our reality and it must be understood. It is important not to confuse national sovereignty with indifference, and not to confuse security with <a href="/wiki/Xenophobia" title="Xenophobia">xenophobia</a>."<sup id="cite_ref-233" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-233">&#91;231&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Haitian nationals send half a <a href="/wiki/Billion" title="Billion">billion</a> <a href="/wiki/Dollars" class="mw-redirect" title="Dollars">dollars</a> total yearly in remittance from the Dominican Republic to Haiti, according to the <a href="/wiki/World_Bank" title="World Bank">World Bank</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-234" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-234">&#91;232&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The government of the Dominican Republic invested a total of $16 billion pesos in health services offered to foreign patients in 2013–2016, according to official data, which includes medical expenses in blood transfusion, clinical analysis, surgeries and other care.<sup id="cite_ref-235" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-235">&#91;233&#93;</a></sup>  According to official reports, the country spends more than five billion Dominican pesos annually in care for pregnant women who cross the border ready to deliver.<sup id="cite_ref-236" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-236">&#91;234&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The children of Haitian immigrants are eligible for Haitian nationality,<sup id="cite_ref-237" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-237">&#91;235&#93;</a></sup> but they may be denied it by Haiti because of a lack of proper documents or witnesses.<sup id="cite_ref-238" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-238">&#91;236&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-239" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-239">&#91;237&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-240" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-240">&#91;238&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-241" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-241">&#91;239&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Emigration">Emigration</span></h3>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Dominican_Day_Parade_2019_(50335870922).jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/73/Dominican_Day_Parade_2019_%2850335870922%29.jpg/220px-Dominican_Day_Parade_2019_%2850335870922%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="147" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/73/Dominican_Day_Parade_2019_%2850335870922%29.jpg/330px-Dominican_Day_Parade_2019_%2850335870922%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/73/Dominican_Day_Parade_2019_%2850335870922%29.jpg/440px-Dominican_Day_Parade_2019_%2850335870922%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="5520" data-file-height="3680" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Dominican_Day_Parade_2019_(50335870922).jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Dominicans in the <a href="/wiki/Dominican_Day_Parade" title="Dominican Day Parade">Dominican Day Parade</a> in New York City, 2019</div></div></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main articles: <a href="/wiki/Dominican_Americans" title="Dominican Americans">Dominican Americans</a> and <a href="/wiki/Dominicans_in_Spain" title="Dominicans in Spain">Dominicans in Spain</a></div>
<p>The first of three late-20th century emigration waves began in 1961 after the assassination of dictator Trujillo,<sup id="cite_ref-Wilderotter_242-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Wilderotter-242">&#91;240&#93;</a></sup> due to fear of retaliation by Trujillo's allies and political uncertainty in general. In 1965, the United States began a military occupation of the Dominican Republic to end a civil war. Upon this, the U.S. eased travel restrictions, making it easier for Dominicans to obtain U.S. visas.<sup id="cite_ref-243" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-243">&#91;241&#93;</a></sup> From 1966 to 1978, the exodus continued, fueled by high unemployment and political repression. Communities established by the first wave of immigrants to the U.S. created a network that assisted subsequent arrivals.<sup id="cite_ref-Annenberg_Foundation_244-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Annenberg_Foundation-244">&#91;242&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In the early 1980s, underemployment, inflation, and the rise in value of the dollar all contributed to a third wave of emigration from the Dominican Republic. Today, emigration from the Dominican Republic remains high.<sup id="cite_ref-Annenberg_Foundation_244-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Annenberg_Foundation-244">&#91;242&#93;</a></sup> In 2012, there were approximately 1.7 million people of Dominican descent in the U.S., counting both native- and foreign-born.<sup id="cite_ref-245" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-245">&#91;243&#93;</a></sup> There was also a growing <a href="/wiki/Dominican_Republic_immigration_to_Puerto_Rico" title="Dominican Republic immigration to Puerto Rico">Dominican immigration to Puerto Rico</a>, with nearly 70,000 Dominicans living there as of 2010<sup class="plainlinks noexcerpt noprint asof-tag update" style="display:none;"><a class="external text" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Dominican_Republic&amp;action=edit">&#91;update&#93;</a></sup>. Although that number is slowly decreasing and immigration trends have reversed because of Puerto Rico's economic crisis as of 2016<sup class="plainlinks noexcerpt noprint asof-tag update" style="display:none;"><a class="external text" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Dominican_Republic&amp;action=edit">&#91;update&#93;</a></sup>.
</p><p>There is a significant Dominican population in Spain.<sup id="cite_ref-INE2018nacidos_246-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-INE2018nacidos-246">&#91;244&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-INE2018nacionalidad_247-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-INE2018nacionalidad-247">&#91;245&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Health">Health</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Health_in_the_Dominican_Republic" title="Health in the Dominican Republic">Health in the Dominican Republic</a></div>
<p>In 2020, the Dominican Republic had an estimated <a href="/wiki/Birth_rate" title="Birth rate">birth rate</a> of 18.5 per 1000 and a <a href="/wiki/Death_rate" class="mw-redirect" title="Death rate">death rate</a> of 6.3 per 1000.<sup id="cite_ref-CIADemo_4-27" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CIADemo-4">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Education">Education</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Education_in_the_Dominican_Republic" title="Education in the Dominican Republic">Education in the Dominican Republic</a></div>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Dominican_kids_in_Santo_Domingo.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/11/Dominican_kids_in_Santo_Domingo.jpg/220px-Dominican_kids_in_Santo_Domingo.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="165" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/11/Dominican_kids_in_Santo_Domingo.jpg/330px-Dominican_kids_in_Santo_Domingo.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/11/Dominican_kids_in_Santo_Domingo.jpg/440px-Dominican_kids_in_Santo_Domingo.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1024" data-file-height="768" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Dominican_kids_in_Santo_Domingo.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Kids taking classes</div></div></div>
<p>Primary education is regulated by the Ministry of Education, with education being a right of all citizens and <a href="/wiki/Youth_in_the_Dominican_Republic" title="Youth in the Dominican Republic">youth in the Dominican Republic</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-248" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-248">&#91;246&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Preschool education is organized in different cycles and serves the 2–4 age group and the 4–6 age group. Preschool education is not mandatory except for the last year. Basic education is compulsory and serves the population of the 6–14 age group. Secondary education is not compulsory, although it is the duty of the state to offer it for free. It caters to the 14–18 age group and is organized in a common core of four years and three modes of two years of study that are offered in three different options: general or academic, vocational (industrial, agricultural, and services), and artistic.
</p><p>The higher education system consists of institutes and universities. The institutes offer courses of a higher technical level. The universities offer technical careers, undergraduate and graduate; these are regulated by the Ministry of Higher Education, Science and Technology.<sup id="cite_ref-249" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-249">&#91;247&#93;</a></sup> The Dominican Republic was ranked 93rd in the <a href="/wiki/Global_Innovation_Index" title="Global Innovation Index">Global Innovation Index</a> in 2021, down from 87th in 2019.<sup id="cite_ref-250" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-250">&#91;248&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-251" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-251">&#91;249&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-252" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-252">&#91;250&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-253" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-253">&#91;251&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Crime">Crime</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Further information: <a href="/wiki/Crime_in_the_Dominican_Republic" title="Crime in the Dominican Republic">Crime in the Dominican Republic</a></div>
<p>In 2012, the Dominican Republic had a <a href="/wiki/List_of_countries_by_intentional_homicide_rate" title="List of countries by intentional homicide rate">murder rate</a> of 22.1 per 100,000 population.<sup id="cite_ref-UNODC_254-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-UNODC-254">&#91;252&#93;</a></sup> There was a total of 2,268 murders in the Dominican Republic in 2012.<sup id="cite_ref-UNODC_254-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-UNODC-254">&#91;252&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The Dominican Republic has become a trans-shipment point for Colombian drugs destined for Europe as well as the United States and Canada.<sup id="cite_ref-CIADemo_4-28" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CIADemo-4">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-NYT_255-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-NYT-255">&#91;253&#93;</a></sup> Money-laundering via the Dominican Republic is favored by Colombian drug cartels for the ease of illicit financial transactions.<sup id="cite_ref-CIADemo_4-29" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CIADemo-4">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> In 2004, it was estimated that 8% of all cocaine smuggled into the United States had come through the Dominican Republic.<sup id="cite_ref-256" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-256">&#91;254&#93;</a></sup> The Dominican Republic responded with increased efforts to seize drug shipments, arrest and extradite those involved, and combat money-laundering.
</p><p>The often light treatment of violent criminals has been a continuous source of local controversy. In April 2010, five teenagers, aged 15 to 17, shot and killed two taxi drivers and killed another five by forcing them to drink drain-cleaning acid. On September 24, 2010, the teens were sentenced to prison terms of three to five years, despite the protests of the taxi drivers' families.<sup id="cite_ref-257" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-257">&#91;255&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Culture">Culture</span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Culture_of_the_Dominican_Republic" title="Culture of the Dominican Republic">Culture of the Dominican Republic</a></div>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:202px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Yoryi_Morel_02.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/0/03/Yoryi_Morel_02.jpg/200px-Yoryi_Morel_02.jpg" decoding="async" width="200" height="223" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/0/03/Yoryi_Morel_02.jpg/300px-Yoryi_Morel_02.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/0/03/Yoryi_Morel_02.jpg 2x" data-file-width="320" data-file-height="356" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Yoryi_Morel_02.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Campesino cibaeño, 1941 (Museo de Arte Moderno, Santo Domingo)</div></div></div>
<p>Due to <a href="/wiki/Cultural_syncretism" class="mw-redirect" title="Cultural syncretism">cultural syncretism</a>, the culture and customs of the Dominican people have a <a href="/wiki/European_culture" class="mw-redirect" title="European culture">European cultural basis</a>, influenced by both <a href="/wiki/African_culture" class="mw-redirect" title="African culture">African</a> and native <a href="/wiki/Ta%C3%ADno" title="Taíno">Taíno</a> elements, although endogenous elements have emerged within Dominican culture;<sup id="cite_ref-EstevaFabregat_258-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-EstevaFabregat-258">&#91;256&#93;</a></sup> culturally the Dominican Republic is among the most-European countries in <a href="/wiki/Spanish_America" title="Spanish America">Spanish America</a>, alongside <a href="/wiki/Culture_of_Puerto_Rico" title="Culture of Puerto Rico">Puerto Rico</a>, <a href="/wiki/Culture_of_Cuba" title="Culture of Cuba">Cuba</a>, <a href="/wiki/Central_Chile" title="Central Chile">Central Chile</a>, <a href="/wiki/Culture_of_Argentina" title="Culture of Argentina">Argentina</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Culture_of_Uruguay" title="Culture of Uruguay">Uruguay</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-EstevaFabregat_258-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-EstevaFabregat-258">&#91;256&#93;</a></sup> Spanish institutions in the colonial era were able to predominate in the Dominican culture's making-of as a relative success in the <a href="/wiki/Acculturation" title="Acculturation">acculturation</a> and <a href="/wiki/Cultural_assimilation" title="Cultural assimilation">cultural assimilation</a> of African slaves slightly diminished African cultural influence in comparison to other Caribbean countries.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Visual_arts">Visual arts</span></h3>
<p>Dominican art is perhaps most commonly associated with the bright, vibrant colors and images that are sold in every tourist gift shop across the country. However, the country has a long history of <a href="/wiki/Fine_art" title="Fine art">fine art</a> that goes back to the middle of the 1800s when the country became independent and the beginnings of a national art scene emerged.
</p><p>Historically, the painting of this time were centered around images connected to national independence, historical scenes, portraits but also landscapes and images of still life. Styles of painting ranged between <a href="/wiki/Neoclassicism" title="Neoclassicism">neoclassicism</a> and <a href="/wiki/Romanticism" title="Romanticism">romanticism</a>. Between 1920 and 1940 the art scene was influenced by styles of <a href="/wiki/Realism_(arts)" title="Realism (arts)">realism</a> and <a href="/wiki/Impressionism" title="Impressionism">impressionism</a>. Dominican artists were focused on breaking from previous, academic styles in order to develop more independent and individual styles.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Literature">Literature</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Literature_of_the_Dominican_Republic" class="mw-redirect" title="Literature of the Dominican Republic">Literature of the Dominican Republic</a></div>
<p>The 20th century brought many prominent Dominican writers, and saw a general increase in the perception of Dominican literature.  Writers such as <a href="/wiki/Juan_Bosch_(politician)" title="Juan Bosch (politician)">Juan Bosch</a> (one of the greatest storytellers in <a href="/wiki/Latin_America" title="Latin America">Latin America</a>), <a href="/wiki/Pedro_Mir" title="Pedro Mir">Pedro Mir</a> (national poet of the Dominican Republic<sup id="cite_ref-259" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-259">&#91;257&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-260" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-260">&#91;258&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-261" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-261">&#91;259&#93;</a></sup>), <a href="/wiki/Aida_Cartagena_Portalatin" class="mw-redirect" title="Aida Cartagena Portalatin">Aida Cartagena Portalatin</a> (poetess par excellence who spoke in the Era of Rafael Trujillo), Emilio Rodríguez Demorizi (the most important Dominican historian, with more than 1000 written works<sup id="cite_ref-262" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-262">&#91;260&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-263" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-263">&#91;261&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-264" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-264">&#91;262&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-265" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-265">&#91;263&#93;</a></sup>), <a href="/wiki/Manuel_del_Cabral" title="Manuel del Cabral">Manuel del Cabral</a> (main Dominican poet featured in black poetry<sup id="cite_ref-266" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-266">&#91;264&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-267" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-267">&#91;265&#93;</a></sup>), Hector Inchustegui Cabral (considered one of the most prominent voices of the Caribbean social poetry of the twentieth century<sup id="cite_ref-268" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-268">&#91;266&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-269" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-269">&#91;267&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-270" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-270">&#91;268&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-271" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-271">&#91;269&#93;</a></sup>), Miguel Alfonseca (poet belonging to Generation 60<sup id="cite_ref-272" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-272">&#91;270&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-273" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-273">&#91;271&#93;</a></sup>), Rene del Risco (acclaimed poet who was a participant in the June 14 Movement<sup id="cite_ref-274" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-274">&#91;272&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-275" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-275">&#91;273&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-276" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-276">&#91;274&#93;</a></sup>), <a href="/wiki/Mateo_Morrison" title="Mateo Morrison">Mateo Morrison</a> (excellent poet and writer with numerous awards), among many more prolific authors, put the island in one of the most important in Literature in the twentieth century.
</p><p>New 21st century Dominican writers have not yet achieved the renown of their 20th century counterparts. However, writers such as <a href="/wiki/Frank_B%C3%A1ez" title="Frank Báez">Frank Báez</a> (won the 2006 Santo Domingo Book Fair First Prize) <sup id="cite_ref-277" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-277">&#91;275&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-278" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-278">&#91;276&#93;</a></sup> and <a href="/wiki/Junot_D%C3%ADaz" title="Junot Díaz">Junot Díaz</a> (2008 <a href="/wiki/Pulitzer_Prize_for_Fiction" title="Pulitzer Prize for Fiction">Pulitzer Prize for Fiction</a> for his novel <i><a href="/wiki/The_Brief_Wondrous_Life_of_Oscar_Wao" title="The Brief Wondrous Life of Oscar Wao">The Brief Wondrous Life of Oscar Wao</a>)</i><sup id="cite_ref-279" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-279">&#91;277&#93;</a></sup> lead Dominican literature in the 21st century.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Architecture">Architecture</span></h3>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Church_and_Convent_Regina_Angelorum_CCSD_07_2018_0762.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/ba/Church_and_Convent_Regina_Angelorum_CCSD_07_2018_0762.jpg/220px-Church_and_Convent_Regina_Angelorum_CCSD_07_2018_0762.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="176" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/ba/Church_and_Convent_Regina_Angelorum_CCSD_07_2018_0762.jpg/330px-Church_and_Convent_Regina_Angelorum_CCSD_07_2018_0762.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/ba/Church_and_Convent_Regina_Angelorum_CCSD_07_2018_0762.jpg/440px-Church_and_Convent_Regina_Angelorum_CCSD_07_2018_0762.jpg 2x" data-file-width="3500" data-file-height="2800" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Church_and_Convent_Regina_Angelorum_CCSD_07_2018_0762.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Church and Convent, Colonial Santo Domingo</div></div></div>
<p>The architecture in the Dominican Republic represents a complex blend of diverse cultures. The deep influence of the <a href="/wiki/Europe" title="Europe">European</a> colonists is the most evident throughout the country. Characterized by ornate designs and <a href="/wiki/Baroque_architecture" title="Baroque architecture">baroque</a> structures, the style can best be seen in the capital city of <a href="/wiki/Santo_Domingo" title="Santo Domingo">Santo Domingo</a>, which is home to the first cathedral, castle, monastery, and fortress in all of the <a href="/wiki/Americas" title="Americas">Americas</a>, located in the city's <a href="/wiki/Ciudad_Colonial_(Santo_Domingo)" title="Ciudad Colonial (Santo Domingo)">Colonial Zone</a>, an area declared as a <a href="/wiki/World_Heritage_Site" title="World Heritage Site">World Heritage Site</a> by <a href="/wiki/UNESCO" title="UNESCO">UNESCO</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-280" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-280">&#91;278&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-281" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-281">&#91;279&#93;</a></sup> The designs carry over into the villas and buildings throughout the country. It can also be observed on buildings that contain stucco exteriors, arched doors and windows, and red tiled roofs.
</p><p>The indigenous peoples of the Dominican Republic have also had a significant influence on the architecture of the country. The <a href="/wiki/Ta%C3%ADno" title="Taíno">Taíno</a> people relied heavily on the mahogany and guano (dried palm tree leaf) to put together crafts, artwork, furniture, and houses. Utilizing mud, thatched roofs, and mahogany trees, they gave buildings and the furniture inside a natural look, seamlessly blending in with the island's surroundings.
</p><p>Lately, with the rise in tourism and increasing popularity as a Caribbean vacation destination, architects in the Dominican Republic have now begun to incorporate cutting-edge designs that emphasize luxury. In many ways an architectural playground, villas and hotels implement new styles, while offering new takes on the old. This new style is characterized by simplified, angular corners and large windows that blend outdoor and indoor spaces. As with the culture as a whole, contemporary architects embrace the Dominican Republic's rich history and various cultures to create something new. Surveying modern villas, one can find any combination of the three major styles: a villa may contain angular, modernist building construction, Spanish Colonial-style arched windows, and a traditional Taíno hammock in the bedroom balcony.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Cuisine">Cuisine</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1097763485"/><table class="box-More_citations_needed_section plainlinks metadata ambox ambox-content ambox-Refimprove" role="presentation"><tbody><tr><td class="mbox-image"><div class="mbox-image-div"><a href="/wiki/File:Question_book-new.svg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/9/99/Question_book-new.svg/50px-Question_book-new.svg.png" decoding="async" width="50" height="39" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/9/99/Question_book-new.svg/75px-Question_book-new.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/9/99/Question_book-new.svg/100px-Question_book-new.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="512" data-file-height="399" /></a></div></td><td class="mbox-text"><div class="mbox-text-span">This section <b>needs additional citations for <a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Verifiability" title="Wikipedia:Verifiability">verification</a></b>.<span class="hide-when-compact"> Please help <a class="external text" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Dominican_Republic&amp;action=edit">improve this article</a> by <a href="/wiki/Help:Referencing_for_beginners" title="Help:Referencing for beginners">adding citations to reliable sources</a>. Unsourced material may be challenged and removed.</span>  <span class="date-container"><i>(<span class="date">June 2020</span>)</i></span><span class="hide-when-compact"><i> (<small><a href="/wiki/Help:Maintenance_template_removal" title="Help:Maintenance template removal">Learn how and when to remove this template message</a></small>)</i></span></div></td></tr></tbody></table>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Cuisine_of_the_Dominican_Republic" class="mw-redirect" title="Cuisine of the Dominican Republic">Cuisine of the Dominican Republic</a></div>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:ChicharronMixto.JPG" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/22/ChicharronMixto.JPG/220px-ChicharronMixto.JPG" decoding="async" width="220" height="165" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/22/ChicharronMixto.JPG/330px-ChicharronMixto.JPG 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/22/ChicharronMixto.JPG/440px-ChicharronMixto.JPG 2x" data-file-width="3072" data-file-height="2304" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:ChicharronMixto.JPG" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Chicharrón mixto, a common dish in the country derived from Andalusia in southern Spain</div></div></div>
<p>Dominican cuisine is predominantly <a href="/wiki/Spanish_cuisine#Spanish_regional_variation" title="Spanish cuisine">Spanish</a>, <a href="/wiki/Ta%C3%ADno#Food_and_agriculture" title="Taíno">Taíno</a>, and <a href="/wiki/African_cuisine" title="African cuisine">African</a> in origin. The typical cuisine is quite similar to what can be found in other Latin American countries.<sup id="cite_ref-282" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-282">&#91;280&#93;</a></sup> One breakfast dish consists of eggs and <i><a href="/wiki/Mang%C3%BA_(dish)" class="mw-redirect" title="Mangú (dish)">mangú</a></i> (mashed, boiled plantain). Heartier versions of <i>mangú</i> are accompanied by deep-fried meat (Dominican salami, typically), cheese, or both. Lunch, generally the largest and most important meal of the day, usually consists of rice, meat, beans, and salad. "La Bandera" (literally "The Flag") is the most popular lunch dish; it consists of meat and red beans on white rice. <i><a href="/wiki/Sancocho" title="Sancocho">Sancocho</a></i> is a stew often made with seven varieties of meat.
</p>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:202px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Patacones.JPG" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8f/Patacones.JPG/200px-Patacones.JPG" decoding="async" width="200" height="150" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8f/Patacones.JPG/300px-Patacones.JPG 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8f/Patacones.JPG/400px-Patacones.JPG 2x" data-file-width="2048" data-file-height="1536" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Patacones.JPG" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Tostones, a fried plantain dish</div></div></div>
<p>Meals tend to favor meats and starches over dairy products and vegetables. Many dishes are made with <i><a href="/wiki/Sofrito" title="Sofrito">sofrito</a></i>, which is a mix of local herbs used as a wet rub for meats and sautéed to bring out all of a dish's flavors. Throughout the south-central coast, <a href="/wiki/Bulgur" title="Bulgur">bulgur</a>, or whole wheat, is a main ingredient in <a href="/wiki/Tabbouleh" title="Tabbouleh"><i>quipes</i> or <i>tipili</i></a> (bulgur salad). Other favorite Dominican foods include <i><a href="/wiki/Chicharr%C3%B3n" title="Chicharrón">chicharrón</a></i>, <i><a href="/wiki/Cassava" title="Cassava">yuca</a></i>, <i><a href="/wiki/Tapioca" title="Tapioca">casabe</a></i>, <i><a href="/wiki/Cuban_pastry" title="Cuban pastry">pastelitos</a></i> (<a href="/wiki/Empanada" title="Empanada">empanadas</a>), <i><a href="/wiki/Sweet_potato" title="Sweet potato">batata</a></i>, <a href="/wiki/Yam_(vegetable)" title="Yam (vegetable)"><i>ñame</i></a>, <i><a href="/wiki/Pasteles" title="Pasteles">pasteles en hoja</a></i>, <i><a href="/wiki/Chimichurris" class="mw-redirect" title="Chimichurris">chimichurris</a></i>, and <i><a href="/wiki/Tostones" title="Tostones">tostones</a></i>.
</p><p>Some treats Dominicans enjoy are <i><a href="/wiki/Rice_pudding" title="Rice pudding">arroz con leche</a></i> (or <i>arroz con dulce</i>), <i><a href="/wiki/Bizcocho_Dominicano" title="Bizcocho Dominicano">bizcocho dominicano</a></i> (lit. "Dominican cake"), <i><a href="/wiki/Habichuelas_con_dulce" title="Habichuelas con dulce">habichuelas con dulce</a></i>, <a href="/wiki/Cr%C3%A8me_caramel" title="Crème caramel">flan</a>, <i><a href="/wiki/Snow_cone" title="Snow cone">frío frío</a></i> (snow cones), <a href="/wiki/Dulce_de_leche" title="Dulce de leche">dulce de leche</a>, and <i>caña</i> (<a href="/wiki/Sugarcane" title="Sugarcane">sugarcane</a>). The beverages Dominicans enjoy are <i><a href="/wiki/Morir_So%C3%B1ando" class="mw-redirect" title="Morir Soñando">Morir Soñando</a></i>, <a href="/wiki/Rum" title="Rum">rum</a>, beer, <i><a href="/wiki/Mama_Juana" title="Mama Juana">Mama Juana</a></i>,<sup id="cite_ref-283" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-283">&#91;281&#93;</a></sup> <i>batidas</i> (smoothie), <i>jugos naturales</i> (freshly squeezed fruit juices), <i><a href="/wiki/Mauby" title="Mauby">mabí</a></i>, coffee, and <i><a href="/w/index.php?title=Chaca_(dessert)&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Chaca (dessert) (page does not exist)">chaca</a></i> (also called <i>maiz caqueao/casqueado</i>, <i>maiz con dulce</i> and <i>maiz con leche</i>), the last item being found only in the southern provinces of the country such as San Juan.
</p>
<div style="clear:both;"></div>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Music_and_dance">Music and dance</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Music_of_the_Dominican_Republic" title="Music of the Dominican Republic">Music of the Dominican Republic</a></div>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Romeo_Santos_y_Juan_Luis_Guerra.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/03/Romeo_Santos_y_Juan_Luis_Guerra.jpg/220px-Romeo_Santos_y_Juan_Luis_Guerra.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="146" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/03/Romeo_Santos_y_Juan_Luis_Guerra.jpg/330px-Romeo_Santos_y_Juan_Luis_Guerra.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/03/Romeo_Santos_y_Juan_Luis_Guerra.jpg/440px-Romeo_Santos_y_Juan_Luis_Guerra.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2144" data-file-height="1424" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Romeo_Santos_y_Juan_Luis_Guerra.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div><a href="/wiki/Merengue_music" title="Merengue music">Merengue</a> and <a href="/wiki/Bachata_(music)" title="Bachata (music)">Bachata</a> are both music genres native to Dominican Republic, popular and traditional in Latin America. In the image two icons of these genres, <a href="/wiki/Juan_Luis_Guerra" title="Juan Luis Guerra">Juan Luis Guerra</a> and <a href="/wiki/Romeo_Santos" title="Romeo Santos">Romeo Santos</a>.</div></div></div>
<p>Musically, the Dominican Republic is known for the <a href="/wiki/Popular_culture" title="Popular culture">world popular</a> <a href="/wiki/Musical_style" class="mw-redirect" title="Musical style">musical style</a> and genre called <i><a href="/wiki/Merengue_music" title="Merengue music">merengue</a></i>,<sup id="cite_ref-Harvey2006_284-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Harvey2006-284">&#91;282&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 376–7">&#58;&#8202;376–7&#8202;</span></sup> a type of lively, fast-paced rhythm and dance music consisting of a tempo of about 120 to 160 beats per minute (though it varies) based on musical elements like drums, brass, chorded instruments, and accordion, as well as some elements unique to the Spanish-speaking Caribbean, such as the <i><a href="/wiki/Tambora_(Dominican_drum)" title="Tambora (Dominican drum)">tambora</a></i> and <i><a href="/wiki/G%C3%BCira" title="Güira">güira</a></i>.
</p><p>Its <a href="/wiki/Syncopation" title="Syncopation">syncopated beats</a> use <a href="/wiki/Latin_percussion" title="Latin percussion">Latin percussion</a>, <a href="/wiki/Brass_instruments" class="mw-redirect" title="Brass instruments">brass instruments</a>, bass, and piano or keyboard. Between 1937 and 1950 merengue music was promoted internationally by Dominican groups like Billo's Caracas Boys, Chapuseaux and Damiron "Los Reyes del Merengue", Joseito Mateo, and others. Radio, television, and international media popularized it further. Some well known merengue performers are <a href="/wiki/Wilfrido_Vargas" title="Wilfrido Vargas">Wilfrido Vargas</a>, <a href="/wiki/Johnny_Ventura" title="Johnny Ventura">Johnny Ventura</a>, singer-songwriter <a href="/wiki/Los_Hermanos_Rosario" title="Los Hermanos Rosario">Los Hermanos Rosario</a>, <a href="/wiki/Juan_Luis_Guerra" title="Juan Luis Guerra">Juan Luis Guerra</a>, <a href="/wiki/Fernando_Villalona" title="Fernando Villalona">Fernando Villalona</a>, <a href="/wiki/Eddy_Herrera" title="Eddy Herrera">Eddy Herrera</a>, <a href="/wiki/Sergio_Vargas" title="Sergio Vargas">Sergio Vargas</a>, <a href="/wiki/To%C3%B1o_Rosario" title="Toño Rosario">Toño Rosario</a>, <a href="/wiki/Milly_Quezada" title="Milly Quezada">Milly Quezada</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Chich%C3%AD_Peralta" title="Chichí Peralta">Chichí Peralta</a>.
</p><p>Merengue became popular in the United States, mostly on the <a href="/wiki/East_Coast_of_the_United_States" title="East Coast of the United States">East Coast</a>, during the 1980s and 1990s,<sup id="cite_ref-Harvey2006_284-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Harvey2006-284">&#91;282&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 375">&#58;&#8202;375&#8202;</span></sup> when many Dominican artists residing in the U.S. (particularly New York) started performing in the Latin club scene and gained radio airplay. They included Victor Roque y La Gran Manzana, Henry Hierro, Zacarias Ferreira, Aventura, and Milly Jocelyn Y Los Vecinos. The emergence of <i><a href="/wiki/Bachata_(music)" title="Bachata (music)">bachata</a></i>, along with an increase in the number of Dominicans living among other <a href="/wiki/Hispanic_and_Latino_Americans" title="Hispanic and Latino Americans">Latino</a> groups in New York, <a href="/wiki/New_Jersey" title="New Jersey">New Jersey</a>, and Florida, has contributed to Dominican music's overall growth in popularity.<sup id="cite_ref-Harvey2006_284-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Harvey2006-284">&#91;282&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 378">&#58;&#8202;378&#8202;</span></sup>
</p>
<div class="thumb tleft"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:302px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Dominican_Republic_Dance.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c8/Dominican_Republic_Dance.jpg/300px-Dominican_Republic_Dance.jpg" decoding="async" width="300" height="185" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c8/Dominican_Republic_Dance.jpg/450px-Dominican_Republic_Dance.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c8/Dominican_Republic_Dance.jpg/600px-Dominican_Republic_Dance.jpg 2x" data-file-width="5746" data-file-height="3535" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Dominican_Republic_Dance.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Dominicans dancing in parade with traditional dress.</div></div></div>
<p><a href="/wiki/Bachata_(music)" title="Bachata (music)">Bachata</a>, a form of music and dance that originated in the countryside and rural marginal neighborhoods of the Dominican Republic, has become quite popular in recent years. Its subjects are often romantic; especially prevalent are tales of heartbreak and sadness. In fact, the original name for the genre was <i>amargue</i> ("bitterness", or "bitter music"), until the rather ambiguous (and mood-neutral) term <i>bachata</i> became popular. Bachata grew out of, and is still closely related to, the <a href="/wiki/Pan-Latinism" title="Pan-Latinism">pan-Latin</a> American romantic style called <i><a href="/wiki/Bolero" title="Bolero">bolero</a></i>. Over time, it has been influenced by merengue and by a variety of Latin American guitar styles.
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Palo_music" title="Palo music">Palo</a> is an <a href="/wiki/Afro-Dominican_(Dominican_Republic)" class="mw-redirect" title="Afro-Dominican (Dominican Republic)">Afro-Dominican</a> sacred music that can be found throughout the island. The drum and human voice are the principal instruments. Palo is played at religious ceremonies—usually coinciding with saints' religious feast days—as well as for secular parties and special occasions. Its roots are in the <a href="/wiki/Congo_region" class="mw-redirect" title="Congo region">Congo region</a> of central-west Africa, but it is mixed with European influences in the melodies.<sup id="cite_ref-285" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-285">&#91;283&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Salsa_music" title="Salsa music">Salsa music</a> has had a great deal of popularity in the country. During the late 1960s Dominican musicians like <a href="/wiki/Johnny_Pacheco" title="Johnny Pacheco">Johnny Pacheco</a>, creator of the <a href="/wiki/Fania_All_Stars" class="mw-redirect" title="Fania All Stars">Fania All Stars</a>, played a significant role in the development and popularization of the genre.
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Dominican_rock" title="Dominican rock">Dominican rock</a> and <a href="/wiki/Reggaeton" title="Reggaeton">Reggaeton</a> are also popular. Many, if not the majority, of its performers are based in Santo Domingo and Santiago.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Fashion">Fashion</span></h3>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Oscar_de_la_Renta_by_foto_di_matti.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/03/Oscar_de_la_Renta_by_foto_di_matti.jpg/220px-Oscar_de_la_Renta_by_foto_di_matti.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="309" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/0/03/Oscar_de_la_Renta_by_foto_di_matti.jpg 1.5x" data-file-width="250" data-file-height="351" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Oscar_de_la_Renta_by_foto_di_matti.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Dominican native, fashion designer and perfume maker <a href="/wiki/Oscar_de_la_Renta" title="Oscar de la Renta">Oscar de la Renta</a></div></div></div>
<p>The country boasts one of the ten most important design schools in the region, La Escuela de Diseño de Altos de Chavón, which is making the country a key player in the world of fashion and design. Noted fashion designer <a href="/wiki/Oscar_de_la_Renta" title="Oscar de la Renta">Oscar de la Renta</a> was born in the Dominican Republic in 1932, and became a US citizen in 1971. He studied under the leading Spaniard designer <a href="/wiki/Crist%C3%B3bal_Balenciaga" title="Cristóbal Balenciaga">Cristóbal Balenciaga</a> and then worked with the house of <a href="/wiki/Lanvin_(clothing)" class="mw-redirect" title="Lanvin (clothing)">Lanvin</a> in Paris. By 1963, he had designs bearing his own label. After establishing himself in the US, de la Renta opened boutiques across the country.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template" style="margin-left:0.1em; white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Please_clarify" title="Wikipedia:Please clarify"><span title="The text near this tag may need clarification or removal of jargon. (September 2017)">clarification needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> His work blends French and Spaniard fashion with American styles.<sup id="cite_ref-286" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-286">&#91;284&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-britannica_de_la_renta_287-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-britannica_de_la_renta-287">&#91;285&#93;</a></sup> Although he settled in New York, de la Renta also marketed his work in Latin America, where it became very popular, and remained active in his native Dominican Republic, where his charitable activities and personal achievements earned him the Juan Pablo Duarte Order of Merit and the Order of Cristóbal Colón.<sup id="cite_ref-britannica_de_la_renta_287-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-britannica_de_la_renta-287">&#91;285&#93;</a></sup> De la Renta died of complications from cancer on October 20, 2014.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="National_symbols">National symbols</span></h3>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:152px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Pereskia_quisqueyana.JPG" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/89/Pereskia_quisqueyana.JPG/150px-Pereskia_quisqueyana.JPG" decoding="async" width="150" height="207" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/89/Pereskia_quisqueyana.JPG/225px-Pereskia_quisqueyana.JPG 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/89/Pereskia_quisqueyana.JPG/300px-Pereskia_quisqueyana.JPG 2x" data-file-width="1412" data-file-height="1944" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Pereskia_quisqueyana.JPG" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Bayahibe rose</div></div></div>
<p>Some of the Dominican Republic's important symbols are the <a href="/wiki/Flag_of_the_Dominican_Republic" title="Flag of the Dominican Republic">flag</a>, <a href="/wiki/Coat_of_arms_of_the_Dominican_Republic" title="Coat of arms of the Dominican Republic">the coat of arms</a>, and the national anthem, titled <i><a href="/wiki/National_Anthem_of_the_Dominican_Republic" title="National Anthem of the Dominican Republic">Himno Nacional</a></i>. The flag has a large white cross that divides it into four quarters. Two quarters are red and two are blue. Red represents the blood shed by the liberators. Blue expresses God's protection over the nation. The white cross symbolizes the struggle of the liberators to bequeath future generations a free nation. An alternative interpretation is that blue represents the ideals of progress and liberty, whereas white symbolizes peace and unity among Dominicans.<sup id="cite_ref-288" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-288">&#91;286&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In the center of the cross is the Dominican coat of arms, in the same colors as the national flag. The coat of arms pictures a red, white, and blue flag-draped shield with a Bible, a gold cross, and arrows; the shield is surrounded by an olive branch (on the left) and a palm branch (on the right). The Bible traditionally represents the truth and the light. The gold cross symbolizes the redemption from slavery, and the arrows symbolize the noble soldiers and their proud military.  A blue ribbon above the shield reads, "Dios, Patria, Libertad" (meaning "God, Fatherland, Liberty"). A red ribbon under the shield reads, "República Dominicana" (meaning "Dominican Republic"). Out of all the flags in the world, the depiction of a Bible is unique to the Dominican flag.
</p><p>The <a href="/wiki/Floral_emblem" title="Floral emblem">national flower</a> is the <a href="/wiki/Endemic" class="mw-redirect" title="Endemic">endemic</a> <a href="/wiki/Pereskia_quisqueyana" class="mw-redirect" title="Pereskia quisqueyana">Bayahibe rose</a> (<i>Leuenbergeria quisqueyana</i>) and the <a href="/wiki/National_tree" class="mw-redirect" title="National tree">national tree</a> is the <a href="/wiki/Swietenia_mahagoni" title="Swietenia mahagoni">West Indian mahogany</a> (<i>Swietenia mahagoni</i>).<sup id="cite_ref-289" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-289">&#91;287&#93;</a></sup> The national bird is the <i>cigua palmera</i> or <a href="/wiki/Palmchat" title="Palmchat">palmchat</a> (<i>Dulus dominicus</i>), another endemic species.<sup id="cite_ref-depaul_290-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-depaul-290">&#91;288&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The Dominican Republic celebrates <a href="/wiki/Dia_de_la_Altagracia" class="mw-redirect" title="Dia de la Altagracia">Dia de la Altagracia</a> on January 21 in honor of its patroness, <a href="/wiki/Juan_Pablo_Duarte" title="Juan Pablo Duarte">Duarte</a>'s Day on January 26 in honor of one of its founding fathers, Independence Day on February 27, <a href="/wiki/Dominican_Restoration_War" title="Dominican Restoration War">Restoration Day</a> on August 16, <i>Virgen de las Mercedes</i> on September 24, and <a href="/wiki/Constitution_Day" title="Constitution Day">Constitution Day</a> on November 6.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Sports">Sports</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Sports_in_the_Dominican_Republic" class="mw-redirect" title="Sports in the Dominican Republic">Sports in the Dominican Republic</a></div>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:202px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Albert_Pujols4.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/98/Albert_Pujols4.jpg/200px-Albert_Pujols4.jpg" decoding="async" width="200" height="299" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/98/Albert_Pujols4.jpg/300px-Albert_Pujols4.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/98/Albert_Pujols4.jpg/400px-Albert_Pujols4.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2329" data-file-height="3478" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Albert_Pujols4.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Dominican native and <a href="/wiki/Major_League_Baseball" title="Major League Baseball">Major League Baseball</a> player <a href="/wiki/Albert_Pujols" title="Albert Pujols">Albert Pujols</a></div></div></div>
<p><a href="/wiki/Baseball" title="Baseball">Baseball</a> is by far the most popular sport in the Dominican Republic.<sup id="cite_ref-Harvey2006_284-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Harvey2006-284">&#91;282&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 59">&#58;&#8202;59&#8202;</span></sup> The <a href="/wiki/Dominican_Professional_Baseball_League" title="Dominican Professional Baseball League">Dominican Professional Baseball League</a> consists of six teams. Its season usually begins in October and ends in January. After the United States, the Dominican Republic has the second highest number of <a href="/wiki/Major_League_Baseball" title="Major League Baseball">Major League Baseball</a> (MLB) players. <a href="/wiki/Ozzie_Virgil_Sr." title="Ozzie Virgil Sr.">Ozzie Virgil Sr.</a> became the first Dominican-born player in the MLB on September 23, 1956. <a href="/wiki/Juan_Marichal" title="Juan Marichal">Juan Marichal</a>, <a href="/wiki/Pedro_Mart%C3%ADnez" title="Pedro Martínez">Pedro Martínez</a>, <a href="/wiki/Vladimir_Guerrero" title="Vladimir Guerrero">Vladimir Guerrero</a>, and <a href="/wiki/David_Ortiz" title="David Ortiz">David Ortiz</a> are the only Dominican-born players in the <a href="/wiki/National_Baseball_Hall_of_Fame_and_Museum" title="National Baseball Hall of Fame and Museum">Baseball Hall of Fame</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-291" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-291">&#91;289&#93;</a></sup> Other notable baseball players born in the Dominican Republic are <a href="/wiki/Jos%C3%A9_Bautista" title="José Bautista">José Bautista</a>, <a href="/wiki/Adri%C3%A1n_Beltr%C3%A9" title="Adrián Beltré">Adrián Beltré</a>, <a href="/wiki/Juan_Soto" title="Juan Soto">Juan Soto</a>, <a href="/wiki/Robinson_Can%C3%B3" title="Robinson Canó">Robinson Canó</a>, <a href="/wiki/Rico_Carty" title="Rico Carty">Rico Carty</a>, <a href="/wiki/Bartolo_Col%C3%B3n" title="Bartolo Colón">Bartolo Colón</a>, <a href="/wiki/Nelson_Cruz" title="Nelson Cruz">Nelson Cruz</a>, <a href="/wiki/Edwin_Encarnaci%C3%B3n" title="Edwin Encarnación">Edwin Encarnación</a>, <a href="/wiki/Ubaldo_Jim%C3%A9nez" title="Ubaldo Jiménez">Ubaldo Jiménez</a>, <a href="/wiki/Francisco_Liriano" title="Francisco Liriano">Francisco Liriano</a>, <a href="/wiki/Pl%C3%A1cido_Polanco" title="Plácido Polanco">Plácido Polanco</a>, <a href="/wiki/Albert_Pujols" title="Albert Pujols">Albert Pujols</a>, <a href="/wiki/Hanley_Ram%C3%ADrez" title="Hanley Ramírez">Hanley Ramírez</a>, <a href="/wiki/Manny_Ram%C3%ADrez" class="mw-redirect" title="Manny Ramírez">Manny Ramírez</a>, <a href="/wiki/Jos%C3%A9_Reyes_(shortstop)" class="mw-redirect" title="José Reyes (shortstop)">José Reyes</a>, <a href="/wiki/Alfonso_Soriano" title="Alfonso Soriano">Alfonso Soriano</a>, <a href="/wiki/Sammy_Sosa" title="Sammy Sosa">Sammy Sosa</a>, <a href="/wiki/Fernando_Tat%C3%ADs_Jr." title="Fernando Tatís Jr.">Fernando Tatís Jr.</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Miguel_Tejada" title="Miguel Tejada">Miguel Tejada</a>. <a href="/wiki/Felipe_Alou" title="Felipe Alou">Felipe Alou</a> has also enjoyed success as a manager<sup id="cite_ref-292" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-292">&#91;290&#93;</a></sup> and <a href="/wiki/Omar_Minaya" title="Omar Minaya">Omar Minaya</a> as a general manager. In 2013, the Dominican team went undefeated <i>en route</i> to winning the <a href="/wiki/2013_World_Baseball_Classic" title="2013 World Baseball Classic">World Baseball Classic</a>.
</p><p>In <a href="/wiki/Boxing" title="Boxing">boxing</a>, the country has produced scores of world-class fighters and several world champions,<sup id="cite_ref-293" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-293">&#91;291&#93;</a></sup> such as <a href="/wiki/Carlos_Cruz_(boxer)" title="Carlos Cruz (boxer)">Carlos Cruz</a>, his brother <a href="/wiki/Leo_Cruz" title="Leo Cruz">Leo</a>, <a href="/wiki/Juan_Guzman_(boxer)" title="Juan Guzman (boxer)">Juan Guzman</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Joan_Guzman" class="mw-redirect" title="Joan Guzman">Joan Guzman</a>. Basketball also enjoys a relatively high level of popularity. <a href="/wiki/Tito_Horford" title="Tito Horford">Tito Horford</a>, his son <a href="/wiki/Al_Horford" title="Al Horford">Al</a>, <a href="/wiki/Felipe_L%C3%B3pez_(basketball)" title="Felipe López (basketball)">Felipe Lopez</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Francisco_Garc%C3%ADa_(basketball)" title="Francisco García (basketball)">Francisco Garcia</a> are among the Dominican-born players currently or formerly in the <a href="/wiki/National_Basketball_Association" title="National Basketball Association">National Basketball Association</a> (NBA). Olympic gold medalist and world champion hurdler <a href="/wiki/F%C3%A9lix_S%C3%A1nchez_(hurdler)" title="Félix Sánchez (hurdler)">Félix Sánchez</a> hails from the Dominican Republic, as does <a href="/wiki/National_Football_League" title="National Football League">NFL</a> <a href="/wiki/Defensive_end" title="Defensive end">defensive end</a> <a href="/wiki/Luis_Castillo_(American_football)" title="Luis Castillo (American football)">Luis Castillo</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-294" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-294">&#91;292&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Other important sports are <a href="/wiki/Volleyball" title="Volleyball">volleyball</a>, introduced in 1916 by U.S. Marines and controlled by the <a href="/wiki/Dominican_Volleyball_Federation" class="mw-redirect" title="Dominican Volleyball Federation">Dominican Volleyball Federation</a>, <a href="/wiki/Taekwondo" title="Taekwondo">taekwondo</a>, in which <a href="/wiki/Gabriel_Mercedes" title="Gabriel Mercedes">Gabriel Mercedes</a> won an Olympic silver medal in 2008, and <a href="/wiki/Judo" title="Judo">judo</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-295" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-295">&#91;293&#93;</a></sup>
</p>

<div class="mw-references-wrap"><ol class="references">
<li id="cite_note-82"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-82">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><i>Terrenos comuneros</i> arose because of "scarce population, low value of the land, the absence of officials qualified to survey the lands, and the difficulty of dividing up the ranch in such a way that each would receive a share of the grasslands, forests, streams, palm groves, and small agricultural plots that, only when combined, made possible the exploitation of the ranch." (Hoetink, <i>The Dominican People: Notes for a Historical Sociology</i> transl. Stephen Ault Pg. 83 (Johns Hopkins Press: Baltimore, 1982))</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-194"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-194">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">The term "indio" in the Dominican Republic is not associated with people of indigenous ancestry but people of mixed ancestry or skin color between light and dark</span>
</li>
</ol></div></div>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="References">References</span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1011085734"/><div class="reflist">
<div class="mw-references-wrap mw-references-columns"><ol class="references">
<li id="cite_note-ONE-Encuesta-Autopercepcion-1"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-ONE-Encuesta-Autopercepcion_1-0">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><style Hasta el presente, ya se tienen registros de unos 600 libaneses, 200 palestinos y 200 sirios. (...) <li id="cite_note-288"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-288">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">
